## ARNOLD
# CLAPMARIUS, <note>Arnoldus Clapmarius (1574–1604) was a German jurist and scholar famous for his writings on the "secrets of state" (arcana imperii), which explored the hidden mechanisms of political power.</note>
## FRANCISCUS
## ROSELLUS
### A N D
## WOLFGANG HEINRICH
## RUPRECHT
->*Conclusions*<-
### O N
# PUBLIC LAW. <note>original: "Iure Publico". Public law concerns the legal framework of the state, including the relationships between the government and its citizens.</note>

->*Amsterdam,*<-
->At the house of Lodewijk Elzevir, 1644.<- <note>The Elzevirs were a celebrated Dutch family of printers and booksellers who revolutionized the publishing industry with high-quality, pocket-sized editions of academic and classical texts.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a collection of legal conclusions on public law by jurists Arnold Clapmarius, Franciscus Rosellus, and Wolfgang Heinrich Ruprecht. Published by the famous Elzevir press in 1644, it represents the seventeenth-century academic focus on the structure and rights of the state.</summary>

<keywords>Public Law, Arnold Clapmarius, Elzevir Press, 17th-century law, Jurisprudence, Amsterdam publishing, political science</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the work to Maurice, Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel. It follows the title page and establishes the author's relationship with his patron.</meta>

->*To the Most Illustrious and Powerful*<-

# PRINCE MAURICE,

## Landgrave of Hesse, etc., <note>The "etc." refers to Maurice's additional titles, which included Count of Katzenelnbogen, Diez, Ziegenhain, and Nidda.</note> my most gracious Lord.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'I' within a square frame, embellished with intricate floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

Great Prince, both the immense greatness of your name and your singular kindness <note>original: "clementia"</note> toward me have had such power that they have not only made me mindful of such great favors, but have even made me bold. For no interval of time or place ought to snatch the memory of your virtues from me, nor could it prevent me from rendering to you an account of my leisure and my duty. Indeed, ever since I arrived at this University <term>Academy (original: Academia): A term for a place of higher learning; here it likely refers to the University of Marburg, which Maurice transformed into a center of Reformed learning.</term> by your authority and counsel, I have applied myself not only to be diligent in the duty that my loyalty required of me, but also to satisfy your expectations and recommendation—by which you easily surpassed both my own weakness and the opinion of many others. Therefore, I had to strive with greater effort, both because of your remarkable judgment of me (which made me feel somewhat modest, given my insignificance <note>original: "tenuitate mea"</note>) and because of the difficulty of this duty of mine, to which I am scarcely equal. I wished for the reason of my leisure to be clear to this University, and to ensure that you would not regret your recommendation. Having entered this arena of studies and labors, while I reflect on my progress and bring forth now one thing and now another, this <term>disputation (original: disputatio): A formal academic discourse or thesis defended in public.</term> concerning Public Law <note>original: "Iure publico"</note> happened to emerge from my work. While the difficulty of this study is great enough to deter some from such a praiseworthy task, its dignity has such power over me that it leads me toward the greatness of so important a subject—

<summary>In this formal dedication, the author expresses his deep gratitude to Maurice, Landgrave of Hesse, for his patronage and explains that this academic work on Public Law is a result of his studies at the university under the Prince's protection.</summary>

<keywords>Maurice of Hesse-Kassel, dedication, patronage, Public Law, University of Marburg, academic disputation, legal studies</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Prince Maurice of Hesse. The author, Arnoldus Clapmarius, transitions from discussing his own modesty to justifying the study of political science as a necessary tool for a ruler, before offering this work as a symbolic payment of his debt of gratitude.</meta>

*Indeed, many are so impatient that unless they expect the greatest hope of profit, they do not even "deserve heaven"—as the saying goes <note>original: "ut ille dicebat" (as that man said); likely a reference to a classical proverb or a specific author such as Seneca, suggesting that people only strive for high goals if there is a reward.</note>—to apply their minds to such a barren study. However, there is the greatest and most distinguished use in the <term>State (original: Respublica): While "Republic" is the literal translation, in this 17th-century context, it refers to the Commonwealth or the organized political body of the state.</term> for this subject, as there is for other things which those people fastidiously despise.*

*For you, who sit at the helm of the State, cannot be ignorant of the laws of the Empire, of Religion, of wars, the treaties of allies, the <term>secrets of state (original: arcana): A central concept in Clapmarius's work, referring to the hidden strategies or "state secrets" used by rulers to maintain power.</term>, the method of delivering an opinion, the laws of decreeing or reporting, and three hundred <note>The number "three hundred" (Latin: trecenta) was often used idiomatically by Roman and Renaissance writers to mean "countless" or "an infinite number."</note> other matters of that kind, without the greatest detriment to the Commonwealth. Should they then fear the difficulty of those things whose necessity and excellence they admit? But for you, Prince—most illustrious both by your own virtue and that of your ancestors—there is a far different mind and a different opinion regarding these studies. For this reason, you pursue with greater favor those who proceed by the "royal road" toward the administration of the State, resulting in both the highest dignity of your rule and the glory of your name, as well as the benefit of your citizens.*

*Furthermore, I wish to ask your Clemency, most submissively and with all devotion of soul, to accept this Disputation <note>A formal academic debate or the published thesis resulting from it.</note> as a small installment for a time, just as kind creditors are accustomed to do. For neither the slenderness of my talent, nor the narrowness of time, nor the magnitude of your merits toward me, allows me to pay off the principal debt itself. Farewell and live long, most excellent of Princes, the immortal glory of the German name. At Altdorf <note>Altdorf bei Nürnberg, the site of a famous university in the "Noric" or Nuremberg territory.</note> in the Noric province.*

->Your Highness’s<-
->Most Devoted<-
->Arnoldus Clapmarius.<-

<summary>Arnoldus Clapmarius concludes his dedication to Prince Maurice by defending the practical necessity of political and legal studies for rulers and offering his academic work as a token of gratitude. He emphasizes that a leader must understand the 'arcana' or secrets of state to govern effectively.</summary>

<keywords>Arnoldus Clapmarius, Prince Maurice of Hesse, Altdorf, secrets of state, arcana imperii, political science, legal education, dedication, statecraft</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the first formal "Disputation" or academic debate of the work. The previous page ended with Clapmarius's dedication to Prince Maurice, justifying the study of politics and statecraft. This page marks the shift from the introductory letter to the scholarly analysis of Public Law using the Roman historian Tacitus as a primary source. The catchword "jus sistat" from the previous page's footer likely refers to a phrase appearing later in the text or a specific legal citation.</meta>

# DISPUTATION I

->Concerning<-

# PUBLIC LAW

->excerpts from Cornelius Tacitus<-

->Presiding<-

## ARNOLD CLAPMARIUS

->Respondent<-

->Johann Christoph Kotzer<-

->THESIS I<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'D' featuring floral and leafy scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>
Tacitus speaks of Cocceius and Capito, Velleius of Scaevola, Pomponius of Tubero, Pliny of Titus Aristo, and others of yet more individuals, noting that they were most knowledgeable in both public and private law. <note>These names refer to famous Roman jurists and historians. For example, Ateius Capito and Cocceius Nerva were leading legal experts during the early Roman Empire.</note> Furthermore, those who deny that public law exists anywhere in historical works are total strangers to the study of history.

->II<-

For those things of which Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (4th century AD), the last major historical writer of the Roman Empire.</note> speaks—certain matters whose sequence it was not permitted to scrutinize, nor was it proper to bring into the public eye even if it had been possible—are not to be understood as "Public Law." Neither was Public Law contained in the <term>Imperial Ledger (original: Rationarium Imperatorum): A detailed record of the empire’s resources, including the number of citizens, soldiers, and tax revenues, famously kept by Augustus.</term>, the sections of which are recounted by Tacitus, Suetonius, and Cassius Dio.

->III<-

<margin>Public Law</margin>
<margin>What the "State" is, and</margin>
<margin>what it consists of.</margin>
Public Law is, as the jurist <term>Ulpian (one of the most influential Roman legal authorities)</term> correctly defines it, that which pertains to the <term>status (original: statum): Here meaning the constitution, well-being, or organized condition</term> of the Roman State. Tacitus uses the same meaning in Book 4 of his *Histories* when discussing the "status of the State and the Empire." This law consists of sacred rites, priests, and magistrates. From this, many believe...

<summary>This page introduces the first academic disputation on Public Law, defining the field by citing classical authorities like Ulpian and Tacitus. Clapmarius argues that Public Law is a distinct study found within history, focusing on the state's constitution, religious rites, and government offices.</summary>
<keywords>Public Law, Arnold Clapmarius, Roman Law, Ulpian, Tacitus, Statecraft, Imperial Ledger, Roman Constitution</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the first formal "Disputation" or academic debate regarding Public Law. It follows from the previous page's introduction of the topic, refuting the claim that public law is identical to the general study of politics as defined by Aristotle. The text uses Roman history and the works of Tacitus to define the specific boundaries and categories of legal and state authority.</meta>

...that public law is the same thing as Politics, but this is false and contrary to the opinion of Aristotle. <note>Aristotle treated "Politics" as a broad ethical and social science, whereas the author argues "Public Law" is a specific legal framework of the state.</note>

### IV.

Tacitus says in the third book of his *Annals*: *"Great,"* he says, *"was the appearance of that day, on which the Senate examined the favors granted by their ancestors, the treaties of allies, the decrees of those who were powerful even before Roman might prevailed, and the very religion of the divine powers."* <note>original: "Magna... introspexit"</note> Truly, these things savor of public law.

### V.

<margin>Chapters of sacred law.</margin>
*The chapters of sacred law are generally these: The laws of Religion, of rites, of sacrifices, of the Augurs, of funerals, of temples, of the spirits of the dead <note>original: "Manium" (The Manes), the souls of deceased ancestors worshipped as protective deities.</note>, and of oracles.* Regarding these, fragments from Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a scholar who wrote extensively on Roman antiquities and religion.</note> survive, and histories abound with examples; if only there are those who can explain them clearly.

### VI.

Tacitus beautifully uses the terms *sacred things of the kingdom* and *sacred things of an embassy*; others use *sacred things of war, sacred things of treaties, and sacred things of the household gods*. So too, the jurists call the laws of Majesty, and those things allowed by the law of the kingdom, the *Sacred of Sacreds*. <note>original: "Sacra sacrorum"</note> Nevertheless, all these are not strictly part of sacred law, but of public law.

### VII.

<margin>Chapters of public law.</margin>
*The chapters of public law are these: The laws of Empire, of Sovereignty, of Magistrates, of the Senate, of the Assemblies, of coinage, of the Orders <note>The "Orders" refers to the social classes of Rome, such as the Senators and the Knights (Equites).</note>, the laws of the citizens' liberty, of voting, of markets, of taxes, of tributes, of the Treasury <term>Aerarium: The public treasury of the Roman state, separate from the Emperor's private funds.</term>, of the Census, the laws of treaties, of embassies, of wars, of victory, and other things of this kind.*

### VIII.

Emperor, Lord, Caesar, King, Prince—and the many other words for sovereignty—were in reality one and the same thing. It was merely a "cataract" <note>original: "Glaucoma," here used metaphorically to mean a medical film over the eyes that obscures vision or a deception placed over the people's eyes to hide the reality of monarchical power.</note> pulled over the eyes of the people. No matter how much Pliny <note>Pliny the Younger, who in his "Panegyricus" tried to distinguish the 'Princeps' (first citizen) from a 'Dominus' (master/tyrant).</note> may argue otherwise. More openly and more truly concerning Vitellius...

<meta>catchword: Tacitus:</meta>

<summary>In this section of the first disputation, the author distinguishes between 'Sacred Law' and 'Public Law,' defining the specific categories and jurisdictions of each. He argues that titles like 'Emperor' and 'Prince' are functionally identical labels used to mask the reality of sovereign power from the public.</summary>
<keywords>Public Law, Sacred Law, Tacitus, Aristotle, Roman Constitution, Sovereignty, Aerarium, Varro, Political Theory</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and historical analysis of Public Law. While the previous page defined the scope of sacred law and its religious components, this page shifts to defining the specific vocabulary of power and tracing the historical control of religious law (jus sacrum) from the Roman Kings and Senate to the Emperors, eventually touching on the conflict between secular and ecclesiastical authority in the Middle Ages.</meta>

# Concerning Public Law.

<sig>A 4</sig>

*Tacitus says: He sent an edict ahead into the city, by which he postponed the title of Augustus; he did not accept the title of Caesar, even though he diminished nothing of his actual power.* <note>original: "Praemisit... detraheret." This refers to the political maneuvering of the early Roman Emperors who maintained absolute power while appearing to respect traditional Republican titles.</note>

->IX.<-

However, there are differences between Majesty, Supreme Command (*Imperium*), or <term>the sovereign power</term> <note>original: τὸ κύριον (*to kyrion*), a Greek term for the supreme authority in a state.</note>, Power, Lordship, Force, Dignity, Authority, the Principate (or the Beginning), Counsel, Necessity, and Dominion. Nevertheless, many writers incorrectly confuse most of these terms with one another.

->X.<-

<margin>*In whose hands the sacred law rested among the Romans.*</margin>
From the beginning, sacred law was in the hands of the Kings; later, it was transferred to the high Senate (*although learned men disagree on this point*). When the Republic was changed, just as with all other things, the Emperors transferred matters pertaining to divine affairs and religion to themselves. Tacitus and Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Romano-Jewish historian.</note> write about this. There also exists a legal opinion by Demetrius Chomatenus <note>A 13th-century Byzantine archbishop and expert in canon law.</note> supporting this view.

->XI.<-

Nevertheless, under Augustus, Tiberius, and a few others, the Roman Senate still held great authority regarding sacred law. This is evident from that <term>Decree of the Senate</term> <note>original: "SCto." abbreviation for *Senatus Consulto*.</note> stating that Christ our Savior should not be counted among the gods. <note>The author refers to a famous tradition, cited by early church fathers like Tertullian, that Tiberius proposed the divinity of Christ to the Senate, who then rejected it.</note> Likewise, the Priests (*Pontifices*) were to see which traditions of the Soothsayers <note>original: "Aruspicum" (Haruspices), diviners who interpreted the will of the gods from the entrails of sacrificed animals.</note> should be retained and strengthened; however, under the remaining Emperors, this authority was meager.

->XII.<-

In the Roman Republic, the Priests never held a rightful legal title of authority over the Emperors, nor even formerly over the Kings, as even Otto of Freising <note>original: "Frisingense." A 12th-century bishop and historian.</note> testifies. Therefore, that subservient—or to use the word of Marcello—abject humiliation of Henry II <note>Likely referring to the penance of the Holy Roman Emperor Henry IV (often confused or numbered differently in older texts) before the Pope, or a similar act of submission.</note> before the Roman Pope is to be detested; Lambert of Hersfeld <note>original: "Scafneburgensis." A medieval chronicler whose work was formerly attributed to "Lambert of Aschaffenburg."</note> writes about this.

->XIII.<-

<margin>*The Roman Republic*</margin>
Under Augustus, the Roman Republic was a mix—

<summary>This page analyzes the evolution of "sacred law" in Rome, arguing that religious authority originally rested with secular rulers (Kings and Emperors) rather than independent priesthoods. It also provides a vocabulary of political power and critiques the medieval submission of Emperors to Papal authority.</summary>

<keywords>Majesty, Imperium, Sacred Law, Roman Senate, Augustus, Tiberius, Priesthood, Henry II, Otto of Freising, political terminology</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of the Roman transition from Republic to Empire. Having established that the Emperors eventually seized control of religious law, the author now analyzes the specific political strategies used by Augustus to blend absolute power with the appearance of traditional liberty, contrasting his "wise" restraint with the behavior of later rulers and the internal power structures of the imperial court.</meta>

# Disputation I.

<margin>
How it was mixed under Augustus.
</margin>
...[liber]ty, though the writer from Angers <note>Referring to an author from Angers, France, likely a legal commentator or historian.</note> denies this. Therefore, what Tacitus said of Nerva applies even more truly to Augustus: that he mixed things once considered incompatible—the Principate <note>The early period of the Roman Empire where the ruler styled himself as "First Citizen."</note> and liberty.

### XIV.

For this reason, Augustus acted wisely by rejecting the titles of Lord, King, and Dictator, and instead took the title of *Princeps* <note>First Citizen</note>. He also wisely compelled the Senate through the exercise of "tribunitian power" <note>The legal authority of a Tribune of the Plebs, which allowed the Emperor to veto legislation and hold personal sanctity.</note>. It was in this same spirit that Gregory the Great sharply warned the Patriarch of Constantinople not to call himself "Universal Lord," but rather "Servant of the Servants of God" <note>original: *Servum Servorum*. A title still used by the Pope to emphasize humility despite holding supreme authority.</note>. The conduct of Vitellius <note>Roman Emperor during the "Year of the Four Emperors," 69 AD.</note> was foolish by comparison; when his Empire was in peril, he issued an edict insisting he be called "Caesar," a title he had previously despised.

### XV.

Tacitus truly writes in the first book of his *Histories*: *There was no other remedy for a country in discord than for it to be ruled by one man.* <note>original: "Non aliud... regeretur."</note> Pomponius the Jurisconsult, Pliny, and Cassius Dio all testify to the same. Therefore, it was rightly and with the best legal justification that *Augustus took under his command all things exhausted by civil discord.* <note>original: "Augustus cuncta discordiis fessa sub imperium accepit."</note> I am not certain, however, if the same can be said of Julius Caesar.

### XVI.

For even if we grant that Caesar had a just cause for waging war against Pompey—which he certainly did—he nevertheless had no cause for overturning the Republic and seizing the empire for himself. Fabius Maximus, Tiberius Gracchus, and others acted much more correctly when they set aside their private hatreds for the sake of the public good.

<margin>
Secrets of the house versus secrets of the court.
</margin>

### XVII.

I distinguish those things which Tacitus calls "secrets of the house" <note>original: *arcana domus*. This refers to the private, often scandalous or informal power dynamics within the Emperor's family.</note> from those things which Lambert of Aschaffenburg <note>An 11th-century German chronicler.</note> calls "laws of the court" <note>original: *jura aulae*, or *leges Palatinas*. These are the formal administrative rules governing the imperial household and palace.</note>. The former should not be made public...

era-

<summary>This page analyzes the political branding of Augustus, who successfully blended absolute rule with Republican traditions, and contrasts his "necessary" rise to power with Julius Caesar's more destructive ambitions. It also introduces a distinction between the private power struggles of a ruling family and the formal laws governing a royal court.</summary>

<keywords>Augustus, Tacitus, Principate, liberty, Gregory the Great, Vitellius, Julius Caesar, Pompey, arcana domus, public law, Roman Empire</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of imperial power and its relationship with both courtly conduct and the military. Having established how Augustus balanced the appearance of liberty with the reality of power, the author now transitions into the practical necessity of military protection (the Praetorians) and the historical separation between religious offices and military command, concluding with a debate over the administration of the imperial guard.</meta>

# On Public Law.
<sig>A 5</sig>

<margin>They differ from courtly manners.</margin>
...they were, according to *Sallust*, <note>Gaius Sallustius Crispus, a Roman historian known for his accounts of the Catiline conspiracy and the Jugurthine War.</note> and for the most part they were scandals; these things ought to have been impressed upon the minds of courtiers.

### X V I I I.

*Julius Caesar* said of *Sulla* that because he had resigned the Dictatorship, he "did not know his letters" <note>original: *literas nescivisse*. A play on words suggesting Sulla was politically illiterate or foolish to give up absolute power.</note>. The same could be said of *Caesar* himself, for he dismissed his Praetorian guards. For this reason, the most learned *Savile* <note>Sir Henry Savile (1549–1622), an English scholar and translator of Tacitus.</note> calls him a "solecism" <note>In this context, a political blunder or error in judgment.</note> and a "bad grammarian." How much more correctly did *Tiberius* station guards at the funeral of *Augustus*, however much he was mocked for it by the people.

### X I X.

Among the *Romans*, the *Flamen Dialis* <term>the high priest of Jupiter, who was subject to many strict religious taboos</term> was once not permitted to go into a province with military command; nor was the priest of Mars <note>referred to here as *Martiali*</note>, as recorded in *Valerius Maximus*. Therefore, the same *Valerius* says that the supreme command "yielded to religion." This was done much more submissively than the way the "spiritual and civil swords" <note>The medieval doctrine of the 'Two Swords,' representing the power of the Church and the power of the State.</note> are used today, as *Otto* <note>Otto of Freising (c. 1114–1158), a bishop and chronicler who wrote on the history of the two powers.</note> testifies, whose words I cannot help but quote here:
> "Those priests," he says, "seem to be entirely at fault who attempt to strike at a kingdom with their own sword—which they themselves hold only by the grace of Kings—unless perhaps they think to imitate David, who first struck down the Philistine by the power of God, and afterwards cut his throat with the Philistine's own sword."
Nevertheless, *Arnold of Brescia* <note>A 12th-century reformer and rebel who argued that the clergy should possess no temporal power.</note> was too harsh in saying that bishops who hold temporal commands and royal rights cannot be saved; for this reason he was burned, according to *Gunther* <note>Gunther of Pairis, a Cistercian monk and poet who chronicled the deeds of Frederick Barbarossa.</note>.

### X X.

<margin>Whether it is more correct to appoint two Praetorian Prefects or one.</margin>
*Maecenas*, in the writings of *Dio* <note>Dio Cassius</note>, recommended two Praetorian Prefects for the Emperor; *Agrippina*, in *Tacitus*, preferred one. Which is more correct? We believe that Emperors were safer with two than with one. Nonetheless, *Agrippina* was no less right [in her reasoning], if the Praetorian cohorts <term>the elite bodyguards of the Roman Emperor</term> were to be ruled by one...

<meta>catchword: appointed.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the practical requirements of maintaining imperial power, specifically through the use of the Praetorian Guard, and explores the historical boundaries between religious authority and military command. It contrasts the ancient separation of these roles with the medieval conflicts between church and state authority.</summary>

<keywords>Praetorian Guard, Julius Caesar, Sulla, Flamen Dialis, Two Swords doctrine, Otto of Freising, Arnold of Brescia, Maecenas, Agrippina the Younger, imperial security, ecclesiastical power</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the administration of the Praetorian Guard and transitions into the relationship between religious observance and state discipline. The previous page ended discussing how Augustus chose the imperial guard from the equestrian order to maintain stricter discipline.</meta>

...that the discipline would be more intense. It was a prudent decision that they were chosen only from the equestrian order; although he <note>Likely referring to a later emperor</note> later changed this.

### XXI.

Therefore, *Tacitus* <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a senator and historian of the Roman Empire.</note> in the 4th book of his *Histories* rightly rebukes *Suetonius* <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve emperors.</note> for claiming that before *Titus*, the Praetorian Prefecture <term>the office of the commander of the Emperor's personal guard</term> was not administered by anyone except a Roman knight. He cites the example of *Arretinus Clemens*, who, although of the Senatorial order, was made Praetorian Prefect.

### XXII.

<margin>Church discipline compared with the Roman censorship.</margin>
Ecclesiastical coercion—which, according to the testimony of *Cyprian* <note>St. Cyprian (c. 210–258 AD), the Bishop of Carthage who wrote extensively on Church discipline.</note>, has fallen into disuse to the great detriment of the Commonwealth—is not absurdly compared in certain respects to the Roman censorship <term>the office of the Censor, responsible for the census and overseeing public morality</term>. It is a shame to our religion that in this matter, Christians are surpassed by the Romans. Furthermore, some disparage the census conducted by *Augustus*, *Germanicus*, and others; however, we say they did so for the benefit of the Commonwealth. Today, because of the neglect of this practice, many Commonwealths are privately powerful but publicly destitute.

### XXIII.

*Appius Pulcher* was rightly condemned by public judgment because, having treated the Augury <term>the practice of interpreting the will of the gods from the flight or feeding of birds</term> with contempt, he threw the sacred chickens into the sea against the laws of religion, and subsequently fought a disastrous battle. According to *Livy*, *Flaminius* was liable for the same crime, as was *Paetus* in the writings of *Tacitus*.

### XXIV.

But did *Papirius* act correctly when, even though false auspices <note>divination rituals</note> had been reported to him, he nevertheless went to battle? He stated: "He who attends the auspice, if he reports anything false, brings the religious guilt upon himself; as for me, a *tripudium* <term>a favorable omen occurring when sacred chickens ate so hungrily that food fell from their beaks</term> has been reported, which is an excellent omen for the Roman people." On this passage, those two most sharp-witted 

<meta>writers disagree...</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the historical qualifications for the Praetorian Prefecture and compares the effectiveness of Roman state censorship and census-taking with the decline of Church discipline. It also examines the legal and religious consequences for Roman commanders who ignored or manipulated sacred omens (auspices) before battle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on public law and administration, moving from the discipline of the Praetorian Guard to the role of religion in statecraft and the strategies for imperial succession. It maintains the previous focus on using Roman history as a blueprint for contemporary governance.</meta>

->On Public Law.<-
A 6
<meta>catchword: Dici-</meta>

Writers from Tuscany, Machiavelli <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), author of *The Prince*.</note> and Ammirato <note>Scipione Ammirato (1531–1601), a political theorist who wrote a counter-critique of Machiavelli.</note>. We follow the latter;

### XXV.

<margin>The reverence of the Roman people concerning divine matters.</margin>
Furthermore, the reverence of the Roman people regarding divine matters was great. Hence Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a prolific Roman scholar.</note> says: *Matters concerning the divine must be referred to the Senate before matters concerning humans.* The Turks have derived the same force of superstition from Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing the city's religious foundations.</note>, who is compared not absurdly in some respects to Muhammad; Polybius <note>A Greek historian (c. 200–118 BC) who analyzed the rise of the Roman Republic.</note> says truly: *This variety of superstitions exists for this purpose: that by instilling a fear of the gods, military minds might be more effectively kept to their duty.* Examples of this are found in the works of Tacitus.

### XXVI.

<margin>Why Augustus wisely adopted several successors.</margin>
Augustus acted wisely in adopting not just one, but many successors, so that, as our author <note>Referring to Ammirato.</note> says, he might stand firm upon multiple fortifications. For this reason, Seneca calls them the "supports of the court" <note>original: *adminicula aulae*</note>. Suetonius calls them "reinforcements of dominion" <note>original: *subsidia dominationis*</note>. Elsewhere Tacitus writes: *A house full of Caesars—a young man, a son, and grown grandsons—offered a check to the desires of Sejanus* <note>Lucius Aelius Sejanus, the ambitious commander of the Praetorian Guard under Tiberius who attempted to seize power.</note>. Even for this reason alone, the continuation of the Roman Empire for a thousand and so many hundreds of years is owed to Augustus. The Turkish Emperors are not ignorant of this same principle.

### XXVII.

<margin>Whether a successor should be designated while the Prince is still living.</margin>
Here it is asked, should a successor be designated while the Prince is still alive? Certainly so; although this seems dangerous to many. Thus Tullia, in the first book of Livy <note>Titus Livius (59 BC – 17 AD), author of the monumental history of Rome, *Ab Urbe Condita*.</note>, said she desired a husband who preferred to have the kingdom rather than to hope for it. Nevertheless, the change of Princes is always dangerous. Otho <note>Roman Emperor during the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 AD).</note> shrewdly remarked: *The transitions of power are more favorable for daring attempts.* Elsewhere Tacitus says: *Action and daring were necessary while Galba’s authority was waning and Piso's had not yet taken root.*

<summary>This page discusses the political utility of religion in maintaining military discipline and the strategic necessity of designating multiple successors to ensure the stability of the state during transitions of power.</summary>
<keywords>public law, religion and statecraft, Numa Pompilius, Augustus, succession planning, political stability, Roman history, Machiavelli</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the structures of power and succession, shifting from the general strategies of Augustus and Seneca to the specific titles granted to imperial heirs and the sovereign rights regarding public assemblies and the minting of currency.</meta>

Disputation I.

### XXVIII.

Furthermore, heirs have been called "Princes of Youth," that is, Princes of the Military <note>original: *Principes militiæ*; the term "youth" (juventutis) was historically used in Rome to refer to the class of men eligible for military service.</note> (for I find the term "youth" was used this way in ancient times), or "the chosen ones of the youth" <note>original: *πρόκριτοι τῆς νεότητος*, as found in the works of Johannes Zonaras, a 12th-century Byzantine chronicler.</note>, likewise "successors to the rule" <note>original: *διάδοχοι τῆς ἀρχῆς*, as found in the writings of Dio Cassius.</note>, also "heirs and partners in power," also "Augusti" and "Caesars" in the works of Tacitus and *Ammianus* <note>Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–397 AD), a Roman soldier and historian.</note>, and likewise "Germanici": for this is what Vitellius <note>Aulus Vitellius, Roman Emperor for eight months in 69 AD.</note> named his son. Later, in the time of Justinian, they were called "Most Noble" <note>original: *Nobilissimi*</note>, according to Procopius. Today in Germany they are called "Kings of the Romans," in France "Dauphins," in Spain "Infantes," and in Scotland "Princes of Scotland."

### XXIX.

<margin>The rights of assemblies belong to the supreme command.</margin>
The rights of assemblies are a matter of supreme command <term>sovereignty</term>, and therefore Tiberius craftily transferred them—as he did all things—from the Field of Mars <note>The *Campus Martius*, where the Roman people traditionally gathered to vote.</note> to the senators, or as Dio suggests, more likely to himself. However, they differ from the assemblies which our historians call *General Days* <note>original: *Dies Generales*; these were formal diets or parliamentary meetings in the Holy Roman Empire and medieval Europe.</note>.

### XXX.

<margin>Whom the assemblies bind.</margin>
An assembly does not so much bind the one who proclaims it as it binds those to whom it is proclaimed. Nor did Turnus Herdonius <note>A Latin leader who opposed Tarquinius Superbus and was ultimately framed and executed by him.</note> advise the Latins correctly when he suggested that, since Tarquin did not present himself, they should go home and observe the day of the council no more than the man who proclaimed it. Conversely, Caesar rightly regarded the Treveri <note>A powerful tribe of the Belgae in eastern Gaul.</note> as defectors because they neither came to the councils nor obeyed his command.

### XXXI.

<margin>To whom the rights of coinage belong.</margin>
Formerly, the rights of coinage <note>original: *jura monetarum*</note> belonged to the Prince alone. And for the "secrets of rule" <term>arcana imperii</term>, the Turkish Emperors <note>The Sultans of the Ottoman Empire.</note> observe these rights today. Li-

It uses examples from Roman history, the Byzantine Empire, and contemporary European kingdoms to illustrate these legal principles.</summary>

# On Public Law.

that the Kings of Hungary once held the same right and kept it sacredly, the illustrious writer Otto of Freising <note>A 12th-century German bishop and historian, famous for his chronicle *The Two Cities*.</note> bears witness. Now, with the state changed, these rights have also been divided.

### XXXII.

Courteous respect for an empire does not take away liberty. Tacitus expresses this in Book 2 of his *Annals*: "For," he says, "although Pharaates <note>Pharaates IV, King of Parthia.</note> had driven back the armies and Roman generals, he directed all the duties of those showing respect toward Augustus." Elsewhere he calls it "reverence toward the Roman people." As Proculus <note>A prominent 1st-century Roman jurist and founder of the Proculian school of law.</note> says, this is added so that it may be understood that the other party is not [entirely] free.

### XXXIII.

<margin>The rights of allies, etc., and patronage are distinct.</margin>
The rights of allies, brothers, and confederates, as well as the rights of patronage (as Festus and Pliny call them), are wrongly confused by some. Furthermore, following this Roman custom, today the Turks call the republics of the Venetians and the Ragusans <note>The Republic of Ragusa, modern-day Dubrovnik.</note> their "relatives" <note>original: *cognatas*; implying a kinship-based alliance or a protected status.</note>.

### XXXIV.

Critognatus <note>A Gallic chieftain of the Arverni during Caesar's Gallic Wars.</note> did not rightly call a surrender "slavery." I feel differently about the surrender of the people of Calais in Froissart <note>Jean Froissart (c. 1337–1405), a French chronicler of the Hundred Years' War.</note>. However, Livy correctly says of the Campanians: the condition of those who have come into trust <term>fides</term> <note>In Roman law, *fides* implied a moral and legal obligation of protection and loyalty.</note> not through a treaty, but through surrender, is quite another thing.

### XXXV.

The Paetian agreements with Vologeses <note>Agreements made by the Roman general Paetus with Vologeses I of Parthia after the Battle of Rhandeia (62 AD), often viewed as a disgrace to Rome.</note> were scarcely agreements at all. Indeed, Paetus was worthy of punishment because he did not strike a bargain in exchange for that danger. I feel almost the same about the peace made at Caudium <note>The Battle of the Caudine Forks (321 BC), where a trapped Roman army was forced to surrender and pass under a yoke of spears.</note>; which was a formal promise <term>sponsio</term>, not a treaty <term>foedus</term>; although the Caudines called it a public violation of faith. It is praiseworthy among the English that those about to set out to foreign lands for the sake of waging war to their King

### XXXVI.

<margin>Consuls, etc., were of royal power.</margin>
Consuls, Triumvirs, Decemvirs, and Dictators possessed royal power <term>regio imperio; supreme authority characteristic of a king</term>, although Cabotius <note>Vincenzo Caboti (1560–1605), an Italian jurist known for his works on Roman law and politics.</note> argues otherwise. Indeed, the expulsion of the Kings brought only a certain appearance <note>original: *simulacrum*; a shadow or likeness</note> of liberty to the Roman people, as witnessed by Livy himself in Book 2. Cicero, in *On the Laws*, states: "Let there be two with royal power." Nor (as others think) does Tacitus disagree.

### XXXVII.

<margin>The religion of the Romans concerning sacred places and things.</margin>
The Roman people held great religious devotion and reverence for sacred places and things even in times of war. Thus, Tacitus sharply rebukes the soldiers because they even forced the gods into their plunder by despoiling temples in the city and carrying off gold. Most praiseworthily, Suleiman <note>Suleiman the Magnificent (1494–1566), Sultan of the Ottoman Empire, noted here for his restraint during the Siege of Rhodes in 1522.</note> always abstained from sacred things during the conquest of Rhodes and other wars. Here a question arises: *Whether Emperor Frederick <note>Likely referring to Frederick I "Barbarossa," who was sometimes accused of seizing church wealth for military needs.</note>, when pressed by a lack of provisions, committed sacrilege by violating sacred places?* I say no, especially since he afterwards rewarded those bishoprics with a notable and more ample benefit, as historians testify. Tacitus also rightly noted concerning Pompey that he entered the Temple of Jerusalem by the right of victory <term>jure victoriae; the legal principle that a conqueror acquires rights over the defeated's property</term>.

### XXXVIII.

To stir up things beyond civil limits, to be stubborn, fierce, turbulent, or violent (which in Ammianus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century Roman historian.</note> is "to kick back against Majesty" <note>original: *recalcitrare Majestati*</note>) are contrasted with what Tacitus calls being modest, obedient, and ready for service. The Greeks call this "pleasing" <note>original: *ἀρέσκον* (areskon)</note>; the French and Italians call such a person a "courtier" <note>original: *un courtisan*</note>; the Germans call him a *Hofmann* <note>A man of the court.</note>. Those [former] types are they who desired the restoration of their ancient liberty, whom Aristotle call—

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the "Masters of Tyranny" and transitions into a discussion of the legitimacy of state gifts, the ethics of warfare, and the preservation of historical records. On Public Law. 15

...rivals and impediments to power <note>original Greek: ἀντιτέχνους καὶ πρὸς τὴν ἀρχὴν ἐμποδιών</note>. These were those who favored the rule of the Caesars and, to speak with the poet Horace, advanced through sycophancy <note>original: *obsequio grassabantur*; describing those who gained influence through flattering their superiors rather than by merit</note>. Those whom Tacitus calls "Masters of Tyranny" <term>Magistros Dominationis; teachers of absolute rule</term>, are today commonly called Machiavellians.

### XXXIX.

Deservedly, the gifts—or rather the extravagances—of Nero were revoked by the Senate. There is more than one example of this kind of reversal in the kingdom of France <note>Likely referring to the "droit de joyeux avènement" or similar laws regarding the revocation of alienated royal property</note>. Rightly, however, Nerva and Trajan preserved the grants of Domitian <note>Domitian was a tyrant, but his legal grants were often upheld for the sake of civil stability</note>.

### XL.

<margin>
Enemies are
to be
avenged not
by trickery
but
openly.
</margin>
Prince Adgandestrius <note>A leader of the Chatti tribe</note> promised in letters the death of Arminius <note>The Germanic leader who defeated the Romans at the Teutoburg Forest</note>, if poison were sent to carry out the killing. The Roman Senate replied that the Roman people avenge their enemies not through trickery or hidden means, but openly and under arms. This was a statement worthy of Roman majesty. Livy, in Book 1, calls this the "Roman art." The matter was far different in the cases of Judith and Mucius <note>Judith assassinated Holofernes by deception, and Mucius Scaevola attempted to assassinate Lars Porsena by stealth</note>. I add that an enemy is not everywhere rightly killed, although the leader of the pragmatists <note>A derogatory reference to Niccolò Machiavelli</note> thinks so. For according to the testimony of Valerius Maximus, this was for the Romans not to earn victory, but to buy it.

### XLI.

There is no other cause for the loss of memory of great events than the scarcity of writers; whatever others may claim regarding changes in religion, floods, and diseases. By this same failing, so many monstrous opinions regarding the Jews, Christians, and Christ himself arose in the works of Tacitus, Suetonius, and others. By the same fate, the acts or documents that were called "public," or as Zosimus calls them, the "registers of republics," have perished; In O-

16. Disputation I.

...among the Eastern peoples there were secret charters in which a great part of public law was contained.

### XLII.

The Germans, as well as the Sarmatians and several other nations, were overcome by the Romans not through superior courage <note>original: *virtute*; often meaning "manliness" or "innate bravery" in this context</note>, but by the type of fighting and weaponry, as Tacitus states; or, as Caesar says, they were defeated not by courage, but by strategy and planning. To this pertains what the greatest of commanders [Caesar] writes: that the Romans were violently terrified by the similarity of the arms which the Aedui <note>A Gallic tribe allied with Rome</note> carried. One might say the same concerning the American war <note>Likely referring to the Spanish conquest of the Americas, where European technology and tactics provided a similar advantage over indigenous populations</note>.

### XLIII.

And therefore, the Barbarians were not more easily defeated by the Romans than when they were driven into narrow spaces. On the contrary, fighting in narrow spaces was more suitable for the Romans than for the Spanish soldier. Yet, there was a remarkable discipline among those same Romans for loosening and expanding their battle line. Tacitus writes: *The twenty-first legion, massed together in a more open space than the others, withstood those rushing forward, and soon repulsed them.* Caesar, in his account of the Gallic War, ordered the standards to be advanced and the manipulos <term>maniple; a flexible tactical unit of the Roman legion consisting of about 120 men</term> to be thinned out, so that they could use their swords more easily.

### XLIV.

<margin>
A new Prince
ought to
begin his
reign with
an act of
mercy.
</margin>
Even a new Prince ought to begin his reign with an act of mercy, as Livia rightly advised Augustus. Thus, regarding Mithridates, Tacitus remarks that he was more cruel than was conducive to a new kingdom. The Tuscan writer <note>Niccolò Machiavelli, born in Florence, Tuscany</note> disagrees—whether out of ignorance, or indeed to serve the cruelty of Borgia? That saying is dangerous: "let them hate, provided they fear" <note>original: *oderint dum metuant*; a famous line associated with the tyrant Caligula</note>; it is most dangerous of all to keep a people in continuous fear. Antonius, in the works of Tacitus, was of the same mind: *Vitellius's forces would still have strength if they reflected, but would be fierce if they despaired.*

<summary>This page discusses how Roman military success was rooted in tactical discipline and weaponry rather than raw bravery, and argues that a stable reign must be founded on clemency rather than the Machiavellian reliance on fear.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on public law and statecraft, moving from military tactics to the "secrets of empire" (arcana imperii) and the nuances of political control, specifically how rulers maintain power through a mix of clemency and administrative secrecy.</meta>

# On Public Law.

### XLV.

And so wisely did Germanicus <note>A popular Roman general and nephew of Emperor Tiberius</note>, after he had reduced Cappadocia to the form of a province, diminish certain of the royal taxes so that—as Tacitus <note>The primary Roman historian used as a source throughout this text</note> puts it—the Roman empire might be expected to be milder. Therefore, this is well-known in public affairs: it holds even more true in matters of religion, which, like Melancholy <note>In the 17th century, "melancholy" was seen as a physical ailment that could be aggravated by harsh medical treatments; the author uses this as a metaphor for religious dissent</note>, is inflamed more and more by excessive remedies, as Augustus testified.

### XLVI.

Tacitus spoke truly concerning Marius and Sulla <note>Roman commanders whose civil wars led to the end of the Roman Republic</note>: that they turned liberty, conquered by arms, into Domination <note>original: *dominationem*; here meaning absolute or despotic rule</note>. For these were Kingdoms in reality, not Dictatorships, whatever others may say.

<margin>*Secrets and of domination*</margin>

### XLVII.

*The secrets of empire <note>original: *Arcana imperii*</note> are certain deep and internal laws or privileges for preserving the original status of the empire or the Republic.* Just as with the secrets of Domination (which to the Italians and French is "reason of state" <note>original: *ragion di stato*</note>). The Germans do not express this term, except perhaps through the "Imperial Estate" <note>original: *Reichsstand*</note> or "Imperial Matters" <note>original: *Reichssachen*</note> (these are certain secret privileges for preserving rule, introduced for the sake of the public good). To these Tacitus opposes the *shameful acts of domination* <note>original: *flagitia dominationis*</note> (which the Italians call *the bad reason of state* <note>original: *la cattiva ragione di stato*</note>), by which faith and religion are violated.

### XLVIII.

Agrippina <note>Agrippina the Younger, mother of Nero</note> makes a request of her husband, but does not obtain it. Whereupon Tacitus says: But Caesar [Claudius], not unaware of how much was being asked of the Republic, [treated it as] a Secret, or rather a right of domination. By the same art, Octavia, the daughter of the Emperor Claudius, is betrothed to Nero, which, says the Historian, was destined to open up even greater things.

### XLIX.

By the ordinance of Augustus, to none of the Senators

This is a secret token <note>original: ὑποκύφιον (*hypokyphion*); a Greek term used here to describe a hidden mark of sovereignty</note> or a sign of royal Domination: just as it was also permitted to no one to write privately to the Senate except for the Prince.

## L.

<margin>
The secrets of domination and the rights of domination are distinct.
</margin>

Furthermore, I distinguish the <term>secrets of domination</term> <note>original: *arcana dominationis*; the hidden tactics used by a ruler to maintain control</note> from those which are the <term>rights of domination</term> <note>original: *jura dominationis*; the legal or quasi-legal powers a ruler possesses by virtue of their office</note>, which the Florentine writer <note>A reference to Niccolò Machiavelli, whose name was often avoided or used as a shorthand for cynical statecraft</note> and all others seem to confuse.

## LI.

And thus they condemn the "rights of domination." Yet, it was not by this right that Nero turned toward the violence of domination when he [punished] Vespasian <note>Likely referring to the incident where Nero banned Vespasian from his court for falling asleep during a musical performance, nearly leading to his execution</note> without any crime, accuser, or witness. Nor was it by this right that Petronius Turpilianus and Cingonius Varro perished, unheard and innocent, an act which brought significant hatred upon the Emperor Galba. Tiberius behaved well—not according to his usual custom—when he said: "Power should not be used where the law can settle the matter." And concerning Philopoemen <note>The "Last of the Greeks" and general of the Achaean League</note>, the general of the Achaeans, Livy <note>The Roman historian Titus Livius</note> writes: "He did not desire them to be safe, but because he did not want them to perish without a cause."

## LII.

Yet, by this right, it is seen that Spurius Maelius <note>A wealthy Roman killed for allegedly seeking to become king</note> was rightly slain by Servilius, the Master of the Horse; Sejanus <note>The disgraced advisor to Tiberius</note> by Tiberius; and not so long ago, the Duke of Guise by Henry III, King of the French <note>A reference to the 1588 assassination of the Duke of Guise, which Henry III ordered to regain control of his kingdom</note>, was killed by force.

## LIII.

Similarly, those who use this "right" to veil and defend the violation of faith, or even the desertion of religion, are in vain. It was correctly said...

# On Public Law.

...laws should be adapted to religion, not the reverse.

### LIV.

Therefore, I do not include here the modern fratricides of the Turks <note>A reference to the Ottoman practice of "royal fratricide," where a new Sultan might execute his brothers to prevent civil war</note>, nor Tarquin’s striking off of the poppy-heads <note>According to legend, King Tarquin the Proud signaled to his son to execute the leading men of a city by silently striking the tallest poppies in his garden with a stick</note>. However, I believe that the asylum opened by **Romulus** <note>The legendary founder of Rome, who established a sanctuary to attract new citizens</note> was established by this right. And the exile of **Lucius Tarquinius** can be excused by this right, as can the abduction of the **Sabine Virgins**; likewise, that religious devotion of the Romans to helping their allies. Although the most elegant of the Belgians <note>Likely referring to the famous scholar Justus Lipsius, known for his refined Latin and political writings</note> thinks otherwise, **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius of Halicarnassus, a Greek historian of Rome</note> is more correct: *The purpose of empires is that no harm can be done.*

### LV.

<margin>It is shown</margin>
In **Ammianus** <note>The historian Ammianus Marcellinus</note>, it seems to be referred also to the <term>right of domination</term> <note>original: *jus dominationis*</note> that *no one should address the army except the Emperor.* **Julian** <note>The Emperor Julian "the Apostate"</note> did not want this, [saying] by way of examples: *lest,* he said, *Marcellinus should seem to have striven for that which Augustus believed was owed to himself alone.*

### LVI.

Likewise, [the rule] that no one should hunt for the devotion of the soldiers through bribery and corruption. For this reason, **Tiberius** took it very badly when, as recorded in the 6th book of the *Annals* <note>The *Annals* by Tacitus</note>, **Junius Gallio** had proposed that the Praetorian guards, their pay having been increased, should obtain the right to sit in the fourteen rows <note>The fourteen rows in the Roman theater reserved for the wealthy equestrian class; Gallio's proposal was seen as an attempt to win the soldiers' favor away from the Emperor</note>; he rebuked him so vehemently by letter, as if reproaching him to his face: *What does he have to do with the soldiers? It is only fitting that they receive neither the Emperor's words*—mark this well in relation to the previous note from Ammianus—*nor rewards, except from the Emperor himself, etc.* And the true **Marcus Antoninus** <note>The Emperor Marcus Aurelius</note> warns: *that one should not have such a man among the troops whom the soldiers listen to or look upon too willingly.*

### LVII.

It was a <term>secret of domination</term> <note>original: *arcanum dominationis*</note>, not a secret of the state, *that no one should enter Alexandria without the permission of Augustus.* <note>Augustus controlled Egypt as a personal province rather than a senatorial one, because of its vital role in Rome's grain supply</note>

20
-> Disputatio I. <-

...should enter. It was with this same strategy that **Vespasian** <note>Roman Emperor, reigned 69–79 AD</note>, when he was about to seize the empire, assigned various provinces to different people, while he himself held onto the keys to Egypt <note>Egypt was the "breadbasket" of Rome; controlling its grain supply allowed Vespasian to starve the capital into submission if necessary</note>.

### LVIII.

Thus, by this right, in the council of the three most powerful freedmen <note>Claudius relied heavily on his high-ranking formerly enslaved administrators: Narcissus, Pallas, and Callistus</note>, **Julia Agrippina** was preferred over **Lollia Paulina** and **Aelia Paetina** to be the Emperor's wife, even though she was the niece <note>original: *consobrina*; Agrippina the Younger was the daughter of Claudius's brother Germanicus. This marriage required a change in law to allow uncle-niece unions</note> of **Claudius**. This was done so that, as **Tacitus** says, a woman of proven fertility and in the prime of youth might not carry the brilliance of the Caesars <note>The prestige of the royal bloodline</note> into another family house. The part that follows is detestable: that nothing at home was considered immodest, unless it served the purpose of domination.

### LIX.

I repeatedly refer back to the <term>secret of domination</term> <note>original: *arcanum dominationis*; a political concept describing the hidden techniques rulers use to maintain power</note> that no one should be permitted to surround coastal cities with walls. This is because citizens are easily incited to rebellion and desertion by such fortifications, as the jurist **Ulpian** testifies. **Agrippa** <note>Herod Agrippa I, King of Judea</note> had begun a wall around **Jerusalem**; however, fearing that the Emperor **Claudius** might interpret the magnificence of the construction as a suspicion of revolution <note>original: *novarum rerum*, literally "new things," the standard Latin term for political upheaval or coup</note> and discord, he ceased the work after only the foundations had been laid.

<margin>The appointment of magistrates belongs to the rights of empire.</margin>
### LX.

The appointment of magistrates belongs to the rights of empire. According to **Modestinus** <note>A famous Roman jurist</note>, the creation of magistrates belongs to the care of the Prince <note>The Ruler or Head of State</note>, not to the favor of the people. And **Zonaras** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine chronicler and legal scholar</note> says: The appointment of magistrates is the greatest part of the Imperial duty.

### LXI.

Therefore, when **Afinius Gallus** proposed that the elections for magistrates should be held for a five-year period: "There is no doubt," says **Tacitus**, "that the secrets of empire <note>original: *arcana imperii*</note> were being probed." For the extension of commands was threatening the present state of the Republic. And so

<summary>This page explores the "secrets of state" regarding dynastic security through marriage, the prohibition of private fortifications to prevent rebellion, and the ruler's exclusive right to appoint public officials.</summary>
<keywords>Vespasian, Agrippina the Younger, Claudius, secrets of state, fortifications, Jerusalem, Herod Agrippa, appointment of magistrates, Tacitus, arcana imperii</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the tenure of magistrates and governors, transitioning into a distinction between the formal "rights of rule" and the "secrets of state" (arcana imperii). It follows from the previous page's mention of how long officials should stay in their posts to prevent them from becoming too powerful.</meta>

<header>On Public Law. 21</header>

...**Tiberius** <note>Roman Emperor, reigned 14–37 AD</note> was right: men grow arrogant with a one-year appointment; what if they were to exercise that honor for five years? But you will say: Why, then, did he keep most governors in the same jurisdictions until the end of their lives? The reasons for this are weightier and will be revealed during the course of our discussion.

### LXII.

However, **Augustus** <note>The first Roman Emperor, reigned 27 BC – 14 AD</note> established most shrewdly and wisely that magistrates, having laid down their office, should remain in the city for five years before departing for a province. This was, of course, to ensure that commands were not held continuously, as **Merserus** <note>Josias Mercier, a 16th-century French scholar</note> notes from the works of **Dio** <note>Cassius Dio, Roman historian</note> and **Suetonius** <note>Roman biographer and historian</note>.

### LXIII.

<margin>*Secrets distinguished from the Right of Rule.*</margin>
Furthermore, I distinguish the <term>secrets of state</term> <note>original: *arcana imperii*; the pragmatic, often hidden strategies of power</note> or the "mysteries" of empire from the "right of rule" in a certain way. **Jovius** <note>Paolo Giovio, an Italian physician and historian</note> says: *Francis <note>King Francis I of France</note>, when dying, communicated to his son Henry the secrets appropriate for guarding the kingdom, and by which specific arts he might best earn the steadfast devotion of the subject nations.* He spoke correctly: these are "secrets," not "rights" of empire. In this same way, the Emperor <note>Likely referring to the Byzantine Emperor Justinian or the legal codes</note> speaks of the *secrets of Jurisprudence*, and **Nicephorus Gregoras** <note>A Byzantine historian and theologian</note> mentions the *secrets of Theology, at which the common people gape, just as cattle do at grass and pastures.*

### LXIV.

This difference between the law and the secrets of state is apparent even from the famous line of **Tacitus** <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus, Roman historian known for his analysis of power</note>: *The secret of empire having been divulged, that an Emperor could be made elsewhere than at Rome.* <note>original: "Evulgato imperii arcano, posse Principem alibi quam Romæ fieri"; referring to the Year of the Four Emperors when provincial armies began naming their own leaders as Emperor</note> This is a "secret," not a "right" of empire. Nor is anyone so dull-witted <note>original: *blennus*</note> that they would refer this to the formal rights of rule, as it seemed to threaten the Emperor’s safety. Furthermore, what in public law is the *divulging of state secrets* is, in private law, nearly the same as *baring one's inner thoughts and opening up the secrets of a business.*

<meta>catchword: From these</meta>

<summary>This page explores the strategic timing of political appointments and introduces the concept of "arcana imperii" (secrets of state) as distinct from formal public law. It highlights how rulers like Augustus used waiting periods to prevent governors from seizing too much power and how political "secrets" are practical techniques of survival rather than legal rights.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of political power. While the previous page distinguished the "right of rule" (the legal basis of authority) from the "secrets of state" (the practical, hidden methods of maintaining that authority), this page introduces a third category: the "shams" or "images" of power used to maintain the illusion of liberty while absolute rule is exercised.</meta>

# First Disputation

->LXV.<-

<margin>State secrets distinguished from the shams of power.</margin>
From these "secrets of state" <note>original: *arcana imperii*; the pragmatic, often hidden strategies of power</note>, one must also distinguish the **shams of power and liberty** <note>original: *simulacra imperii & libertatis*</note>—or rather, these shams should be contrasted with the secrets. For, as **Tacitus** <note>A famous Roman historian known for his analysis of power</note> says elsewhere, these are **empty secrets** <note>original: *Arcana inania*</note>; in **Aristotle's** <note>The Greek philosopher</note> view, they are **sophisms and fabrications** <note>original: *σοφίσματα καὶ κατασκευάσματα*</note>. Not dissimilar to these are the terms used by **Plato**: **eye-pleasing and well-faced** <note>original: *ἐνόφθαλμα καὶ ἐμμερόσωπα*; essentially, things that look good on the surface but lack substance</note>. **Pliny** <note>Roman author and administrator</note> calls it the **shadow of liberty** <note>original: *libertatis umbra*</note>, while Tacitus calls them the **empty and deceptive aspects of rule** <note>original: *imperii inania & subdola*</note>, as well as the **pretense of liberty** <note>original: *species libertatis*</note>. **Velleius Paterculus** <note>A Roman historian</note> refers to it as **rule without the reality** <note>original: *imperium sine re*</note>. **Otto of Freising** <note>A medieval German bishop and historian</note> writes that the heirs of **Berthold of Zähringen**, Duke of Swabia, held such a status: they were "Princes without a Principality," possessing the title alone but having no part in the actual substance of power.

->LXVI.<-

Opposed to these shams is **force and true power** <note>original: *vis ac vera potentia*</note> as described by Paterculus, and what Tacitus calls the **force of dominion** <note>original: *vis dominationis*</note>. Therefore, those words of Tacitus—**the Consuls and Praetors kept their own appearance** <note>original: *Sua Consulibus, sua Praetoribus, species*</note>—denote only a sham and an image of the former Republic.

->LXVII.<-

For **Augustus** <note>The first Roman Emperor</note>, having seized the empire, most prudently left the titles of the traditional magistrates unchanged; the **sharp writer from Etruria** <note>A reference to Niccolò Machiavelli, the Florentine political philosopher</note> would disagree with him. **Tiberius** <note>Augustus's successor</note> also followed Augustus's lead, rightly initiating all business through the Consuls, as if the old Republic still existed and he were hesitant about his own power to command. Furthermore, **Machiavelli** is impious in that passage.

->LXVIII.<-

<margin>The establishment of taxes belongs to the supreme Prince.</margin>
The establishment or imposition of <term>taxes</term> <note>original: *vectigalium*; specifically customs duties or indirect taxes</note> belongs to the supreme Prince, as the jurist **Hermogenianus** <note>A Roman legal authority</note> testifies. **Nero** <note>The Roman Emperor</note> granted this right only to the Consuls and Tribunes out of a "sharp desire for liberty," as it seemed (according to Tacitus).

->LXIX.<-

**Tiridates**, King of the Parthians, requested from the Romans that he not be required to surrender his sword, nor be barred by an embrace from the provinces...

<meta>catchword: vincias (pro-vincias)</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes how absolute rulers use "shams" or "images" of the old Republic to hide their true power from the public. It also asserts that the power to levy taxes is a fundamental right of the sovereign, using Roman history to illustrate these political strategies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "shams of liberty" (simulacra libertatis), providing historical examples from the Roman Empire and the Middle Ages where rulers used hollow honors and traditions to placate the public while retaining absolute control.</meta>

### On Public Law

...governing provinces <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing the word "provincias" from the previous page</note> should be kept away, or stand before their doors, and that there should be as much honor in Rome for them as for the consuls. Foolishly; for having scorned the actual power, he pursued only empty things and the image of liberty. Wherefore our Writer <note>referring to the Roman historian Tacitus</note> sharply says: *Indeed, for one accustomed to foreign pride, there was no knowledge of us, among whom the <term>right of rule</term> <note>original: *jus imperii*; the legal authority to command</note> holds weight, while empty things are passed along.*

->LXX.<-

**Tiberius** <note>The second Roman Emperor, known for his complex and often deceptive relationship with the Senate</note> indirectly rebuked the Senators <note>original: *Patres*; literally "The Fathers"</note> via letters because they referred everything to the Prince <note>the Emperor</note>. I refer this to the **shams of liberty**. Thus Tiberius spoke grandly: *Something greater and more lofty,* he said, *is required in a Prince.* Of course, he granted to the Senate those matters which were of no importance and were tied to unpopularity <note>original: *invidia*; the "envy" or hatred resulting from making difficult or controversial decisions</note>, while he himself held the true force of power.

->LXXI.<-

It was also a **sham of liberty** when **Ulpian** <note>A prominent Roman jurist and legal authority</note> warns a Proconsul not to enter a province through any other part than that which is customary to enter; *For,* he says, *the provincials make much of having that custom and such <term>prerogatives</term> <note>rights or privileges held by specific groups</note> preserved for them.* To this pertains those words of **Velleius Paterculus** <note>A Roman historian</note> in Book 2: *Marcus Livius Drusus,* he says, *in those very things which he was striving for on behalf of the Senate, had the Senate as an adversary—they did not understand that if any measures for the common people's benefit were handled by him, they were done as a bait and a lure for the sake of the multitude, so that they might allow greater things in exchange for smaller concessions.*

He who is wise willingly indulges a people with such things. Just as cleverly and prudently, **Henry IV** <note>The Holy Roman Emperor (1050–1106)</note>, when asked by someone why he permitted **Rudolf** <note>Rudolf of Rheinfelden, a rebel who claimed Henry’s throne but died in battle</note>—who was not King—to be buried with royal honors, replied: *I wish all my enemies lay as honorably.*

<summary>This page explores historical "shams of liberty," where rulers like Tiberius and Henry IV granted symbolic honors or maintained traditions to soothe the public while keeping actual political power for themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Tiberius, Ulpian, Velleius Paterculus, Marcus Livius Drusus, Henry IV, Rudolf of Rheinfelden, shams of liberty, jus imperii, political strategy, Roman Senate</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of political theory and the "shams of liberty," moving from the historical examples of Roman emperors to the practical application of political wisdom based on the specific form of a government.</meta>

<header>Disputation I.</header>

### LXXII.

<margin>
Prudence varies according to the variety of the State.
</margin>
Political prudence varies according to the variety of the Commonwealth. Tacitus notes this, following Aristotle and Cicero: Formerly, when the common people were powerful, or when the Senators <note>original: *Patres*; literally "The Fathers," the Roman aristocratic class</note> held sway, it was necessary to know the nature of the masses and the methods by which they might be moderately managed. Those who had most thoroughly learned the character of the Senate and the elite were considered clever in their times and wise. Thus, with the state of the Commonwealth changed—for it is no different than if one man were to rule—it has been useful to investigate and pass down these matters, etc.

<margin>
Secrets of state vary according to its form.
</margin>
Wherefore <term>sophisms</term> <note>original: *σοφίσματα*; here meaning political maneuvers or "tricks of the trade"</note>, or <term>secrets of state</term> <note>original: *arcana imperii*; the hidden principles or "mysteries" of power specific to a regime</note>, also vary according to the form of rule: the secrets of our Empire are one thing, those of the Kingdom of France another, those of the Venetians another, and those of the people of Nuremberg <note>original: *Noricorum*</note> yet another. The Germans express this in a way as **loyal to the King** or **loyal to the City** <note>original German: *gut Königſch, gut Städtisch*; referring to whether one's political allegiance lies with the monarchy or the independent city-states</note>.

### LXXIII.

The Elector of Mainz <note>one of the most powerful prince-electors of the Holy Roman Empire</note> said in the Frankfurt assembly—as recorded by Sleidanus in Book 1—that the modern Empire is Aristocratic. We call it **mixed**. Nor do we admit here that saying of the Tenecteri <note>a Germanic tribe mentioned by Tacitus in his *Histories*</note>: *Liberty and Masters do not easily mix.* Jovius <note>Paolo Giovio, a 16th-century historian</note> spoke most falsely when he said: *The rights of rule in Germany are in the hands of 70 cities.*

### LXXIV.

The Byzantines built their city in a fertile place and by the sea. The Chalcedonians, by contrast, chose a sterile site. Which acted more correctly? I agree with the Oracle given to the Byzantines by Pythian Apollo: that they should seek a seat "opposite the land of the blind" <note>A famous historical anecdote; the Chalcedonians were called "blind" because they founded their city first but missed the obviously superior site of Byzantium on the opposite shore</note>. In this dispute I follow Aristotle, whatever Plato may say, and whatever others bring forward from Caesar regarding the Belgians, the Ambiani, the Suebi, and other nations.

### LXXV.

There was a sharp disagreement between Marcellus and Hel-

# On Public Law.

<margin>
How ambassadors <note>original: *Legati*; in a Roman context, these were often senatorial envoys</note> are to be chosen.
</margin>
Helvidius Priscus <note>a Roman senator known for his Stoic opposition to imperial overreach</note> debated whether ambassadors should be chosen by the votes of a magistrate under oath, or instead by lot—that is, using urns. Although Marcellus was motivated by a personal sense of shame—as Tacitus says—fearing that he might be seen as less worthy if others were elected while he was passed over, his opinion nevertheless prevailed, and rightly so.

### LXXVI.

But if votes are to be admitted, is it better for them to be open or secret? We repeatedly award the prize to the "Ballot Law" <note>original: *Lex Tabellaria*; a series of Roman laws that replaced oral voting with secret stone or wooden tablets to prevent intimidation</note>; although Pliny the Younger, in his *Letters*, called it a "flawed remedy."

<margin>
Whether it is correctly inferred that adultery was a capital offense at that time, based on the fact that the adulterous Apuleia, as well as the daughter and granddaughter of Augustus, were punished with death and exile.
</margin>

### LXXVII.

Apuleia was rightly charged with treason <note>original: *maiestas*; short for *crimen laesae maiestatis*, a crime against the "majesty" or dignity of the Roman state or its ruler</note> on account of adultery, as were the daughter and granddaughter of Augustus. Their adulterous partners were treated no differently than Horatius in *Book 1* of Livy, who was charged with high treason <note>original: *perduellio*; an archaic form of treason involving internal hostility to the state</note> for killing his sister, according to the ancient grammarian Charisius. For it was considered a kind of hostility to the state to kill a sister during a time of war. For this reason, I find it difficult to understand Tacitus's comment about Augustus—that he overstepped the mercy of his ancestors and his own laws.

### LXXVIII.

Thus, the adulterers of these women were rightly punished by death and exile. However, the conclusion some draw from this—that adultery was a capital crime <note>a crime punishable by death</note> at that time—is certainly misplaced in this context. For this specific adultery, committed with the daughter of the Prince <note>the Emperor</note>, was joined with the crime of treason, so as to ensure that no one should violate the Imperial House.

### LXXIX.

Certain things creep into the Republic against good morals which are better changed of their own accord than by sharp coercion; indeed, coercion often brings more harm than benefit to the State. Tiberius acted prudently. *How unseemly—*

# Disputation I.

And regarding Felix, Tacitus says that he inflamed faults with excessive remedies. And regarding Manlius, Livy writes in Book 6 that he provoked sedition with a remedy.

-> LXXX . <-

<margin>Whether luxury should be restrained in a flourishing Republic.</margin>
From this it is asked: *should luxury be restrained in a flourishing Republic?* Quintus Haterius, of Consular rank, affirmed this based on the Fannian, Licinian, Aemilian, and Julian laws <note>These were "sumptuary laws" designed to limit extravagance in banquets, clothing, and lifestyle to preserve traditional Roman frugality</note>. Afinius Gallus argued against it, using arguments that were merely plausible on the surface but not absurd for that particular time. Whoever wishes to attempt such a thing must wait for the right moment; therefore, Tiberius added that it was not the time for censorship.

-> LXXX I . <-

I cannot depart from the opinion of *Michel de Montaigne, a French writer* <note>original: *Michaelis Montani*; the famous Renaissance essayist</note>, who is both faithful and learned. However much I might wish that *Cornelius Tacitus* had judged some matters not so much from the certainty of the facts as from his own natural disposition and wisdom—by which, I may say without ambition, he surpassed all other historians. I find more than one example of this.

-> LXXX I I . <-

Tiberius was severe in the administration of law and the exercise of judgments, primarily for this reason: so that he might transport all power and all jurisdiction into himself with a "slow effort" <note>original: *lento nisu*; a quote from the ancient poet Manilius</note>, if I may speak with the ancient poet. Tacitus notes this when he says: *While truth was consulted, liberty was being corrupted.* In this context, I understand "truth" to mean equity or justice. Caesar said: *it is not true that those who could not defend their own borders should occupy those of others.*

-> LXXX I I I . <-

Under Augustus, the authority of the Decrees of the Senate <note>original: *SCtorum*, an abbreviation for *Senatus Consultorum*</note> was great, but under Tiberius it was truly diminished. Because
