<meta>This page continues the legal and historical commentary on the transition of Roman governance, moving from Tiberius’s individual conduct to broader questions of public law, the origins of imperial authority, and the nature of the right to appeal.</meta>

**On Public Law.**

B 2

<margin>Why Senate decrees were called "authorities."</margin>
Cassius Dio and Xiphilinus <note>Dio was a Roman historian; Xiphilinus was a later monk who summarized Dio’s massive history</note> note this. By this name, Tacitus often calls Senate decrees <note>original: *SCta* or *Senatus consulta*</note> not only "authorities" but also "flatteries" on many occasions. For I do not entirely agree with the Prince of Jurisconsults <note>likely referring to Jacques Cujas, a preeminent legal scholar of the Renaissance</note>, who thinks Senate decrees were called "authorities" only for the reason that they had not yet been delivered to the treasury.

### LXXXIV.

Almost nothing was established in the Roman Republic that did not take its origin from the cradle of the empire. It is of such great importance for the foundations of states to be laid correctly. Examples of this are found even in our Tacitus.

### LXXXV.

<margin>Whose right is it to give the signal to the Praetorian guards?</margin>
*Giving the watchword to the Praetorian guards was a specific right of the Emperor.* And you would correctly count this among the signs—indeed, among the rights—of accepted supreme power <note>original: *imperium*</note>.

### LXXXVI.

Regarding Augustus, Tacitus says that after many things were abolished, he gave laws by which the Roman Republic might enjoy peace and a Leader. Hence Plutarch says: when the state is changed, the laws are changed. Hence we say: since today’s state deviates greatly from that former one, it is also necessary that some laws do not suit the modern state. Indeed, on Aristotle’s authority, contrary laws overturn a state.

### LXXXVII.

<margin>Whether the right of appeal belongs to Supreme Power.</margin>
*Whether the right of appeal <note>original: *provocatio*; the legal right of a citizen to appeal a magistrate's decision to the people</note> belongs to supreme power:* Some doubt it; we assert it. And this was the firmest bulwark of the Roman commoners against the Magistrate, as Livy testifies.

### LXXXVIII.

Was there truly a right of appeal from the Kings? I say no, nor does Cicero contradict this. Was there truly an appeal from Augustus? This also I deny. Rather, from the Senate to...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on imperial rights and public law, specifically moving from the right of appeal to the management of war spoils and the necessity of state taxation.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>Disputation I.</header>

Many passages in Tacitus and other authors testify that an appeal could be made to the Emperor.

### LXXXIX.

<margin>To whom the rights of spoils belong.</margin>
*The rights to spoils belong to the Emperor.* Just as Aemilius <note>referring to the Roman general Aemilius Paullus</note> rightly said: the decision regarding spoils belongs to the Commanders, not the soldiers. The Romans acted wisely in this: those things which were not brought into the public treasury <term>fiscus: the private or imperial treasury</term> were divided among the citizens, so that provision was made for those who, by the consul's order, had remained in the forum to support the needs of the war. In our military service, as with many other things, these rights have fallen into disuse. Hence it happens that, just as Tacitus says of our ancestors, soldiers pursue spoils while neglecting the enemy. This disease is far too hereditary among our people; the most recent example was in the Battle of Eger <note>original: *prælio Agriensi*; referring to the 1596 Battle of Mezőkeresztes near Eger/Agria, where imperial troops lost a victory because they stopped to plunder the Ottoman camp</note>.

### XC.

<margin>Taxes are the sinews of the Empire.</margin>
Ulpian <note>a prominent Roman jurist</note> rightly calls taxes and revenues the sinews of the empire. Therefore, they cannot be abolished or transferred to others. When Nero wished to abolish all taxes and give what he thought was a "most beautiful gift" to the human race, the Roman Senate taught him better, saying *that the dissolution of the empire would follow if the revenues by which the State is sustained were diminished.* <note>original: "dissolutionem imperii futuram, si fructus, quibus Respublica sustinetur, deminuantur"</note>

### XCI.

Furthermore, it is in the interest of the empire's splendor that the revenues pledged as collateral by Charles IV <note>Holy Roman Emperor, 1346–1378, known for the Golden Bull and for mortgaging imperial assets</note> return to the emperor. But is this truly in the interest of our current state? I do not know. This was, however, affirmed by Julius Pflug, Bishop of Naumburg <note>Naumburgensis; Pflug was a key 16th-century diplomat and theologian</note>, the uncle of Christopher Pflug—a youth born for greatness and for the glory of Germany.

### XCII.

<margin>The penalty for high treason.</margin>
Just as the punishment for treason and high treason <term>perduellio: specifically high treason against the state or the ruler</term> was the bulwark of the Roman Principate,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on imperial rights and public law, transitioning from fiscal matters to the legal protections of authority, including the Pope's power of excommunication and the Roman law of treason (majestas).</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>
<sig>B 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: seditious [tiosos]</meta>

<margin>*The Principate; Excommunication is the bulwark of the Papacy.*</margin>
Furthermore, the Pope's excommunication—which Guicciardini <note>Francesco Guicciardini, a famous Italian historian and statesman</note> calls "sacred or celestial arms," and Lambert of Hersfeld <note>original: *Scafneburgensi*; an 11th-century German chronicler</note> calls the "rod of Apostolic authority"—is the shield of the Papacy. For this reason, the same Guicciardini not undeservedly calls them "Princes of nations" rather than "priests of sacred matters."

### XCIII.

It is shameful, however—indeed, it is seen as not in the Prince's best interest—for the law of Treason <term>crimen laesae maiestatis: the crime of "injured majesty" or high treason</term> to be reduced to every minor and ridiculous matter, although Pompeius Macer <note>a Roman official under Augustus and Tiberius</note> felt otherwise. Yet Alexander <note>the Great</note> was not ignorant of this either, of whom Curtius <note>Quintus Curtius Rufus, historian of Alexander</note> writes: "The King, having examined the case, pronounced that one crime had been overlooked by the accusers, and it was the greatest: the despair of his own safety." (This is what he calls "injured majesty"). "For those who had either hoped for his safe return from India or believed he would return would never have dared such things." Tacitus says it aptly: *The crime of treason was the complement <note>meaning the "sum total" or "catch-all"</note> of all accusations.*

### XCIV.

Moreover, according to Tacitus, it was Augustus, not Sulla, who first applied the law of treason to defamatory pamphlets <note>original: *famosos libellos*; slanderous writings intended to damage a reputation</note>. However, Cicero seems to suggest otherwise in Book 3, *Letter 11*. These two passages were first compared by the noble and most learned jurist Scipio Gentilis <note>a famous legal scholar and professor at the University of Altdorf</note>, the "Sun of our Academy."

### XCV.

<margin>*Factions, popularity, and flattery: how they are harmful.*</margin>
Just as factions are the most ruinous thing in an Aristocratic state, and seeking popularity in a Democracy; so too is flattery in a monarchy. Truly, however, in some cases flattery toward a Prince is seen as worthy of all praise. Examples of this are found in the Roman Senate toward Drusus Nero <note>likely referring to the son of Tiberius</note> and others. Otho <note>a short-lived Roman Emperor during the year 69 AD</note> used this same flattery correctly toward seditious soldiers...

<summary>This page explores the legal "shields" of rulers, from the Pope's use of excommunication to the Roman emperors' application of treason laws (majestas) and the dangers of flattery in a monarchy.</summary>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>Dispute I.</header>

...seditious people, and Cerialis <note>referring to Quintus Petillius Cerialis, a Roman general</note> toward the Treviri, and Agricola toward the Britons. Tacitus shrewdly remarked that "among the inexperienced it was called culture, when it was actually a part of their servitude." <note>original: *apud imperitos humanitas vocabatur, cum pars servitutis esset*; from Tacitus’s *Agricola*, 21, describing how Romans used luxury to pacify subjects</note>

---

->XCVI.<-

<margin>Lest two heirs weigh down the Republic.</margin>
It was not without reason that, according to Tacitus, the Roman people feared lest two heirs—Agrippa and Tiberius—might weigh down the Republic and eventually tear it apart. Therefore, among the ancient Germans, it was wisely arranged that a single person would take over the estate rights, or even the rights of the principality, while the others were ordered to depart with a small sum of money <note>original: *pecuniola*; a modest payout to younger sons to prevent the division of lands</note>. A writer from Fulda <note>likely referring to the monk and historian Rudolf of Fulda</note> testifies that the same practice was customary among the Princes of Flanders. He says, *"Lest the province, divided among many, should see its luster fade through the poverty of the family estate."* And today, many Princes in Germany, Italy, and France observe this. I believe this is done with great luster and benefit both to the state and to the family itself.

---

->XCVII.<-

After the Great Cosimo de' Medici <note>Cosimo I de' Medici (1519–1574), the first Grand Duke of Tuscany</note> had founded the Order of Saint Stephen <note>original: *Episcopatum S. Stephani*; while this literally means "Bishopric," the context refers to the dynastic Knightly Order he established</note>, he provided by law that its administration should remain with the Dukes of Florence. Should they fail, it would pass to those who used the title of Grand Duke of Tuscany <note>original: *Hetruriæ*</note>. From this arose those pacts, or treaties of mutual succession, which we believe were beneficially introduced into Italy and Germany—regardless of whatever that curious "hunter of secrets" <term>arcanarum rerum auceps: a derogatory term for a nosy political theorist or spy</term> might say to the contrary.

---

->XCVIII.<-

<margin>To what extent the Prince is exempt from laws.</margin>
It is clear from Tacitus, Cassius Dio, and Ulpian that the Prince is exempt <note>original: *solutum*; literally "unbound" or "loosed"</note> from all written laws. Concerning this law, the learned Doctors of Law work themselves into a lather, or as the saying goes, "they look for a storm in a ladle." <note>original: *in simpulo fluctum quærunt*; a Latin idiom similar to "a tempest in a teapot"</note> The same Prin— <meta>catchword: cip (Princeps)</meta>

<summary>This page argues for the benefits of primogeniture and indivisible inheritance to maintain state power, using examples from Roman history, ancient Germany, and the Medici dynasty. It also introduces the legal debate over whether a sovereign Prince is bound by written laws.</summary>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

...the Prince <note>original: *Princeps*; the Roman emperor or supreme ruler</note> can release others from specific laws. For example, Tiberius released his own children from the laws governing the minimum age for office <note>original: *annariis*</note>, and Augustus released his wife from the Voconian law <note>original: *lege Voconia*; a law that limited the ability of women to inherit large estates</note>, and so on.

### XCIX.

<margin>The empire should be enclosed by borders.</margin>
Augustus acted wisely when he enclosed that vast empire within its own borders and restrained it. I hold the same opinion of Tiberius, who was rightly unconcerned with expanding the empire. But can the same be said of Hadrian? I do not say so for certain. However, I believe Claudius acted rightly and in accordance with the dignity of the Republic when he added Britain to the empire. Augustus declined to expand not out of laziness, nor out of envy—as some have thought—but out of fear, which is to say, out of prudence. Indeed, I find the word "fear" <note>original: *metu*</note> used in this sense more than once in the writings of Tacitus.

### C.

Similarly, those who set their minds on occupying the most remote regions waste their time and effort <note>original: *operam & oleum perdunt*; literally "lose their work and their oil," an idiom for wasting labor and resources like a lamp burning late at night</note>. There is a beautiful speech by the Venetians in Guicciardini <note>Francesco Guicciardini (1483–1540), an Italian historian and statesman</note>, book 3: *“The city of Pisa is indeed a favorable opportunity for the Venetians, but because they cannot reach it except through the territory and ports of others, it can only be preserved with difficulty and not without great expense against the harassments of the Florentines.”*

### CI.

<margin>Whether wives should follow their husbands to war.</margin>
There was a great disagreement between Caecina and Messallinus <note>referring to a debate in the Roman Senate recorded by Tacitus between Aulus Caecina Severus and Marcus Valerius Messallinus</note> as to whether wives should follow their husbands into war. We side with Caecina, even though his opinion was mocked in the Roman Senate. Today, there are almost more camp-followers <note>original: *focariæ*; literally "hearth-women" or concubines</note> and servants <note>original: *Calones*; soldiers' servants</note> than there are actual soldiers. As Tacitus said of the army of Vitellius, it was *"more an army in name than in strength."*

### CII.

In the Roman army, one could easily judge the situation from the soldiers' shout, since in that place...

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>Disputation I.</header>

...how military affairs stood, as mentioned everywhere in the writings of Livy and Tacitus. Those who today condemn this custom among the Turks as if it were barbarian are, in truth, ignorant of the utility of this practice, which was of the greatest importance among the Romans. <note>The author is likely referring to the Roman practice of building fortified camps every night, a discipline the Ottoman Turks also maintained.</note>

### CIII.

<margin>Whether Roman fortifications are rightly disparaged.</margin>
Many today disparage the use of fortifications <note>original: *propugnacula*</note> in Roman military science. This is absurd. Certainly, the Romans themselves placed much hope and strength in them. Tacitus writes of the city of Cremona that it was a bastion <note>original: *propugnaculum*</note> against the Gauls living across the Po river, and against any other force that might rush through the Alps. Most absurd of all are those who deny that such defenses have any use in this present time. Frederick, Duke of Swabia <note>original: *Fridericus Dux Alemanniæ*; likely referring to a medieval German leader noted for his strategic building</note>, thought differently and rightly placed many castles along the Rhine; hence it became a proverb: *“Frederick always drags a castle along in his horse's tail.”* <note>A metaphor for a general who secures every step of his progress with new fortifications.</note>

### CIV.

Therefore, I consider what Ammirato <note>Scipione Ammirato (1531–1601), an Italian historian who wrote commentaries on Tacitus</note> thinks regarding Augustus: that when he received news of the Varian disaster <note>original: *clade Vari*; the catastrophic defeat of three Roman legions in the Teutoburg Forest in 9 AD</note>, his army had been so miserably destroyed only because of a lack of fortifications. Suleiman <note>Suleiman the Magnificent, Sultan of the Ottoman Empire</note> felt the same fear regarding the Tartars, whom he knew could raid as far as Constantinople without any hindrance. We must fear the same result if the Turk should occupy Vienna—which God forbid. Regarding this matter, the great Jan Zamoyski <note>Jan Zamoyski (1542–1605), a Polish nobleman and military commander</note>, the glory and protector of the Polish nation, responded bravely to Sinan Pasha <note>An Ottoman grand vizier and general</note>.

### CV.

<margin>The battle line of the Romans.</margin>
It is said that Julius Sacrovir, a leader of the Gauls, drew up his battle line imprudently when he placed the heavily armored gladiators <note>original: *Crupellarios*; specialized gladiators clad in full plate armor</note> in the front, the infantry cohorts in the middle, and the half-armed troops in the rear. The Romans, who were more skilled in war than all other nations, acted far differently. Therefore, in the comparison of Alexander with the lead-

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

For the Roman generals in the 9th book of Livy <note>Titus Livius (64 BC–12 AD), the great Roman historian who chronicled the rise of Rome.</note>, there was a danger that someone might be more skillful than any of the Roman camp in choosing a location, (note here the heads of *military science* <note>original: *scientiæ militaris*</note>), arranging for supplies, guarding against ambushes, choosing the time of battle, drawing up the line of battle, and strengthening it with reserves. I disagree: rather, it is clear to me from history that in all these matters the Romans were the most prepared of all.

### CVI.

Two men were destined for power under the rule of *Tiberius*: *Germanicus* and *Drusus*. While *Germanicus* was still safe, *Tiberius* held a balanced judgment between the two; he did this so that he would not cast aside his own authority by strengthening one, or stir up the passions of the common people against himself by preferring the other. Therefore, it was only after *Germanicus* died that he promoted *Drusus* to the highest affairs. In this matter, the Emperor *Galba* erred shamefully. *Agrippina* acted wisely when, once *Nero* was destined for the throne, she stripped *Britannicus* of all honor and power. But as in other public business, so too here the saying of *Tacitus* <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a historian known for his cynical analysis of power.</note> holds true: *“It is not as easy to judge what would have been best to do, as it is to judge that what was actually done was the worst.”* <note>original: *Non perinde dijudicari posse quid optimum factu fuerit, quam pessimum fuisse quod factum est.*</note>

### CVII.

<margin>Whether a fleeing enemy should be pursued.</margin>
*Dolabella* was right that a fleeing and wandering enemy should not be pursued with a heavy or single-pronged attack. *Caesar* was also right to order that an enemy who yields should be followed more moderately. Because of ignorance of this principle, *German* soldiers have often suffered massive slaughter by the *Turks* <note>Referring to the Ottoman Empire’s forces, a major military concern for Europe at the time of this writing.</note>, as *Bonfinius* <note>Antonio Bonfini (1427–1502), an Italian scholar and historian to the Hungarian court.</note> shows with many examples.

### CVIII.

<margin>Whether informers should be tolerated in a Republic.</margin>
Informers <note>original: *delatores*; state-sponsored snitches who accused citizens of crimes to seize their property.</note> or emissaries are not to be tolerated in a good Republic. It is the voice of a tyrant, not a Prince, to say: “They would rather subvert good laws than remove those who guard them.” In—

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>Discussion I.</header>

Those who openly praise a man but harass him in his absence are unworthy of human society; as Tacitus says, they "reproach by making excuses." <note>original: "excusando exprobrant"</note> The same author remarks, *"The worst kind of enemies are those who praise."* <note>original: "Pessimum inimicorum genus laudantes"</note>

->CIX.<-

Tiberius used to assign honors by looking laudably at the nobility of ancestors, fame in military service, and distinguished mental arts. Today, the Turks <note>The author refers to the Ottoman Empire, which contemporary European scholars often noted for promoting officials based on merit and talent rather than noble birth.</note> still maintain this method. Among us, the practice regarding Vitellius is in use: "it is enough to be the son of a thrice-consul." <note>A reference to Aulus Vitellius, who became Emperor more due to his father’s political legacy than his own merit.</note> In this passage, the sharpest censor of books—otherwise a keen critic—is far too fussy in his editing.

->CX.<-

The arguments brought against the Cincian Law <note>The *Lex Cincia* (204 BC) was a Roman law that prohibited advocates from taking fees or gifts from their clients to prevent the exploitation of the poor by the powerful.</note> by those "purse-milkers" <note>original: *crumenimulgis*</note> and "chatterers" <note>original: *tintinabulis*, literally "little bells."</note> are merely customary <note>original Greek: *νόμιμα* (nomima)</note> and carry no weight.

->CXI.<-

<margin>
Whether the conquered should be admitted to the rights of the state.
</margin>
The Aedui <note>A Gallic tribe allied with Rome.</note> petitioned the Romans for the right to become Senators. Many in the Roman Senate opposed this. However, the Emperor Claudius granted it to the Aedui. One must ask: which path was more beneficial for the Republic? We believe Claudius acted rightly and for the sake of Roman greatness. But was it right to admit the conquered to the rights of the state simply because it was profitable? Certainly, if you wish the Republic to grow. As Claudius said: *"What else brought destruction to the Spartans or the Athenians, though they were powerful in arms, except that they kept the conquered away as foreigners?"*

->CXII.<-

Military discipline cannot be established with enough severity to match the ferocity of the soldiers. Our author <note>Tacitus.</note> speaks correctly of Corbulo: *"You should know him to be intent and inexorable toward great crimes, a man whose severity was believed to be just as great even toward slight ones."* <note>Gnaeus Domitius Corbulo (7–67 AD) was a Roman general famous for his strict, old-fashioned discipline.</note> Furthermore, those who deny that such discipline can be restored are mistaken. If only there were more men like Manlius or Papirius; more like Avidius or Cassius. <note>These names refer to legendary Roman disciplinarians: Manlius Torquatus, who executed his own son for disobeying orders, and Papirius Cursor, known for his relentless rigors.</note>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

### CXIII.

<margin>The variety of punishments in the Roman Republic.</margin>
Many believe that the variety of punishments according to a person's social status was practiced in the Roman Republic, but this is a mistake. Examples can be found in the cases of Horatius, the Manlii, Marcus and Titus, and others. The Romans, as Livy writes, beautifully called it "not granting the right of favor." <note>original: "jus gratiæ non dare"</note> Hence Tacitus says: *"Once hatred and favoritism ceased, the law prevailed."* <note>original: "ut odium & gratia desiere, jus valuit"</note> The opposite occurred in the case of Virgulania <note>Virgulania was a woman of high status protected by Livia, the mother of Tiberius, showing how personal influence could bypass the law.</note> in the writings of Tacitus, which went against the custom and severity of Tiberius. Clearly, Justinian was not the only one who indulged the power of women.

### CXIV.

Galba acted foolishly—and did not follow the example of Augustus, Tiberius, Claudius, and Nero—when he denied a bonus <note>term: *donativum* — a large cash gift given by Roman emperors to their soldiers, often upon their accession or the naming of an heir, to ensure loyalty.</note> to the soldiers at the same time he designated an heir to the empire. It was well known that their spirits could have been won over by even the smallest bit of generosity from the stingy old man. It was much more foolish of him to think of a distribution <note>term: *congiarium* — originally a gift of oil or wine, it came to mean a cash handout to the Roman populace.</note> only once the soldiers were already enraged. Remedies come too late when a wound to the throat has already led to the final breath. It is too late to brace the walls when the house is already collapsing during a banquet.

### CXV.

<margin>Other Emperors whose severity was ill-timed.</margin>
In this regard, Galba—as well as Cato, Pompey, Pertinax, and others—were severe at the wrong time. It was done with good intentions, to be sure; who could deny that? But while they were too stubborn in maintaining their integrity, they brought much suffering upon themselves and the Republic. Our writer <note>The "writer" refers to the historian Tacitus.</note> says of Galba: "A saying of Galba’s was added—noble for the Republic, but dangerous for himself—that he chose his soldiers, he did not buy them." <note>original: "legi è se militem non emi"</note> This was the saying that destroyed him, just as the word "Let us serve" <note>original: "militemus"</note> destroyed Pertinax. Thus Tacitus writes: *"For the rest of the state was not up to this standard."* And in the same work: *"He was harmed by ancient rigor and excessive severity, to which we are no longer equal."* Similarly, Cicero imprudently demanded much of Antony at a shameful time.

<sig>B 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: non</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of impartial justice in the Roman Republic and criticizes Emperor Galba for his ill-timed stinginess and rigid adherence to "old-fashioned" virtues that led to his downfall.</summary>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Discussion I.</header>

...just like that "untimely" man <note>original: ἄκαιρος (*akairos*)</note> in the *Characters* of Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC) was a Greek philosopher; his work *Characters* describes various personality types, including the "Unseasonable Man" who does things at the most awkward moments.</note> who, having been invited to a wedding, raved against the female sex while among the women. All political writers say with one voice: "those who act must always look to what is appropriate for the moment." <note>original: "δεῖ αὐτοὺς ἀεὶ τοὺς πράττοντας τὰ πρὸς τὸν καιρὸν σκοπεῖν"</note>

### CXVI.

<margin>
Whether Nero’s parricide can be excused.
</margin>
Everyone rightly detests Nero’s parricide <note>The murder of his mother, Agrippina the Younger, in 59 AD.</note>. We shall see, if Tacitus is to be believed, whether Nero can be excused in some part. This is because Agrippina had set traps for the Roman Prince <note>term: *Princeps* — the title used by Roman emperors to mean "First Citizen."</note> and had incurred the crime of high treason <note>term: *laesae majestatis* — "injured majesty," a legal term for crimes against the state or the emperor's person.</note>. A similar example happened in the kingdom of France in a previous age. I feel the same about Timoleon, who saw to it that his brother, who had become a tyrant, was killed. (Plutarch). <note>Timoleon was a Greek statesman who famously chose the safety of the city over his own family by allowing the execution of his brother, Timophanes.</note>

### CXVII.

Only the Prince held the symbols of power <note>original: *insignia imperii*</note>: he alone, through the power of the Tribune’s veto <note>term: *intercessio* — the right of a tribune (and later the emperor) to halt a legal proceeding or execution.</note>, could rescue from death one condemned by the Senate; he alone could restore someone to the legal rights of their social rank. However, the condemnation of Antistius <note>Antistius Sosianus, a Roman praetor who was exiled for writing satirical verses about Nero.</note> cannot be referred to this, since he was neither condemned by the Senate, nor exempt from death by Nero’s intercession.

### CXVIII.

<margin>
How to deal with a rioting populace.
</margin>
It is better to grant things voluntarily to a rioting and fierce people—even those things they demand against good morals—than to call the State into danger. For later, once the people have been calmed, these decisions can be reconsidered. Tacitus says: *"The things that they had extorted through sedition were changed."* <note>original: "Mutata... quæ per seditionem expresserant"</note> And elsewhere: *"the ill-advised decisions of the last sedition were abolished for the future."* <note>original: "proximæ seditionis male consulta abolita in posterum"</note> In this matter, men who are otherwise prudent in the State often err.

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

### CXIX.

The sanctity of the public good is so great that even the Prince himself must yield to it. Just as Otho chose death rather than permit so many of the Roman youth and so many distinguished armies to be struck down again and snatched from the republic. <note>Emperor Otho (r. 69 AD) committed suicide after a military defeat to end a civil war and prevent further bloodshed among Roman citizens.</note> This is clearly in accord with that saying of Caiaphas: *It is expedient that one man should die for the people.* <note>John 18:14; Caiaphas was the Jewish High Priest who argued for the execution of Jesus to maintain political stability.</note>

<margin>
The election of the Prince.
</margin>

### CXX.

Galba to Piso: *“Under Tiberius, Caligula, and Claudius, we were like the inheritance of a single family; it will serve as a substitute for liberty that we have begun to be chosen.”* <note>Galba was the first emperor of the "Year of the Four Emperors" (68–69 AD). He adopted Piso as his heir to establish a merit-based succession rather than a hereditary one.</note> He rightly says "as a substitute for liberty." For it cannot be shown from this passage, nor from others, that the same liberty remained for the Romans—even though the legal experts <note>term: *ICtus* — an abbreviation for *Iurisconsultus*, a professional legal scholar or jurist whose opinions helped shape Roman law.</note> believe this to be the height of liberty.

### CXXI.

The same Galba said no less well: *“To be fathered by and born to Princes is a matter of chance, and is valued no higher; but in adoption, judgment is unimpaired, and if you wish to choose, the consensus of all points the way.”* These words were remarkable both for that time and for the nature of the people who, according to the same witness, could neither endure total slavery nor total liberty. <note>Tacitus, *Histories* 1.16. This reflects the Roman struggle to balance the need for a strong leader with their traditional hatred of kingship.</note>

### CXXII.

There is that golden saying of Tacitus: *“Every great example of punishment has some element of injustice in it, which is weighed against the public utility in the face of individuals.”* <note>Tacitus, *Annals* 14.44.</note> Relying on this saying, we approve of the decimation of soldiers <note>term: *decimatio* — a brutal military discipline where every tenth man in a disgraced unit was executed by his comrades.</note>, and we judge it right that six hundred slaves were put to death by the magistrate following the murder of a single master according to Roman law. <note>Referring to the execution of the household slaves of Pedanius Secundus in 61 AD; Roman law dictated that if a master was murdered by a slave, all slaves in the house were executed to deter such acts.</note>

<margin>
On Tyranny.
</margin>

### CXXIII.

Livy, Book 1: "Here," he says, "Lucius Tarquinius, the first of the Kings [to break] the custom handed down by the Elders..." <note>Livy 1.49; This refers to Tarquinius Superbus, the last king of Rome, who began to rule by fear and without consulting the Senate.</note>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>Disputation I.</header>

<margin>
Points regarding Lucius Tarquinius.
</margin>
...he freed himself from the custom of consulting the Senate on all matters, and administered the Republic through domestic <note>original: *domesticis* — meaning private or household-based rather than public</note> counsels. By himself, he made and broke war, peace, treaties, and alliances with whomever he wished, without the order of the people or the Senate. These are, for the most part, not so much marks of sovereignty as they are points <note>original: *capita*</note> of Tyranny. The account by Tacitus regarding the beginning of Nero's reign is clearly different and diverse: *Nothing in his household was for sale, or open to ambition; he kept his home and the Republic separate; the Senate and the [ancient offices] should retain their traditional functions...* <note>original: *Nihil in panatibus [penatibus] suis venale...* — Tacitus, *Annals* 13.4. Nero initially promised to separate his private affairs from state governance to avoid the corruption seen under his predecessor, Claudius.</note>

->CXXIV.<-

It was for more than one reason that Corbulo requested a commander of his own for the defense of Armenia. <note>Gnaeus Domitius Corbulo was a prominent Roman general under Nero tasked with settling the Armenian succession.</note> In our own republic, offices are mixed and duplicated to the greatest detriment of the state. How well that saying about Metiochus in Plutarch fits our situation today: *Metiochus manages the bread-basket, Metiochus handles the flour, [Metiochus oversees the roads, Metiochus checks the weights].* <note>Metiochus was a contemporary of Pericles; Plutarch uses this poem to mock officials who insist on overseeing every minor detail themselves rather than delegating tasks.</note>

->CXXV.<-

<margin>
Whether Ostracism is rightly criticized.
</margin>
Ostracism, <note>A political practice in ancient Greece where a citizen could be expelled from the city for ten years to prevent tyranny.</note> which was used not only by the Greeks but also by the Romans, is unfairly criticized <note>original: *vapulat* — literally "is flogged"</note> by some theologians and philosophers. Certainly, by this means, men—however great they might be—were looking out for themselves and for the crowds because their excessive power made them suspected; they withdrew themselves from those who were wickedly defaming them, just as Nero wrote to Plautus. <note>Rubellius Plautus was a rival to Nero; the emperor "advised" him to retire to his estates in Asia to avoid political unrest.</note> Scipio Africanus did the same, and gladly departed from the city. It was also a noble practice that some were sent away under the guise of an embassy or some other honor.

->CXXVI.<-

<margin>
Regarding Corbulo destroying the city of Artaxata in Armenia.
</margin>
Corbulo destroyed the city of Artaxata in Armenia and leveled it to the ground. Because, as Tacitus says, *the city could neither be held without a strong garrison due to the great extent of its walls, nor did we have the strength of numbers to both maintain a garrison and carry on the war.* <note>Tacitus, *Annals* 13.41. Artaxata was the capital of Armenia.</note> They did the same at the stronghold of Mon-teli... <note>The text is cut off at the binding.</note>

<meta>catchword: teli</meta>

<summary>This page contrasts the tyrannical, centralized rule of Tarquin with the early, more balanced promises of Nero, while also defending the practice of ostracism as a safeguard for both the state and the individual. It concludes with the strategic military justifications used by General Corbulo for destroying captured Armenian fortifications.</summary>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

<margin>Whether the deed reflects cruelty.</margin>
"Happy Germans," as Guicciardini testifies <note>original: *felices Germani* — The OCR "telices" is a typo for "felices." Francesco Guicciardini was a famous Italian historian and statesman.</note>. Nor does this reflect cruelty. Fulvius, however, acted differently: though Capua was very powerful, nearby, and hostile—leading some to think it should be destroyed—he nevertheless preserved it. Others have provided for themselves and the conquered in various ways, and perhaps even more laudably. <note>original: *Fulejus* — Likely referring to Quintus Fulvius Flaccus, the Roman general who captured Capua during the Second Punic War but resisted calls for its total destruction.</note>

<margin>Whether the Romans considered age when granting honors.</margin>
### C X X V I I.

It is false what wise men claim—relying on passages from both Livy and Tacitus—that among the Romans there was no consideration of age when granting honors. From this, great men ask whether it is expedient to appoint a young man as a commander in war, as is read concerning Alexander, Scipio, Corvinus, Pompey, Octavian, and others. Indeed, [choosing a youth] is much more correct than the Cardinals of old, who elected a Pope at 100 years of age; having been placed upon a mule, he expired three days later from that unaccustomed riding and the burdensome nature of the office.

<margin>Whether a successor should be designated early.</margin>
### C X X V I I I.

Galba designated Piso as his successor too late. Augustus, Tiberius, and others were more prudent in this matter. Thus, Henry III commanded the princes of the realm to swear an oath and loyalty to his son Henry while he was still a "catechumen" <note>original: *catechumeno* — used here to mean a child or someone still in the early stages of training for his role.</note>. In this way, as Tacitus says of Tiberius, they believed the wicked hopes of others were kept in check.

### C X X I X.

Are they <term>traitors<gloss>original: *perduelles* — those who commit high treason or take up arms against the state</gloss></term> who take up arms for the sake of liberty? Against this view there exists a most serious speech of the Saxons to Henry III <note>While the text names Henry III, this likely refers to the Saxon rebellion against his son, Henry IV, famously chronicled by Lambert of Hersfeld.</note> in the works of Schafnaburgensis <note>original: *Scafneburgensem* — referring to the chronicler Lambert of Aschaffenburg.</note>. But nevertheless, I do not believe it is permitted for Christians. Truly, Tacitus says: *Liberty and fair-sounding names are used as a pretext; nor has anyone ever desired the subjection of others and dominion for themselves without using these same words.*

<meta>catchword: Whether (An)</meta>

<summary>This page explores three key political themes: the strategic mercy shown to conquered cities, the preference for energetic young leaders over elderly ones, and the illegality of armed rebellion even when justified by the name of 'liberty.'</summary>
<keywords>Guicciardini, Livy, Tacitus, leadership age, papal election, Henry III, succession, rebellion, liberty, treason</keywords>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Disputation I.</header>

->CXXX.<-

Whether it is indeed lawful for a Prince to force his subjects to religion by arms or war? I do not think so as a general rule, especially since most people "slip into ambition under the appearance of religion," to use the words of Tacitus <note>original: *relligionis specie in ambitionem delabantur* — A critique of how political leaders often use religious piety as a cover for their own desire for power.</note>. Much less do I accept here that maxim of Papinian: *The highest principle is that which acts on behalf of religion.* <note>original: *Summa ratio est quæ pro relligione facit* — Papinian was a celebrated Roman jurist; this maxim suggests that religious interests should override common legal rules, a point the author here contests in the context of war.</note>

<margin>The body of the Commonwealth ought to be one.</margin>
->CXXXI.<-

Asinius Gallus <note>A Roman senator known for his friction with the Emperor Tiberius.</note> spoke rightly in the works of Tacitus—though perhaps not from his true conviction—when he said that the body of the Commonwealth is one and must be ruled by a single mind. Emperor Constantius expressed the same sentiment in the writings of Marcellinus <note>Ammianus Marcellinus, a 4th-century historian; the quote refers to Constantius II.</note>: that the Republic can neither be divided nor ought to be, but that everyone must bring help to it with all their strength when it is wavering. The modern variety of empires in Europe is the cause of so many rebellions and wars. This was the prophecy of Cerealis in Tacitus: *If the Romans are driven out—which may the gods forbid—what else will follow but the wars of all nations among themselves?* <note>original: *Pulsis... Romanis, quid aliud quam bella omnium inter se gentium existent* — Petillius Cerialis was a Roman general; he argued that Roman rule, despite its faults, prevented constant tribal warfare.</note>

<margin>To what the Porcian Law concerning the life of a Roman citizen should be attributed.</margin>
->CXXXII.<-

The Porcian Law <note>The *Lex Porcia* (c. 199–184 BCE) was a landmark of Roman liberty that protected citizens from being scourged or executed without the right to appeal to the people.</note> concerning the life of a Roman citizen is not so much to be attributed to the mere "images of liberty," but rather to the very <term>right of sovereignty</term> <note>original: *jus imperii* — The supreme legal authority or power held by the state.</note>, despite what others seem to feel who rely on the 5th book of Aristotle’s *Politics*, chapter 9.

<margin>Loyalty must be kept to the Prince.</margin>
->CXXXIII.<-

Sabinus, the brother of the Emperor Vespasian, when he was besieged on the Capitoline Hill, sent word to the Emperor Vitellius with commands and a complaint that their agreements were being disturbed—though perhaps in vain, according to the opinion of that master of supreme domination <note>Referring to the volatile power struggle during the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 CE).</note>. It was different for Numa, who, in the infancy of the empire, decreed that faith and oaths should rule the Roman city once the fear of punishment was removed. And so, when we say that someone has acted treacherously... <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword is "So."</note>

# On Public Law. 41

### CXXXIV.

Thus, against the law of nations—or as Tacitus calls it, "human law"—Mark Antony enticed the King of the Armenians <note>original: *Regem Armericorum* — referring to Artavasdes II, who was captured by Antony in 34 BCE.</note> under the appearance of friendship, and Tiberius drove the King of the Cappadocians <note>King Archelaus of Cappadocia, who died in Rome after being summoned by Tiberius.</note> to his death after summoning him by trickery. However, I cannot accept this charge against Caesar, who wished to kill Commius <note>A leader of the Atrebates tribe who was once an ally but became an enemy of Caesar.</note> under the pretense of a parley. In other respects, Caesar observed the rights of parley most sacredly.

### CXXXV.

A certain comparison can be made between the Roman military system and our own. Although even in the Roman service, as Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian known for his commentary on Virgil’s *Aeneid*.</note> testifies in his commentary on the second book of the *Aeneid*, the variety of leaders over time always changed the discipline of the military.

<margin>The source of the longevity of the German Empire.</margin>

### CXXXVI.

Truly, that "Etruscan" <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary scholar writing under a pseudonym or a specific Italian legal writer; the "little book" refers to a treatise on the German constitution.</note> says in his little book on the state of Germany that the fourfold order in the empire is the cause of the tranquility and longevity of the German Empire. Regarding the Roman Republic, our author <note>Tacitus, *Histories* 4.74.</note> writes: *This fabric has grown together through the fortune and discipline of eight hundred years, and it cannot be torn apart without the destruction of those who tear it.* Let those who think otherwise see what a great fire they may be lighting.

<margin>How the wages of the soldiers sufficed under Tiberius.</margin>

### CXXXVII.

The wages of the soldiers under Tiberius were ten <term>asses</term> <note>The *as* was a bronze Roman coin; ten *asses* was a meager daily wage for a soldier.</note> a day. It is a wonder to many how Roman soldiers could sustain life on so little. For from this, says Tacitus, they had to pay for their clothing, weapons, and tents, as well as buy their way out of the cruelty of centurions and purchase exemptions from duties. Savile <note>Sir Henry Savile (1549–1622), an English scholar and translator of Tacitus, famous for his detailed historical commentaries.</note> solves this knot eruditely and truly, I believe: When those things that exist only by law <note>original: *τὰ νόμῳ μόνον ὄντα* — referring to money and artificial currency.</note> abound copiously, then those things that exist by nature <note>original: *τὰ φύσει ὄντα* — referring to essential goods like food and grain.</note>, which nature requires, are also necessarily drawn to a higher price in the market. By which argument...

# Disputation I.

The same author rightly defends the common reading in Polybius <note>A Greek historian of the Hellenistic period known for his work *The Histories*.</note> regarding the remarkable fertility of Cisalpine Gaul <note>The region of Northern Italy between the Alps and the Apennines.</note> against a most learned Jurisconsult <note>original: *ICtum* — an abbreviation for *Iurisconsultum*, a legal expert.</note>.

### CXXXVIII.

<margin>Whether a Prince should be moved by rumors.</margin>
Tacitus rightly says that it is the lot of Princes that their principal actions must be directed toward their reputation <note>original: *fama*.</note>. However, those Princes who are moved by the rumors of the common people must be "mushrooms" <note>original: *fungos* — a Latin metaphor for someone senseless or soft-headed.</note> and empty-headed. Tacitus says otherwise of Tiberius: "Tiberius," he says, "was free [of worry] by despising rumors." Likewise, time must be allowed for rumors to grow old <note>And thus fade away naturally.</note>. Vitellius foolishly suppressed rumors by an edict: "That," says Tacitus, "was food for their fame" <note>Tacitus, *Annals* — meaning the act of suppression only gave the rumors more weight and longevity.</note>.

### CXXXIX.

Cicero truly said that money is the instrument of war; and Mucianus <note>Gaius Licinius Mucianus, a general and advisor to the Emperor Vespasian.</note> in Tacitus truly said that money is the sinews of civil war, however much that sharp writer from Sulla’s colony <note>A reference to Niccolò Machiavelli, who famously argued in his *Discourses* that good soldiers, not gold, are the sinews of war. Florence, Machiavelli's home, was a colony originally settled by Sulla's veterans.</note> might struggle against it.

### CXL.

Although the artillery <note>original: *Tormenta* — ancient siege engines that used tension or torsion, such as catapults.</note> and war machines of the Romans differ greatly from those of today, examples will confirm that they had nearly the same power; therefore, modern warfare does not eliminate the distinction of courage any more than it did in the past, even though many believe it does—not soldiers, but "Murci" <note>original: *Murci* — an ancient term for cowards or shirkers, derived from those who allegedly cut off their thumbs to avoid military service.</note>, to use the old word.

### CXLI.

On the contrary, our modern windings, or slants, and the projections of earthworks and ramparts were known to the ancients. Although the "new geniuses," as they are called, deny this today, the ancient manner is described in Tacitus. Consider for me even the city of Jerusalem alone in the same Tacitus <note>Tacitus provides a detailed description of the sophisticated zigzagging walls and fortifications of Jerusalem in Book 5 of his *Histories*.</note>.

### CXLII.

<margin>Against all associates...</margin>
Against each and every associate of a conspiracy not

<meta>This page continues the author's collection of political maxims and historical observations. While the previous page discussed the influence of rumors and the financial "sinews of war," this page shifts toward the legal and ethical management of domestic threats, specifically how a ruler should handle conspiracies, seditions, and the possibility of a subject's repentance.</meta>

# On Public Law.

<margin>Whether a conspiracy should be punished.</margin>
[One] should not anxiously investigate, much less punish [a conspiracy], although those bloodsuckers <note>original: *sanguinisugæ* — a derogatory term for professional informers (delators) who profited from the execution of those they accused.</note> feel otherwise. Tacitus speaks excellently regarding the Emperor Tiberius: "Although many from the Prince's household, as well as Knights and Senators, were said to have sustained [the conspiracy] with their wealth or aided it with their advice, no inquiry was made." Seneca writes in Book 1 of *On Clemency* regarding Augustus that after he had forgiven Cornelius Cinna <note>Cn. Cornelius Cinna Magnus, who conspired against Augustus but was famously pardoned and later became a loyal consul.</note>, he was never again the target of a plot by anyone.

### CXLIII.

The same principle holds true in cases of sedition. Concerning the murder of Postumius <note>Marcus Postumius Regillensis, a Roman military tribune killed by his own soldiers in 414 BCE.</note>, Livy writes in Book 4: "The Consuls, acting with the greatest moderation and lenience through the punishment of a few—whom it was widely believed had brought death upon themselves—could not, once the matter was concluded, prevent the common people from taking the outcome very bitterly." And Tacitus says of Valens <note>Fabius Valens, a Roman general during the Year of the Four Emperors.</note>: *He, with a useful moderation, did not demand the punishment of anyone; and lest he become more suspect by feigning ignorance, he accused only a few.*

### CXLIV.

Indeed, many things are committed in a Republic for which it is enough to show repentance. Fabius, Germanicus, and Agricola <note>Three Roman figures noted for their strategic restraint and leadership: Quintus Fabius Maximus, the general Germanicus, and Gnaeus Julius Agricola.</note> were wise in this regard. Agrippina acted imprudently, for she did not want to wait for her son's <note>The Emperor Nero.</note> repentance or for him to grow weary of his vices. Likewise Caecina <note>Aulus Caecina Alienus, a general who was often quick to use force.</note>, who sought to avenge every fault immediately before there was time for the offender to repent. How much more moderately did the Prince of Flanders <note>Likely a reference to the historical governors or leaders during the Dutch Revolt (the Eighty Years' War).</note> treat the rebels: when they begged for pardon for their deed, he received them harshly at first; but nevertheless, lest this evil—if poorly handled—should spread further to his disadvantage, he forgave them their entire fault.

### CXLV.

Mutianus <note>Gaius Licinius Mucianus, a key advisor to Vespasian.</note>, however, ordered the son of Vitellius to be killed, claiming that discord would remain unless he extinguished the seeds of war. I deny that this is true, even...

<meta>The catchword "co ntradi-" indicates the next page begins with a contradiction or counter-argument to Mutianus's harsh logic.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the political virtue of clemency, arguing through Roman and early modern examples that rulers often secure their power better by pardoning conspirators and rebels than by rigorous punishment.</summary>
<keywords>clemency, conspiracy, sedition, repentance, Augustus, Tacitus, political stability, Roman history, statecraft</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on how a ruler should handle internal opposition and speech. The previous page ended with a discussion on the general Valens practicing moderation by not punishing every dissenter. This page moves into the specific handling of political rivals, the freedom of speech, and the protection of the office of the Prince.</meta>

# Disputation I.

...even though the shrewdest of political thinkers <note>original: *acutissimo Politicorum* — likely a reference to Niccolò Machiavelli, whose pragmatic and often ruthless political theories were frequently debated by later writers.</note> might disagree. How much more noble was Diocletian <note>The author likely means Marcus Aurelius here, as historical accounts attribute this specific act of mercy toward the family of the rebel Avidius Cassius to him, rather than Diocletian.</note>, who not only did not kill the children and friends of Cassius, but even bestowed honors upon them.

### CXLVI.

<margin>Whether freedom of speech should be left to the people.</margin>
A Prince acts not unwisely when he leaves the freedom of speech to the people, while he himself—as Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the de facto ruler of the Florentine Republic.</note> used to say—simply acts. Tacitus notes this: "**their deeds**," he says, "**were criticized, but their words went unpunished.**" And regarding Tiberius in the second book of his *Annals*: "**he did not wish for insults thrown against him to be called to trial.**" There exists a beautiful speech by Cremutius <note>Aulus Cremutius Cordus, a Roman historian who famously defended the freedom of the press before the Senate after being accused of treason for praising Brutus and Cassius.</note> on this subject. Surely Theodosius tempered this rightly in the Code, *If anyone should speak ill of the Emperor* <note>original: *C. si quis Imper. maled.* — Refers to a famous law in the Codex Justinianus (9.7.1) where the Emperors Theodosius, Arcadius, and Honorius decreed that verbal slanders against the ruler should be ignored rather than punished.</note>.

### CXLVII.

What the barbarian nations called pride and arrogance in the Roman people, we call **magnanimity** <note>Greatness of soul or high-mindedness.</note>. What they called servile hesitation, we call **patience**. What they called fear, we call **power and deliberation**. What they called treachery, we call the **law and the justice of the people**.

### CXLVIII.

<margin>Whether the killers of Galba were rightly slain by Vitellius.</margin>
Vitellius, although an enemy, ordered all the killers of Galba <note>Galba and Vitellius were rivals during the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 CE). Vitellius executed those who had murdered his predecessor, Galba, to discourage future regicide.</note> to be sought out and killed; not, as Tacitus says, out of honor for Galba, but according to the tradition passed down to princes—as a protection for the present and a vengeance for the future. Cosimo the Great <note>Cosimo I de' Medici (1519–1574), the first Grand Duke of Tuscany.</note> did the same, as did David and Alexander in the past, along with many others; and they acted rightly. Our author says sharply: "**whoever has succeeded [to the throne] is the avenger.**" And Caesar, after he had erected a statue of Pompey, made his own statue more lasting. They are imprudent and impious who do otherwise.

### CXLIX.

<margin>To what extent ambassadors are sacred.</margin>
An ambassador <note>original: *Legatum* — in this context, a diplomatic envoy or representative.</note> (which name, on Caesar’s testimony, has always been **sacred and inviolable** among all nations) is not to be harmed with impunity, even if he attempts hostile acts.

<summary>This page argues for the strategic benefits of political clemency and freedom of speech, while emphasizing that a ruler must strictly punish those who kill a predecessor to ensure their own future safety.</summary>
<keywords>freedom of speech, clemency, political stability, regicide, ambassadors, Lorenzo de' Medici, Tacitus, Vitellius, diplomacy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of political stability and the Law of Nations. # On Public Law

...to be harmed. Livy <note>Titus Livius (64 BC – 17 AD), the primary historian of early Rome.</note> writes concerning the ambassadors of Tarquinius <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the last king of Rome. His ambassadors were caught plotting a coup, but were released to respect diplomatic immunity.</note>: "*Although they seemed to have acted in such a way that they should be treated as enemies, the inviolable **law of nations** <term>jus gentium</term> <note>The 'law of nations'—an early conceptual framework of international law that governed interactions between different peoples, particularly regarding the safety of diplomats.</note> nevertheless prevailed.*" Thus, as Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56 – 120 AD), a Roman senator and historian known for his biting analysis of power.</note> testifies: <margin>Tacitus</margin> "*The Parthian ambassadors, although they had come with the most arrogant instructions, were nevertheless sent back with gifts.*"

### 150.

<margin>Whether Piso was fit for the Empire.</margin>
[The author argues] that **Piso** <note>Lucius Calpurnius Piso Frugi Licinianus, the short-lived heir of Emperor Galba. He was known for his rigid, stoic morality.</note> was too severe and—as Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the famous Roman orator and statesman.</note> used to say—too much a man of the old ways <note>original: *antiquorum hominum*</note> to be fit for seizing and holding the empire, regardless of what Galba <note>The elderly Emperor Galba, whose brief reign ended in a bloody coup in 69 AD.</note> might have thought to the contrary. Tacitus notes this in many places, especially when he says: "*Nothing else was said or done in public by Piso for the next four days.*" But regarding **Otho** <note>Galba's rival and successor, who courted the Roman people and the military through populist gestures.</note>, what kind of man was he? Otho did not fail to stretch out his hands to worship the mob, to throw kisses, and to do everything servilely for the sake of power. Truly, the favor of the people was sought more quickly through bribery and ambition than through **virtuous skills** <note>original: *bonas artes* — the traditional Roman virtues of leadership and character.</note>.

### 151.

**Vespasian** <note>The emperor who emerged victorious from the civil wars of 69 AD, founding the Flavian dynasty.</note> truly said that *for those desiring power, there is no middle ground between the highest peak and the precipice.* And Tacitus says elsewhere of Fabius Valens <note>A commander under Emperor Vitellius.</note>: "*Later, he rejected both plans—which is the worst thing in dangerous times—while he followed a middle course, he neither dared enough nor provided enough.*" Nor do I listen here to certain legal experts. Nevertheless, the **Emperor Henry** <note>Likely Henry IV (1050–1106), the Holy Roman Emperor, whose conflicts often led to questions of loyalty and neutrality.</note> acted unjustly when he accused neutrals of **high treason** <term>læsæ majestatis</term> <note>Lese-majesty: a crime against the dignity or security of the state or its ruler.</note>, according to the chronicler of Fulda <note>The *Annales Fuldenses*, a major source for 9th-century Frankish history.</note>. Although I do not deny that neutrality can be most dangerous.

### 152.

<margin>The political error of Vitellius in scattering the army after seizing power.</margin>
**Vitellius** <note>The third emperor of the year 69 AD, often portrayed as gluttonous and incompetent.</note> acted with too much security; once he had seized power, he scattered the legions, disarmed the cohorts, and reduced the number of troops. You might rightly call this a **solecism** <note>While a 'solecism' is usually a grammatical error, the author uses it here as a metaphor for a fundamental mistake in the "language" of politics and statecraft.</note> in political science. For in this way, as Mucianus <note>Gaius Licinius Mucianus, a general who was instrumental in bringing Vespasian to power.</note> testifies, he provided new seeds for war. On the contrary, Vespasian correctly [handled] the defeated legions, lest they remain...

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of diplomatic immunity and analyzes the political failures of Roman emperors like Piso and Vitellius, emphasizing that there is no safe "middle ground" in the pursuit of power.</summary>
<keywords>diplomatic immunity, law of nations, Piso, Otho, Vespasian, neutrality, Vitellius, political science, Roman history</keywords>

It focuses on the roles of fortune, betrayal, and the necessity of enduring even flawed rulers for the sake of stability.</meta>

...while they still acted with ambiguity in the civil war, they dispersed through Illyricum. Most shamefully, Caecina and Vitellius, according to Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56 – 120 AD), the primary historian for this period of Roman civil war.</note>, and the Gauls, according to Guicciardini <note>Francesco Guicciardini (1483–1540), a famous Italian historian of the Renaissance who wrote the *History of Italy*.</note>, divided their army; they would have been strong enough, as Tacitus testifies, if they had attacked together.

### 153.

<margin>Whether skill or fortune achieved more in the civil war between Vitellius and Vespasian.</margin>
In the civil war between Vitellius and Vespasian, as in others, fortune achieved more than skill <note>original: *virtus* — in this context, it refers to military excellence or strategic merit rather than just moral goodness.</note>, whatever the "clever Tuscan" <note>A reference to Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the Florentine philosopher who famously debated the roles of *virtù* (skill/drive) and *fortuna* (luck) in politics.</note> might deny. Anyone who accurately weighs the sum of the war will admit this. Ammianus Marcellinus <note>A 4th-century Roman soldier and historian.</note> speaks as a soldier—that is, candidly: *"Skill and fortune, though usually at odds, came together; if either had failed, the campaign would not have reached the perfect peak."*

### 154.

<margin>Whether those who betrayed Vitellius to Vespasian were treacherous.</margin>
Although it was in the public interest for Vitellius to be defeated, those who betrayed Vitellius to Vespasian cannot charge others with treachery, since they themselves had already deserted Galba <note>The Emperor whose death sparked the civil war; his successors were often judged by their initial loyalty to him.</note>. Tacitus writes in the third book of his *Histories*: Claudius wisely told the Parthian ambassadors that "the temperaments of Kings must be endured." And Cerialis <note>Quintus Petillius Cerialis, a Roman general known for putting down the Batavian rebellion.</note> said: "Just as one must tolerate drought, or excessive rains, and other natural evils, so must one tolerate the luxury or greed of those in power." Let those who call themselves **tyrannicides** <note>original: *τυραννοκτόνους* — a Greek term for those who kill a tyrant to liberate a state.</note> and "liberators of the world" (if the gods permit) take note of this. It is a common saying regarding Cassius <note>Gaius Cassius Longinus, one of the lead assassins of Julius Caesar.</note>, who killed Caesar, that he hated the King, but not the kingship itself.

### 155.

Tiberius was mocked by some because he sought a **Quaestorship** <term>quaestura</term> <note>The first official post in the Roman 'path of honors' (*cursus honorum*), primarily involving financial and administrative duties.</note> for his son Nero <note>Nero Julius Caesar (6–31 AD), the grandson of Tiberius, not to be confused with the later Emperor Nero.</note>. For as the legal expert <term>ICtus</term> <note>Abbreviation for *Jurisconsultus*, a professional authority on Roman law.</note> says, the quaestorship is merely an entry point and, as it were, the beginning of holding honors. And yet, Tacitus says, "these were the beginnings of the summit for the Caesars." It was well and according to the custom of the ancestors...

<summary>This page analyzes the Roman civil wars of 69 AD, arguing that luck often outweighs strategic merit and that citizens must endure bad rulers to avoid the chaos of constant rebellion. It also discusses the formal entry into political life through the office of the quaestorship.</summary>

# On Public Law.

...of the ancestors, Callistratus <note>Callistratus was a prominent Roman jurist active during the reigns of Septimius Severus and Caracalla.</note>: a certain order was applied to the holding of honors. For no one can hold a higher magistracy unless they have first undertaken a lower one.

## CLVI.

<margin>With what purpose Legates were joined to Proconsuls</margin>
It was not without reason that Legates <term>Legatus: a high-ranking Roman military officer or deputy governor who served as a subordinate to a higher commander.</term> were joined to Proconsuls <term>Proconsul: a governor of a Roman province, typically one who had previously served as a consul in Rome.</term> as if they were rivals and, in a sense, inspectors. And yet, the result of this was that the rights of the Legates sometimes increased, while those of the Proconsuls were diminished. Furthermore, because of this rivalry, great affairs are often hindered. Examples are found in the cases of Volumnius and Appius Claudius, Fabius Maximus and Marcellus, Corbulo and Vinidius. Truly, that saying of Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC), a Roman poet and philosopher.</note> fits here:

-> *From the very heart of the fountain of charms* <-
-> *rises something bitter that chokes one* <-
-> *even among the flowers.* <-
<note>original: *Medio de fonte Leporum / Surgit amari aliquid quod in ipsis floribus / angat.* This quote from Lucretius's *On the Nature of Things* suggests that even in the best arrangements, there is often a hidden source of trouble.</note>

## CLVII.

<margin>The insolence of the Triumvirs toward the sons of the proscribed.</margin>
The Triumvirs <term>Triumviri: specifically the Second Triumvirate (Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus), who held dictatorial power after the death of Julius Caesar.</term> insolently, as they did in many other things, prohibited the sons of the proscribed <note>The "proscribed" were political enemies listed by the Triumvirs to be legally killed and their property confiscated.</note> from the right of seeking honors. At the same time, they ensured that the sons of Senators would both bear the heavy burdens of their rank and lose their rights. Although Velleius Paterculus <note>A Roman soldier and historian (c. 19 BC – c. 31 AD).</note> considers this most shameful, it must be said that it was done according to the law of war, and done correctly. See Cicero, and Quintilian, book 11, chapter 1.

## CLVIII.

<margin>The wisdom of Augustus following the advice of Maecenas.</margin>
Wisely, and following the advice of Maecenas <note>Gaius Maecenas (70–8 BC), a wealthy advisor to Augustus and a famous patron of poets like Horace and Virgil.</note>, Augustus indulged the Roman people with the pleasure of the theater <note>original: *Histrionicam voluptatem* — specifically referring to the performance of stage actors or mimes.</note>; Cicero calls these "popular gifts." Much more wisely, although he loathed it, Tiberius did the same: "A different path," says Tacitus, "than that of his own character." <note>original: *Alia... ex morum via.* Tiberius was known for his personal austerity but realized he could not immediately force his own strict lifestyle upon a public accustomed to luxury.</note> But he did not yet dare to turn toward harsher ways a people who had been treated softly for so many years. Those who act otherwise commit a mistake.

## CLIX.

The rule of the *centesima* <term>Centesima rerum venalium: a one-percent tax on goods sold at auction, established by Augustus to fund the military treasury.</term> was most sacred among the Romans,

# Discussion I.

...[among the Ro]mans, and today among the Turks. And the lack of this thing <note>The author is referring to a "military treasury" or a dedicated fund for soldier pay, as evidenced by the mention of the *militare aerarium* below.</note> excites so many seditions in our own military service, whereas—according to an English writer—scarcely any movement regarding pay was ever stirred up in the Roman army. I do, however, except the civil wars. Tiberius said most truly that the military treasury rests upon the support of a tribute. <note>original: *militare ærarium subsidio niti.* This refers to the *Aerarium Militare*, established by Augustus to pay veteran benefits, funded by inheritance and sales taxes.</note>

<margin>Bad counsel should be punished, not outcomes.</margin>
### CLX.

It was a shameful thing in the law of Majesty <term>Majestas: the Roman law regarding high treason or any offense against the dignity and security of the state.</term> that anyone who had conducted the affairs of the Republic poorly should fall into a charge of treason. How much more prudently did the Athenians, and even the Romans of old, punish bad counsel—and indeed, most mildly—rather than the outcome.

<margin>The magnificence of Germanicus.</margin>
### CLXI.

Germanicus expressed the "magnificence" of Aristotle to the life <note>Germanicus (15 BC–19 AD) was a beloved Roman general; Aristotle defined "magnificence" (*megaloprepeia*) in his *Nicomachean Ethics* as the virtue of spending large sums of money appropriately for the public good.</note>; for while the Gauls, Italians, and Spaniards were offering weapons, horses, and gold, he praised their zeal but took only the weapons and horses for the war, and provided for the soldiers with his own money. The Romans did the same toward the Neapolitans, and toward King Philip of Macedonia. Livy <note>Titus Livius, the Roman historian.</note> says in Book 22: "Thanks were given to the ambassadors for their magnificence and care; a golden bowl of great weight was accepted."

<margin>Whether more account should be taken of infantry or cavalry.</margin>
### CLXII.

No greater account should be taken of the infantry than the cavalry, even if great men feel otherwise. The same must be thought regarding the Roman military, and the same regarding the Turkish military against Jovius <note>Paolo Giovio (1483–1552), an Italian physician and historian known for his works on the Ottoman Empire.</note> and others, who believe the entire strength of the war lies in the Janissaries <term>Janissaries: the elite infantry units that formed the Ottoman Sultan's household troops and bodyguards.</term>; by whom also the Janissaries are wrongly compared with the Praetorian soldiers. <note>The Praetorian Guard were the elite bodyguards of the Roman Emperors.</note>

<margin>What the Tribunician power is.</margin>
### CLXIII.

The Tribunician power <term>Tribunitia potestas: the authority granted to a Tribune of the Plebs, which included the power to veto actions of other magistrates and sacrosanctity of person.</term> is the next...

<header>On Public Law.</header>

...[while it pertained] to the empire, it was nevertheless not the "Supreme Empire," just as Tacitus refers to it by the term "the highest summit." <note>original: *summi fastigii vocabulum*. This refers to the *Tribunicia Potestas* (Tribunician Power), which Augustus used as his primary title to hold absolute power without the unpopular title of "King" or "Dictator."</note> Under this name, Augustus continued his rule for thirty-seven years, and he sought the "tribunician power" for the Princes of Youth. <note>The *Principes Iuventutis* (Princes of Youth) were Augustus's grandsons and intended heirs, Gaius and Lucius Caesar.</note>

### CLXIV.

<margin>To what extent certain things are rightly done against the laws in a Republic.</margin>
Sometimes in a Republic, certain things are done against the laws, and they are done rightly. Therefore, some rules in Civil Law must be weighed according to the circumstances and the momentum of the times and persons—which, as Aulus Gellius <note>An author from the 2nd century AD known for his *Attic Nights*, a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note> testifies, is difficult to include in formal precepts. Maenius, the camp prefect, had ordered two mutinous men to be executed against military law. For, as Vegetius <note>A late Roman author whose manual on military matters was widely read in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> testifies in Book 2: only the positioning of the camp, the layout of the rampart and ditch, and the care of military baggage, etc., pertained to the camp prefect <term>Praefectus castrorum: the third-in-command of a Roman legion, responsible for logistics and the camp itself.</term>; but he did not truly possess the right to punish a soldier. Dio Cassius, Book 53. Thus Tacitus adds that this was done more as a "good example" <note>Meaning it served as a necessary deterrent for the sake of discipline.</note> than by a granted right.

### CLXV.

<margin>The seat of Empire must be held firm.</margin>
Tiberius prudently stayed close to the seat of empire—that is, as Velleius and Tacitus call it, the "head of affairs" <note>original: *caput rerum*. This refers to the city of Rome or the central administrative capital.</note>—regardless of how the common people complained. Augustus, Antoninus Pius, Justinian, King Charles of France against Edward <note>Likely referring to Charles V of France and Edward III of England during the Hundred Years' War.</note>, Murad <note>original: *Amurathes*. Likely Sultan Murad I or II of the Ottoman Empire.</note>, and others did the same for the empire. Antonius asserted this reason: that the retinue of the Prince would be too heavy a burden for the people of the provinces. Verus, according to Capitolinus <note>One of the authors of the *Historia Augusta*, a collection of Roman imperial biographies.</note>, argued it was to prevent the seat of empire from being occupied by conspirators while the Emperor was absent.

### CLXVI.

Yet Otho acted imprudently when, while the ultimate fate of things was being contested, he left the army and went to Brescello... <note>During the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 AD), Otho withdrew from his troops at the Battle of Bedriacum, which contributed to his defeat by Vitellius.</note>

<header>Disputation I.</header>

[...he] yielded: as Tacitus <note>Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD) was a Roman senator and historian whose works are the primary source for the author's political theories.</note> says, "This was the first day that crushed the faction of Otho." <note>original: *Is primus dies Ottonianas partes afflixit*. This refers to the civil wars of 69 AD, the "Year of the Four Emperors."</note>

### CLXVII.

<margin>Feigned ignorance is often beneficial.</margin>
In a Republic <note>original: *Republica*. In this context, it refers generally to the State or Commonwealth, rather than a specific form of representative government.</note>, it is sometimes the mark of a wise person to appear not to understand. Tactics were understood, says Tacitus, but it was a part of their obedience not to let it be seen they were detected. <note>original: *intelligebantur artes... ne deprehenderentur*.</note> And regarding Agrippina <note>The mother of Emperor Nero, known for her political maneuvering.</note>, the only remedy against her plots was to act as if they were not understood. The writer Syrus knew this, saying to Dromo: *If you are wise, Dromo, you do not know what you know.* <note>original: *tu quod scis, nescis si sapis Dromo*. A quote from Publilius Syrus, a Roman writer of moral maxims.</note> However, I do not know if the feigned stupidity of Brutus <note>Lucius Junius Brutus, the legendary founder of the Roman Republic, who supposedly acted like a "brute" or fool to escape execution by King Tarquin.</note> should be categorized here. Certainly, the pretense used by Vitellius and Valens was empty and ridiculous. "Deceits are not deceits," as the saying goes, "unless you cultivate them with cunning." How much better it was when Galba openly acknowledged the mutiny of two legions; otherwise, as Tacitus notes, a concealed mutiny might be believed to be greater than it actually was.

### CLXVIII.

Sometimes in a Republic, certain things are done by force so that, as Tacitus says, there may be peace for the rest. <note>original: *cæteris quies sit*. This is the classic "Reason of State" argument: that one must occasionally act harshly to prevent wider chaos.</note> Nor do these actions conflict with Christian piety: Caesar <note>Julius Caesar, during the Gallic Wars.</note> judged that the others should be deterred by the example of punishment; therefore, he cut off the hands of all who had taken up arms but granted them their lives, so that the punishment of the wicked might be more visible. Yet, I would not wish to include that story of Valens, who, to strike terror into the Gauls, killed four thousand innocent people—nor the Prince of Flanders, who, after seizing Grandmont <note>Grandmontio, referring to the town of Geraardsbergen in modern Belgium. This likely refers to an event during the 16th-century religious wars.</note>, put everyone to death, including the elderly, pregnant women, and infants.

### CLXIX.

<margin>In seizing power, some pretext or title must be sought.</margin>
In every seizure of power, some pretext or title is adopted. Thus, the Emperors begged for—or rather, fabricated—the **Royal Law**, <term>Lex Regia: A law by which the Roman people supposedly transferred all their power and authority to the Emperor.</term> and the Popes fabricated the **Donation of Constantine**. <term>Donatio Constantini: A forged Roman imperial decree by which the Emperor Constantine supposedly transferred authority over Rome and the western Roman Empire to the Pope.</term>

<summary>This page discusses the strategic use of "simulated ignorance" and calculated force in statecraft, citing Roman examples to justify harsh measures while condemning unnecessary cruelty. It concludes by noting how rulers use legal pretexts, like the Royal Law or the Donation of Constantine, to legitimize their seizure of power.</summary>
