<header>On Public Law.</header>
<page-num>51</page-num>

...tini. <note>Likely the end of a name such as Justinian or Constantine from the previous page.</note> Suleiman <note>Suleiman the Magnificent (1494–1566), the longest-reigning Sultan of the Ottoman Empire.</note> did the same thing in order to occupy Rome, which he had already devoured in his hopes, as Jovius <note>Paolo Giovio (1483–1552), an Italian physician and historian known for his contemporary accounts of the Ottoman Empire.</note> testifies. Tacitus <note>The Roman historian Publius Cornelius Tacitus.</note> writes clearly of Tiberius: *Rumor also suggested that he should appear to have been called and chosen for power, rather than having crept in through a wife's ambition and an old man's adoption.* <note>original: "Dabat & fama ut vocatus, edoctusque potius videretur, quam per uxorium ambitum & senili adoptione inrepsisse." This refers to Tiberius becoming Emperor through the maneuvering of his mother Livia and his adoption by the aging Augustus.</note> Pope Paul III spoke simply and candidly: *"O,"* he said, *"you Scholastics, <note>Academic theologians or philosophers whose theoretical debates the Pope is dismissing here.</note> ignorant of all things, who seek the legal right of this empire; which we have already possessed for so many years against the force of all, and which we defend with the sword."*

### CLXX.

<margin>It is expedient for a Prince to designate a powerful heir.</margin>
It is beneficial for a Prince to designate a powerful heir rather than a weak one. For this reason, when Pyrrhus <note>Pyrrhus of Epirus (319–272 BC), a Greek king and statesman.</note> was asked to whom he would leave his empire, he replied, "to the one who has the sharper sword." And Tiberius, according to Tacitus, planned a long and continuous absence so that, once his father <note>His adoptive father, Augustus.</note> was removed, Drusus might perform the duties of the consulship alone; that is, so that the one he intended for the empire might grow in power and dignity. Thus today in France, the heirs of the kingdom take the title of the Dauphiné of Vienne; in England, the Duchy of Normandy; <note>The author uses Normandy here, though by this period the title "Prince of Wales" was standard; Normandy remained a historical claim of the English crown.</note> in Aragon, the Principality of Girona; and in Scotland, the Prefecture of Cumbria, both as a name and for their support.

### CLXXI.

Just as Religion, the Republic, the Senate, and Liberty are frequently used as a pretext <note>original: "obtentui," a mask or cover for true intentions.</note>—and there are examples of this in Pompey, Otho, Henry IV, Ferdinand, Ludovico Sforza, and others—so too many crimes are committed under the guise of embassies and treaties. Those who, for this reason, condemn treaties and embassies, surely they have a pumpkin for a heart. <note>original: "peponem pro corde habent." A Latin idiom, famously used by Seneca, meaning to be a fool or "soft in the head."</note>

### CLXXII.

I do not agree with Vegetius <note>Publius Flavius Vegetius Renatus, a late Roman writer on military matters.</note> in *Book 2, Chapter 1*, that the Romans were far [greater] in their auxiliary forces than in their legions...

<header>First Disputation.</header>
<page-num>52</page-num>
<warning>Left margin text is partially lost in the binding gutter.</warning>

...enrolled a larger number of soldiers. There are infinite contrary examples in Tacitus, <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a Roman historian and politician.</note> Livy, <note>Titus Livius (64 BC–17 AD), author of a monumental history of Rome.</note> and others. Moreover, what Tacitus once said regarding the Gauls, Germans, and Britons—that they lent their blood to a foreign domination—is today applicable to those Christians who are enrolled as Janissaries. <note>original: "Janitzaros." The Janissaries were elite infantry units of the Ottoman Sultan's household, often recruited from Christian subjects through the devshirme system.</note>

### CLXXIII.

<margin>
Legions should be recruited with careful selection.
</margin>
Theodosius acted incautiously, as Zosimus <note>Zosimus (fl. 490s–510s), a Greek historian known for his "New History" which critiques Roman imperial policy.</note> writes, because he admitted the barbarians from across the Danube into the legions without any selection. And elsewhere [it is noted] there was no order in the army, nor was there any distinction <note>original: "discrimen"</note> between a Roman and a barbarian. Thus, Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a philosopher-bishop who famously urged Emperor Arcadius to expel Goths from the Roman army.</note> rightly warned Arcadius to fill the ranks and legions with citizens. This was no small cause of the empire's ruin. Today, because war against the Turk is waged almost entirely with auxiliary troops, many outstanding matters are neglected. A notable and hateful example of this occurred at Aschaffenburg <note>original: "Schafneburgensem."</note> under Henry III. <note>Henry III (1017–1056), Holy Roman Emperor.</note>

### CLXXIV.

<margin>
The authority of Proconsuls and Legates.
</margin>
Rightly among the Romans, the Proconsuls <note>High-ranking Roman officials acting on behalf of a consul, typically governing a province.</note> held the highest command. Rightly also, legates <note>Envoys or high-ranking military officers.</note> were sent by them with no fixed instructions, so that they might act according to the situation and see to it that the Republic suffered no harm. <note>original: "ne Respublica detrimentum caperet," the standard Roman formula for granting emergency powers.</note> *Caesar* and *Tacitus* mention this in more than one place. Jurists <note>original: "ICti" (Iurisconsulti), legal experts.</note> describe this as having the free administration of all affairs.

### CLXXV.

<margin>
With what kind of instructions a General should be sent into war.
</margin>
Rightly also was this command expanded for Pompey the Great, Germanicus, and Corbulo <note>Three of Rome's most famous generals; Corbulo was a successful general under Claudius and Nero.</note> beyond the usual order, and I consider this a most useful practice. Following this same policy, the British Prince <note>Likely referring to a King of England or a high-ranking sovereign.</note> handed over blank papers <note>original: "cartas puras"</note> to his Vice-Chancellor, but signed with his seal, so that he might use them at his discretion in public business. Commines, Book [I]. <note>Philippe de Commines (1447–1511), a writer and diplomat whose memoirs are a key source for 15th-century European politics.</note>

<meta>catchword: business</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the integration of unselected foreign mercenaries into national armies, blaming such practices for the fall of Rome and contemporary military failures. It also advocates for granting generals and high officials broad, discretionary powers during times of war or public necessity.</summary>

<header>On Public Law.</header>
<page-num>53</page-num>

### CLXXVI.

Thus Justinian confirmed that he would ratify everything Belisarius did in war, and even after his own death, should he pass away first. <note>Emperor Justinian I (r. 527–565 AD) granted his general Belisarius extraordinary autonomy to act in his name during the reconquest of the Roman West.</note> According to Procopius, this act increased both Belisarius's authority and his morale. <note>Procopius of Caesarea (c. 500–565 AD) was the principal historian of Justinian's reign.</note> Sinan Pasha, for a full seven years under Amurath, exercised supreme command of the war with the same power in the camp as the Emperor himself. <note>Likely referring to Koca Sinan Pasha (d. 1596), a Grand Vizier of the Ottoman Empire under Sultan Murad III (Amurath).</note> This is attested by Sorantius, a wise and faithful writer; whom Jacob Geuder, the pride of the nobility of Nuremberg, <note>original: "Noricæ" - referring to the region around Nuremberg.</note> recently translated into Latin.

### CLXXVII.

Today, most unworthily, military commanders go to war with fixed mandates; they conduct affairs like sailors following a manual <note>original: "ex Commentario"</note>, as Galen describes. <note>Galen (129–c. 216 AD), the influential Greek physician whose writings often touched on philosophy and method.</note> From this it happens that, because of the vast distances of land, the plans are delivered after the events have already occurred, as Tacitus puts it. But by Hercules, Kings—*as Livy says in Book 9*—are not only free from all hindrances, but as masters of circumstances and time, they lead all things by their counsel rather than following them. Most foolishly and inhumanely of all acted Nero, who had ordered the fleets to return to Campania by a fixed day, without making any exceptions for the hazards of the sea.

### CLXXVIII.

<margin>Imperial Strategies.</margin>
Shrewdly and praiseworthily, Antonius—in order to spread the belief among the supporters of Vitellius <note>Vitellius was a claimant to the Roman throne during the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 AD). Antonius Primus was a general supporting the rival claimant, Vespasian.</note> that Mucianus was arriving with so many legions—ordered the army to salute one another and advance their steps, as if they had been bolstered by fresh reinforcements. They are fools <note>original: "Blenni" - a derogatory term for someone slow-witted or a blockhead.</note> who condemn these things. Polybius calls them "thefts of war" <note>original: "furta belli," referring to ruses, deceptions, or stratagems.</note>. Much more grandly, Marcus Tullius Cicero calls them "imperial strategies" <note>original: "consilia imperatoria"</note>. Tacitus calls these and other things "the remedies of war" <note>original: "medicamenta belli"</note>, Ammianus calls them "war devices" <note>original: "belli commenta"</note>, and Vegetius "field meditations" <note>original: "campestres meditationes"</note>. Although these were properly "military exercises" <note>original: "exercitia bellica"</note>: of which as and...

<meta>catchword: camp- (castro-)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of granting military commanders supreme autonomy, arguing that rigid mandates from a distant central government lead to failure. It also justifies the use of "thefts of war" or strategic deceptions as essential tools for a successful general.</summary>

<header>Discussion I.</header>
<page-num>54</page-num>

...of setting up camps or other matters in our modern warfare, there is neither a trace nor a track, as the saying goes. <note>original: "nec vola... nec vestigium" — a Latin idiom meaning there is not the slightest hint or evidence left of these ancient practices.</note>

### CLXXIX.

<margin>
The rights of victory are noted.
</margin>

The rights of victory include imposing garrisons and taxes, demanding hostages, disarming citizens, and fortifying the state with new laws; and as Caesar says, *“It is the law of war that those who have conquered may command those they have conquered in whatever way they wish.”* <note>original: "Ius est belli, ut qui vicerunt iis quos vicerunt, quemadmodum velint imperent." This quote is from Julius Caesar's "Commentaries on the Gallic War," specifically regarding the customs of the Gauls and Germans.</note> Therefore, the advisors of Henry, King of the Romans, repeatedly and fruitlessly argued that it was contrary to the royal majesty to give hostages to the Saxons. <note>Likely referring to Henry IV (1050–1106), who faced long-standing conflicts with the Saxons during his reign over the Holy Roman Empire.</note>

### CLXXX.

<margin>
Whether it is better to meet an approaching enemy or await their arrival.
</margin>

Otho sent Spurina ahead to occupy the banks of the Po River, hoping that Caecina could be halted within the borders of Gaul; but even then, as Tacitus says, Caecina had already crossed the Alps. <note>In the "Year of the Four Emperors" (69 AD), the Emperor Otho attempted to stop the invading forces of his rival Vitellius, led by the general Caecina.</note> Here, political thinkers ask whether it is better to go out and meet an approaching enemy, or rather to wait for their arrival and repel them with all one's strength. The Etruscan <note>A common scholarly nickname for Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the Florentine author of "The Prince" and "The Art of War."</note> prefers the latter; however, we believe it is more dangerous for an enemy to wage war within their own territory than outside of it.

### CLXXXI.

<margin>
A Senate Decree against false reports of military deeds.
</margin>

According to Polybius, there was a most beneficial Senate Decree <note>original: "SCtum" or "Senatus Consultum," a formal decree of the Roman Senate.</note> against those who falsely reported to a Tribune or General that they had performed some brave deed. This was called the crime of "false battle," for which Tacitus rightly criticized Domitian, as did Marcellinus regarding Constantine, for both celebrated triumphs based on false victories. <note>The Roman historian Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD) and Ammianus Marcellinus (c. 330–397 AD) often critiqued emperors who staged elaborate victory parades for battles that were never won or were insignificant.</note> Q. Thermus was also charged with this crime by M. Cato the Censor in a speech titled: *Against Q. Minucius Thermus regarding false battles*, as the most learned Mercerus <note>Josias Mercerus (1560–1626), a French philologist known for his editions of classical texts.</note> correctly reads it, though others mistakenly read "penalties" instead of "battles." <note>The author is clarifying a textual variant in the Latin manuscript tradition of Cato's speeches, distinguishing between "pugnis" (battles) and "poenis" (penalties).</note>

### CLXXXII.

There is no reason why those same people should not be liable to the same crime...

...they were guilty: Pompeius, Verannius, Terentinus Varro, Paetus, and others. This Paetus wrote back from his camp as if the war were already finished, in words which, as Tacitus says, were magnificent but empty of substance. <note>original: "verbis... magnificas, rerum vacuas" — referring to Lucius Caesennius Paetus, a Roman general whose incompetence in Armenia led to a humiliating surrender in 62 AD.</note>

### CLXXXIII.

<margin>
Whether Tiberius’s deceit should be detested.
</margin>
Those who so greatly detest the deceit <note>original: "simulationem" — the act of feigning or hiding one's true feelings.</note> in the Emperor Tiberius certainly do not understand the precarious position in which both he and the Republic stood at that time. Therefore, Tacitus frequently calls his words *obscure*, elsewhere *perplexed*, elsewhere *suspended*, and likewise *struggling to break out*. <note>Tiberius (r. 14–37 AD) was famous for his cryptic and indirect style of speech, which historians like Tacitus interpreted as a tool for political survival.</note> Cassius Dio calls it *preference and hypocrisy* <note>original: "αἱρέσειαν καὶ ὑπόκρισιν"</note>; the Greeks elsewhere elegantly call such speech *intrigues and evasions*. <note>original: "πλόκας καὶ παλιλλογίας" — literally "weavings" and "recapitulations" or "talking in circles."</note> An ancient poet expressed it more forcefully: *maliciousness spoken with cunning*; Cicero calls it *the wrappings of words*. Rightly did Tiberius say, and Louis XI, King of France, followed him in this: *He who knows not how to dissimulate, knows not how to reign.* <note>Louis XI (1423–1483) was known as the "Universal Spider" for his complex political schemes and belief that secrecy was essential to kingship.</note>

### CLXXXIV.

Domestic discord is the poison of families, whatever Cato might have said. Much more truthfully, Titus discusses the inconveniences of domestic suspicions in the works of Tacitus, as does Demetrius in the 40th book of Livy.

### CLXXXV.

<margin>
Why Nero, the son of Germanicus, did not attain the Empire.
</margin>
Although Nero, the son of Germanicus, was of the Augustan house, these were the reasons he did not attain imperial power: <note>This Nero (6–31 AD) was the eldest son of the popular general Germanicus and the ill-fated Agrippina the Elder; he was once the heir of Tiberius but fell victim to political intrigue.</note> 1. The premature incitement of his freedmen and clients. 2. Defiant and ill-advised talk about seizing the empire. 3. His mother’s more ready affection for Nero than for his brother Drusus. 4. The excessive favor of the common people. 5. His mother’s poorly concealed ambition. Let anyone placed in such a precarious position beware of these things.

### CLXXXVI.

Tacitus writes in the 5th book of his *Histories* that the city of Jerusalem had split into two factions, until the approaching Romans brought external war...

<header>Discourse I.</header>

...would produce harmony. Therefore, those who seize this opportunity are acting in vain. Filippo, the Duke of the Milanese, used to say cleverly that those follies (as he called the discords of the Florentines) cost him two million gold pieces. <note>original: "viginti centenis millib. aureorum" — Filippo Maria Visconti (1392–1447) frequently exploited the internal political strife of Florence for his own territorial gains.</note>

->CLXXXVII.<-

Tiberius sharply reproached Macro: *The setting sun is being deserted by him, the rising sun is being watched.* <note>original: "Occidentem ab eo deseri, orientem spectari" — Tiberius was accusing his prefect, Naevius Sertorius Macro, of shifting his loyalty to the heir apparent, Caligula, as Tiberius's health failed.</note> Following this is that "star of England," as witnessed by Buchanan in his *History of Scotland*. <note>George Buchanan (1506–1582) was a Scottish historian; the "star of England" likely refers to the rising power of Queen Elizabeth I or a similar succession event.</note> Both sayings originated with Pompey, who replied to Sulla: *that more people worship the rising sun than the setting sun.* <note>original Greek: "ὅτι τὸν ἥλιον ἀνατέλλοντα πλείονες ἢ δυόμενον προσκυνοῦσιν"</note> However, we deny that this is absolutely true.

->CLXXXVIII.<-

<margin>
Whether Thraseas was justly rebuked.
</margin>
Thraseas was rebuked because he pursued his senatorial freedom in matters that were too trivial. Why, they asked, did he not instead persuade or dissuade on matters of war, peace, taxes, and laws, and the other things that sustain the Roman state? (Note here, in passing, certain chapters of public law.) They argued that if these great matters were passed over with total silence, how much more should he abstain from the trivial? Thraseas, on the contrary, argued that he was doing honor to the fathers <note>The "fathers" refers to the Roman Senate.</note> so that it might become clear that those who pay attention even to small things would not feign their concern for great matters. Doubtless, he was looking back to that saying of the Philosopher: *the slight difference.* <note>original Greek: "τὸ παρὰ μικρὸν" — a reference to Aristotle’s principle that a small error in the beginning leads to a great error in the end.</note>

->CLXXXIX.<-

<margin>
Whether war should be prolonged.
</margin>
As I immerse myself in history, I encounter infinite examples of those who, although inferior in power, became superior by prolonging the war. Thus, in Tacitus, Suetonius <note>Gaius Suetonius Paulinus, the Roman general who defeated Boudica.</note>, who was most skilled in wars, argued that haste was useful to the enemy, while delay was useful to themselves. Let Sertorius be the model here, pulling out hairs one by one: <note>Quintus Sertorius (c. 123–72 BC) famously demonstrated to his troops that they could not pull a horse's tail off all at once, but could do so by pulling hairs individually, illustrating the power of persistence over brute force.</note> Because

<page-num>57</page-num>
# **On Public Law.**

...since he had been ignorant of this, Louis, King of Hungary, was killed along with the slaughter of his entire army. <note>Louis II of Hungary was defeated and killed by the Ottoman forces at the Battle of Mohács in 1526.</note> Therefore, that calamity <note>original Greek: $\pi\alpha\theta\acute{o}\varsigma$ (*pathos*), here referring to a tragic experience or suffering.</note> is not a reason to believe one must always yield to the more powerful—though that is what the leader of modern political thinkers <note>likely referring to Niccolò Machiavelli</note> almost believes.

->CXC.<-

It is false what they say, that a good man can in no way be held in honor or value under a tyrant. Opposite examples are found in Lepidus, Agricola, Lamia, Nerva, L. Piso, Volucrus, and others. <note>These historical figures served under difficult Roman emperors while maintaining their dignity or effectiveness.</note> Alexander rightly said: *The dispositions of Princes are softened by obedience.* <note>original: "obsequio mitigantur"</note> Callisthenes was too abrasive and harsh; <note>Callisthenes was a historian executed by Alexander the Great for his lack of tact and refusal to perform ritual prostration.</note> he could have proceeded on a path between abrupt defiance and demeaning compliance, a path free from both ambition and danger. It is equally false, as Machiavelli writes, that there is greater constancy and wisdom in the people than in the Prince.

->CXCI.<-

<margin>
Tiberius’s
cunning in
the case
of Libo
and the daughter of Sejanus.
</margin>
Libo is accused of treason. <note>Marcus Scribonius Libo Drusus was a Roman senator accused of conspiracy under Emperor Tiberius in 16 AD.</note> While Libo denied the charges, it was decided that his slaves should be interrogated under torture. But because under the old legal experts <note>original: "ICto" (Iureconsulto)</note> an interrogation involving the life of a master was forbidden, the cunning Tiberius—inventor of a new law—ordered the slaves to be sold individually to the public agent <note>By transferring ownership to the state, Tiberius bypassed the law protecting masters from their own slaves' testimony.</note>. He did the same in the case of the young daughter of Sejanus, who was violated <note>original: "compressa" — Roman custom forbade the execution of virgins, so she was sexually assaulted by the executioner to technically bypass this law before being strangled.</note> next to the noose and the executioner, because it was considered unheard of for a virgin to be subjected to capital punishment by the triumvirs. Similarly, the triumvirs of the Republic ordered children to be killed, but only after they had been dressed in the adult toga. Although these actions were meant to ensure that no fraud was committed against the laws, I can hardly grasp the reasoning.

->CXCII.<-

The Romans acted rightly in this matter by passing a Decree of the Senate <note>original: "SCto" (Senatus Consulto)</note> so that a "simulated adoption" would not help in any part of public duty, nor even benefit those using it to secure inheritances—

<meta>This page continues the legal and political commentary from page 57, which discussed the behavior of virtuous men under tyrants and critiqued Machiavelli's views on the stability of the people versus rulers. This page moves into specific Roman legal precedents regarding land ownership and military rewards.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
# **Disputation I.**

...satisfied. <note>The syllable "fet" is the conclusion of a word from the previous page, likely "satisfecit" (satisfied/fulfilled).</note> And commendably, C. Licinius was fined ten thousand pounds of bronze by M. Popilius Laenas under his own law, because he possessed one thousand acres <note>original: *jugerum*</note> of land together with his son, and by emancipating his son, he had committed fraud against the law. *Livy, book 7.* <note>Caius Licinius Stolo, who helped pass laws limiting land ownership, was later prosecuted for using a legal loophole—transferring land to his son—to exceed those very limits.</note>

### CXCIII.

<margin>What the military rewards of the Romans were.</margin>
Those who deny that Roman military rewards can be reintroduced are mistaken. See, I ask you, what kind were they? Increased pay, oak or grass crowns, spears, ornamental trappings, pounds of spelt, and quarts of wine. Arminius, as a barbarian man, despised these, calling them "cheap rewards of slavery." <note>Arminius was the Germanic leader who defeated the Romans at the Battle of Teutoburg Forest. This reference comes from Tacitus, *Annals* 2.9.</note> Livy expresses the truer reason: *A small thing to say,* he says, *but scarcity had made it significant: it was a great proof of affection, when each man, cheating himself of his own food, contributed what was taken from his own body and necessary uses for the honor of one man.* <note>Livy, *History of Rome* 25.12. The author argues that the value of the reward lay in the soldiers' shared sacrifice, not the material cost.</note> And what colonies were to the Romans, today are fiefs or commendams, <note>A "commendam" was a type of land grant or benefice often held in trust.</note> and for the Turks, *Timars*. <note>A *Timar* was land granted by the Ottoman Sultans between the 14th and 16th centuries in exchange for military service.</note> I feel the same regarding military punishments.

### CXCIV.

<margin>The excessive wealth or poverty of a great man is dangerous to the Republic.</margin>
Excessive riches, and equally the excessive poverty of any great man, are most dangerous in a Republic, regardless of what the "Etruscan Writer" <note>This refers to Niccolò Machiavelli, who was from Tuscany (ancient Etruria).</note> argues. Tacitus says of Otho: *Poverty was scarcely tolerable even for a private citizen.* <note>Otho's massive debts were a driving factor in his conspiracy against the Emperor Galba.</note> He says the same regarding Sulla and Plautus: *Sulla was destitute, which was the source of his exceptional audacity; Plautus possessed great wealth.* <note>Referencing the contrast between the ambitious, poor Sulla and the wealthy Rubellius Plautus, whom Nero feared because of his resources.</note> Therefore, and wisely for this very reason, Tiberius and Nero assisted the censuses of Senators with money; Claudius acted imprudently when he removed poor Senators from their rank.

### CXCV.

*Tacitus, Histories, book 5.* Cerialis, having laid waste to the island of the Batavians in a hostile manner, left the civilian fields and villas untouched, by a well-known artifice of generals. <note>Petilius Cerialis was a Roman general. This "artifice" was a psychological tactic used to sow discord between the rebel leaders (whose property might be destroyed) and the common civilians (who were spared), or to show that the war was only against the combatants.</note>

<meta>catchword: Surely (Nimirum)</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

Truly, [this was done] in order to make the enemy suspicious among his own people. He who disapproves of this is overly scrupulous. Hannibal used the same cunning against Fabius, and Zeno against Phalaris. <note>This refers to the strategy of sparing an enemy leader's property during a raid to make his fellow citizens suspect him of treason. Hannibal famously did this to the Roman general Fabius Maximus to undermine public trust in his leadership.</note>

### CXCVI.

<margin>
How laws
are some-
times
passed in
the Republic.
</margin>
Tacitus says that although laws were sometimes passed against criminals by edict, more often they were carried through by force due to social class discord, the desire to obtain illicit honors, or to banish illustrious men, and for other depraved reasons. <note>A reference to Tacitus, *Annals* 3.27-28.</note> From this, however, that man Andius unwisely attempts to weaken the authority of civil law. In the same passage, Tacitus truly remarks: *In the most corrupt Republic, the most laws exist.* Today we have come to the point where, contrary to the clear constitution of Justinian <note>The 6th-century Byzantine Emperor who codified Roman Law.</note>, cases are judged and decided not so much by the authority of the Laws <note>original: *LL.*, referring to the *Leges* or statutory laws.</note> as by the authority of the Doctors. <note>original: *DD.*, referring to the *Doctores*, the legal commentators and scholars whose interpretations often carried more weight in court than the original statutes.</note> In this era, that saying seems even truer: *as we once suffered from crimes, now we suffer from laws.*

### CXCVII.

Aristotle wisely omitted certain minor matters—which did not suit the majesty of royal knowledge—from the science of law-making <term>nomothetic science: the branch of political science dealing with the creation of laws</term>. Nevertheless, public law is not among these omitted things, since it deals with the same things that are <term>established matters</term> <note>original Greek: *ta estōta*, referring to the fixed institutions or principles of a state.</note> in civil science. The subject matter is almost the same; only the method of handling it varies. Examples of this can be found in our Tacitus.

### CXCVIII.

Furthermore, public law, just as history, seeks praise even from the smallest things—that is, it pursues even those things which for philosophers are <term>non-essential matters</term> <note>original Greek: *ta epousiōdē*, referring to incidental or accidental details rather than the core essence of a thing.</note>. Tacitus truly says: *It would not be without use to look into those things which seem trivial at first glance, from which the movements of great events often arise.* <note>Tacitus, *Annals* 4.32.</note> And a little later: *We [record] cruel commands, continuous accusations...*

<sig>C 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: fallacious</meta>

<summary>This page explores the corruption of legal systems, quoting Tacitus to argue that a surplus of laws indicates a failing state. It also defines Public Law as a discipline that, like history, must attend to small details to understand the causes of great political events.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the first "Disputation" or formal academic argument. The previous page (59) ended mid-sentence while discussing the corruption of the law. This page begins with the conclusion of a thought—likely a quotation from the historian Tacitus—about the repetitive and tragic nature of political history, before moving on to the specific duties and knowledge required of a Senator.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Disputation I.</header>

*...deceitful friendships, the destruction of the innocent; we link these same causes at the end, due to the obvious similarity and repetition of events.* <note>original: "fallaces amicitias, perniciem innocentium eaſdem exitu cauſas conjungimus, obvia rerum ſimilitudine & ſatietate." This is a quote from Tacitus, *Annals* 4.33, where he explains that political history often repeats the same tragic patterns of corruption and betrayal.</note>

### CXCIX.

<margin>A Senator ought to know the Republic.</margin>
However, a Senator should not be content merely with these matters while serving the Republic. Beyond these, he must know (to use the words of <term>Tacitus</term><note>A famous Roman historian and politician known for his analytical and often cynical view of power.</note>) the public resources: how many citizens and allies are under arms, the strength of the fleets, the tribute from the provinces, the taxes, the necessary expenditures, and the public grants. Marcus Tullius <term>Cicero</term><note>The most famous Roman orator and statesman, often cited as the ultimate authority on civic duty.</note> says in his third book *On the Laws*: it is necessary for a Senator to know the Republic, and that requirement covers a wide scope: how many soldiers it has, what its treasury is worth, which allies the Republic has, which friends, and which tributaries; he must know by what law, condition, or treaty each one is bound, and he must maintain the custom of decreeing and know the examples of the ancestors, etc.

### CC.

Nevertheless, these specifics are to be sought neither from political theory, nor from public law, nor entirely from history, but from the <term>registries of the Republics</term><note>original: "Rerumpublicarum regiſtris." These were official government records or archives, distinct from general history books or legal manuals.</note>. When two consuls serving among the <term>Ligurians</term><note>A people inhabiting the coastal region of what is now northwest Italy and southeast France.</note> were ignorant of these matters, the Roman Senate rightfully censured them, as <term>Livy</term><note>Titus Livius, a Roman historian who wrote a monumental history of Rome from its founding.</note> testifies in Book 40; Cicero likewise censured Calenus in his *Philippic Orations*.

<meta>The catchword at the bottom "CORO" suggests the next page begins with the word "COROLLARIUM" (Corollary).</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the practical knowledge required of a Senator, emphasizing that a statesman must look beyond law and theory to study the actual state archives, military strength, and financial records. It cites classical authorities like Tacitus, Cicero, and Livy to argue that ignorance of a state's resources and treaties is a punishable failing in a leader.</summary>

## COROLLARIES.
<note>In academic writing of this period, corollaries are secondary propositions that follow naturally from the main argument already proven.</note>

->1.<-

Just as the reading of history is necessary for a <term>Jurist</term><note>original: "Iurifconfulto." A legal expert or consultant learned in the law.</note>, so too is the knowledge of <term>civil law</term><note>original: "fcientia juris civilis." The body of laws governing the private rights and duties of citizens.</note> necessary for a Historian.

->2.<-

*Public law is handled poorly without the study of private law, but not the other way around.* <note>The author argues that while an understanding of individual rights (private law) is a prerequisite for understanding the laws of the state (public law), one can study private law effectively without a deep dive into public law.</note>

<summary>This page presents two concluding principles for the student of law: the essential symbiotic relationship between history and legal expertise, and the foundational necessity of private law for understanding public law.</summary>

# DISPUTATION
### REGARDING
# PUBLIC LAW

->A defense undertaken to obtain doctoral privileges in both laws <note>original: "in utroque jure." This refers to the study of both Civil (Roman) law and Canon (Church) law.</note> by decree of the Legal Faculty of Basel.<-

# BY FRANCISCO ROSELLO
->of Porrentruy.<-

## POSITION I.

<margin>To what extent public law is said to have once been hidden in the inner chambers of the Priests.</margin>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'P' (translated from the Latin 'I') featuring floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
Public law, which flows from the Laws of the Twelve Tables <note>The ancient foundation of Roman law, established around 450 BCE.</note>, was once hidden in the inner chambers of the Priests. This means, as <term>Pomponius</term><note>Sextus Pomponius, a Roman jurist of the 2nd century AD, known for his history of Roman law.</note> says, that the knowledge of interpreting that law and the legal procedures <note>original: "actiones." The specific formulas required to bring a case to court.</note> belonged to the college of priests. I do not understand this as if the actual exercise and pronouncement of that public law was solely in their power; rather, I believe the <term>Jurisconsult</term><note>original abbreviation: "IC." (Iurisconsultus); a legal expert who provided opinions on the law.</note> is speaking about the knowledge and interpretation of it. And when he says that someone was appointed to preside over private citizens each year, I interpret this as having the authority to explain the law to the people. For one is said to "preside" in many ways: by teaching, explaining, pronouncing the law, or instructing others in a trial. <note>original: "Præeſſe enim multis modis quis dicitur..."</note> *As Cicero says in the first book of On the Orator: "And I would preside over many who would either contend with you by injunction, or summon you to join hands according to the law."* <note>original: "Multiſque... praeeſſem, qui aut interdicto tecum contenderent, aut te ex jure manus conſertum vocarent." This refers to "manum consertio," a ritualized legal act of laying hands on a disputed property in the presence of a magistrate.</note> In the same manner, "predecessors" <note>original: "antecessores."</note> was the term used to refer to professors.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Francisco Rosello’s doctoral thesis, focusing on the historical origins of public law and its early interpretation by the Roman college of priests.</summary>
<keywords>Public law, Twelve Tables, Pomponius, Cicero, Jurisconsult, Priestly college, legal interpretation, Basel, doctoral defense</keywords>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

### II.

<margin>Why they [the compilers] dealt with public law in the Digests [so briefly].</margin>
Many wonder, and indeed some even accuse (among whom is Pratejus in *Omissions, chapter 2*), that they dealt with public law very briefly in the <term>Digests</term><note>The *Digest* (or *Pandects*) is a massive compilation of juristic writings organized by order of the Emperor Justinian I in the 6th century AD.</note>. However, I think these things should be blamed on <term>Tribonian</term><note>The chief official and legal expert responsible for overseeing the compilation of the Corpus Juris Civilis under Justinian.</note> more sparingly. For it is believable that very few writings of the ancients regarding that law existed in the age of <term>Justinian</term><note>The Eastern Roman Emperor who sought to revive the empire's greatness and codified Roman law.</note>, since already in a much more elegant and ancient century—in which the study of public law should have been most excited due to the extreme difficulties of the times at home and abroad—scarcely any account of it was held, according to <term>Cicero</term><note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman and orator.</note> in *Book 1 of the Laws*, where he asserts that public law was unknown because it was less necessary in practical use.

### III.

Do you see the reason for this neglect? (Namely, the <term>Jurisconsults</term><note>Legal experts and scholars in ancient Rome.</note> advise that everyday matters are to be treated more fully <note>original Latin reference: "l. legavi. D. de lib. leg." Referring to a law in the Digest concerning legacies of books.</note> and those things <term>which occur most frequently</term><note>original Greek: "ἐπὶ τὸ πλεῖστον" (epi to pleiston), a phrase used in legal logic to mean matters that happen in the majority of cases.</note>.) From this, I think it happened that the books of <term>Tubero</term><note>Quintus Aelius Tubero, a jurist and historian from the 1st century BC known for his archaic style.</note> on public law perished; yet a longer life would have sustained them uncorrupted if he had preferred to season them with the fresh salt of common speech rather than with the old leaven. But <term>Pomponius</term><note>Sextus Pomponius, a Roman jurist of the 2nd century AD.</note> says the obsolete words of the author were less pleasing.

### IV.

Added to the cause brought forth by Cicero is that public law does not remain solely within the ranks <note>original: "militibus," here used metaphorically to mean the specific service or confines of Roman civil law.</note> of Roman civil law, but becomes common to the world and to that "great Republic and state" (of which <term>Seneca</term><note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Stoic philosopher.</note> and <term>Lactantius</term><note>An early Christian author (c. 250–325 AD) who blended classical rhetoric with theology.</note> speak). For nature willed that man be a social animal, and that human society be governed by a certain <term>law of nations</term><note>original: "jure quodam gentico," referring to the *ius gentium*, or universal law thought to be common to all humanity.</note>. Whence we see that the <term>fetial laws</term><note>original: "jura fæcialia." The religious and legal rules governing international relations, such as declarations of war and the signing of treaties, overseen by the college of Fetial priests.</note> of the Romans were almost the same as other peoples' in regard to their substance...

<meta>catchword: ...stance [substantiam]</meta>

<summary>This page explains why public law is underrepresented in the Justinian Digest, attributing it to a lack of ancient sources and the archaic style of earlier writers like Tubero. It also argues that public law is a universal "law of nations" rather than a strictly Roman concern.</summary>

<header>Disputation II.</header>

[...even] things were common; for it was not permitted either for different peoples to wage war against one another, nor for philosophers of different sects to debate, without laws and principles adapted and accepted by both sides. To write down universal laws that would survive through common reason and along with the very machinery of the world seemed to most <term>jurisconsults</term><note>Legal experts and scholars of the law.</note> (if one may guess) a superfluous task.

->V.<-

For this reason, <term>Tullius</term><note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman.</note> might be subject to criticism when he writes in *On Duties, Book 1*: <note>original: "Belli quidem æquitas sanctißimè fæciali populi Romani jure perſcripta eſt."</note> *The fairness of war indeed was most sacredly prescribed by the <term>fetial</term><note>The Fetials were a college of priests in ancient Rome who advised on international relations and declarations of war.</note> law of the Roman people.*

->VI.<-

He could, however, have been referring to certain solemn rites of the <term>Quirites</term><note>A formal name for Roman citizens in their civil capacity.</note>. Of such a kind was the <term>clarigatio</term><note>A formal demand for restitution or satisfaction made by a herald before a declaration of war.</note>, which scholars believe was so named because the <term>pater patratus</term><note>The chief of the fetial priests, who acted as the representative of the Roman people in concluding treaties or declaring war.</note> went to reclaim property with a clear voice <note>original: "clara voce"</note>. This was a feature of Roman law: for one can see other customs elsewhere, such that the declaration of war might be made by written notice. <term>Ludovicus Avila</term><note>Luis de Ávila y Zúñiga (c. 1500–1564), a Spanish diplomat and historian who wrote a famous account of the Schmalkaldic War in Germany.</note> testifies concerning the German war that the Duke of Saxony and the Landgrave sent to the Emperor a certain noble boy with a piper, who carried letters of defiance (as our Doctors of Law call it, <term>diffidatio</term><note>A formal renunciation of allegiance or a declaration of hostility.</note>) on the end of a staff, and he says this is the custom of Germany in declaring war. So also the <term>sagmina</term><note>Sacred plants or sod plucked from the Roman Citadel, which ambassadors carried to protect them from harm.</note>, by which Roman envoys were made sacred, were a matter of Roman law. For other emblems protected Greek envoys, according to the testimony of Marcianus in *Book 8 on Various Matters*. <term>Suidas</term><note>A 10th-century Greek encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> is the source for the fact that barbarians used crowns and leaves instead of a <term>caduceus</term><note>A herald’s staff, typically entwined with snakes, used as a symbol of peace and diplomatic immunity.</note>. Furthermore, there was not the same form for striking a treaty among all nations, as <term>Alexander ab Alexandro</term><note>Alessandro Alessandri (1461–1523), an Italian jurist known for his work "Genialium Dierum" (Genial Days), which explored ancient customs.</note> taught in his *Genial Days*. Thus, finally, the peculiar [rites of the citizens...]

...was peculiar to [Roman law], regarding the embassy of nations (which natural equity otherwise tempers) that he who had struck an ambassador should be surrendered to the enemies. <note>original: "libr. ult. ### VII.

Furthermore, just as our authors <note>The Roman legal scholars or jurists.</note> were less diligent in writing down that law whose authority is bounded only by the world <note>The *ius gentium* or Law of Nations.</note>; so they are found to have been more studious regarding the public laws which were contained within Roman borders. Did not the ancients explain at length concerning the magistrates? Concerning their jurisdiction and power in prosecuting crimes? To whom indeed is it unknown that <term>Macer</term><note>Aemilius Macer, a Roman jurist of the 3rd century AD.</note> and <term>Arrius Menander</term><note>A Roman jurist and specialist in military law.</note> wrote on military matters, and others here and there on captives—all of which, however, referred back to the camps and soldiers of the Roman people? To whom can that <term>Rubric</term><note>A title or heading in a legal code, traditionally written in red ink.</note> concerning embassies be hidden—not indeed those concerning foreign nations and belonging to outsiders, but provincial ones directed back toward the Roman Republic?

### VIII.

<margin>
The multiple meanings of public law.
</margin>
Now let us approach the matter more closely. There is more than one meaning of "public law." For sometimes it is taken for <term>common law</term><note>In this context, *ius commune* refers to law that applies generally to all citizens, rather than the modern English legal system.</note> <note>original: "lib. nihil consensui §. 1. D. de reg. jur."</note>, which will become manifest if you compare the final law <note>original: "l. ult. C. de in int. restit. min. & l. inter eos, §. pen. D. fidejusso."</note>. 
<margin>
1. It is taken for "common."
</margin>
The reason for this name is that laws are established not for one person, but for all and for the whole people (from which "public" is named, as if it were *populic*, or "belonging to the people"). 
<margin>
2. For that which depends on authority.
</margin>
Sometimes also public law is that which depends on the authority of the people or the Prince, who represents the people. Thus the <term>making of a will</term><note>The legal capacity to draft a testament, known in Latin as *testamenti factio*.</note> is said to belong to public law <note>original: "l. 3. D. qui testam. fac. poss."</note>, nor can those...

<summary>This page discusses how Roman jurists prioritized domestic public law (magistrates, military, and provincial matters) over universal international law. It also begins to define the multiple legal meanings of the term "public law," distinguishing between general common law and law derived from state authority.</summary>

<header>Disputation II.</header>

<margin>
which depends on the authority of the people or the Prince.
</margin>
...which [depends on the authority of the people or the Prince], for a right denied to a private individual can later be granted [by the Prince]. <note>original: "quod de- jure denegata privato concedi post." citing Law 6 of the Digest, "On the goods of freedmen."</note> Hence, I believe **Pardoux Prate** <note>original: "Pardulphum Pratejum" (1520–1570), a French jurist and humanist scholar.</note> was mistaken in the first chapter of his book *On Inversions*, where he argued that the subject of testaments <note>The making of wills.</note> pertains to the study of public law. The law he cites does not support his view; indeed, it has even been denied that the wills of soldiers are part of the public law we are currently investigating.

->IX.<-

<margin>
What it is, properly speaking.
</margin>
Public law, in its proper and restricted sense, is that which regards the **state of the Roman Commonwealth**; <note>original: "statum rei Romanæ"</note> I understand this in terms of its primary purpose. For otherwise, that which is publicly useful is also useful privately, and vice-versa. <note>The author notes that while laws serve the state, they also benefit individuals.</note> I include the law of Roman citizenship within "civil law," as I am not inclined to believe **Cujas**, <note>original: "Cujacio" — Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), one of the most influential legal scholars of the Renaissance.</note> who seems to wrongly tear these concepts apart in his lectures on justice and law.

<margin>
What is its origin?
</margin>
Regarding the origin of public law, there is a significant doubt as to whether its streams flow from the same springs as private law. We believe that neither art nor nature forbids public law from being collected from **natural, international, and civil precepts**, <note>The author argues that public law, like private law, is built from universal human reason (natural), common customs of nations (gentile/international), and specific state statutes (civil).</note> just as private law is—even if it seems otherwise to certain great masters. **Guillaume Ranchin** <note>original: "Guilielm. Ranchin." (1560–1605), a French law professor.</note> accused them of error in the third book of his *Miscellaneous Readings*; yet he himself is not beyond reproach, as he denies that public law also flows from civil law.

->X.<-

<margin>
Is Sacred Law correctly separated from Public Law?
</margin>
Another difficulty, no less significant, arises from the fact that the poet **Ausonius** separates **Sacred Law** from Public Law in these verses:

> Triple is the law, which the twelve-fold tables sanctioned thrice; <note>original: "Ius triplex, tabula quod ter sanxere quaternæ." Referring to the Twelve Tables, the foundation of Roman law. Ausonius suggests law is divided into three parts: Sacred, Public, and Private.</note>

<summary>This page defines public law as the study of the Roman state's welfare and argues that it shares the same origins (natural and civil) as private law. The author disputes contemporary scholars like Prate and Cujas while introducing the classical "triple" division of law including Sacred Law.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of public law. On the previous page, the author argued against Pardoux Prate's view that private wills belong to public law. <page-num>67</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

*Everything that exists anywhere is either sacred, private, or the common property of the people.* <note>original: "Sacrum, privatum, & populi commune quod usquam est."</note>

->And Horace<-
---
> *—— —— this was once wisdom,*
> *To separate public things from private, and sacred things from profane.* <note>original: "fuit hac sapientia quondam, Publica privatis secernere, sacra profanis." From Horace's *Ars Poetica*.</note>

To these may be added **Quintilian** <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a Roman rhetorician.</note> in Book 2, Chapter 4, who divides law into the sacred, the public, and the private. However, **Ulpian** <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 AD), one of the most influential Roman jurists.</note> includes the "sacred" under the category of "public law." **Pratejus** <note>Pardoux Prate, the jurist mentioned on the previous page.</note> considers this to be the more correct view in his *Dicaeologia*, Chapter 4. But to me, if I may speak my true mind, the matter appears differently. I do not hesitate to assert that it was a characteristic of paganism—the traces of which we ought not to imitate—to place "sacred law" under the heading of public law, and thus under civil law. That this was the practice of the Romans is sufficiently attested by **Tullius** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC).</note> who wrote: *No one shall have gods separately, nor new or foreign ones, unless they are publicly adopted; they shall worship privately.* <note>original: "Separatim nemo habessis Deos, neve novos sive advenas, nisi publicè adscitos, privatim colunto." From Cicero's *De Legibus*.</note> This was certainly fitting for the Romans, who adapted and subjected religion to the interests of the Republic, rather than adapting the Republic to religion—as **Machiavelli** <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527). The author refers here to Machiavelli's analysis of Roman religion as a tool for political stability.</note> aptly notes. To us, however, this is unworthy; for we do not acknowledge gods peculiar to this or that nation, but believe in a universal Church, one shepherd, and one fold.

### XI.

<margin>Divine law is separated from human law, and that which is civil is divided into public...</margin>
Therefore, we separate **divine law** from **human law**, and we divide human law—insofar as it is civil—into public and private. We believe that divine law—which gives commands concerning faith, doctrine, the worship of God, the appointment of ministers of the Word, and their duties and office—is sufficiently handed down in the **Holy Scriptures**. We judge that neither the decrees of **Justinian** <note>The Byzantine Emperor Justinian I, whose *Codex* forms the basis of civil law.</note> in the first book of the *Codex* can prejudice these Scriptures, nor even less the traditions of recent Roman... <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to "Romanists" or "Roman Catholics."</note>

<meta>catchword: mano- (likely continuing "manorum" or "Romanorum")</meta>

<summary>The author argues against the Roman tradition (supported by Ulpian and Machiavelli) of subordinating sacred law to public law, asserting instead that divine law is independent and derived solely from Scripture.</summary>

...of the Romans, which you would truly say were invented according to the custom of ancient sacred civil law and devised to strengthen the Papal kingdom. **Gratian** <note>Johannes Gratian (12th century), the canon lawyer who compiled the *Decretum Gratiani*.</note> certainly, in his *Decretum*, includes sacred law under the category of public law.

->XII.<-

<margin>
Whether
Ulpian
correctly
limits
public law
to sacred
matters and
magistrates.
</margin>
**Ulpian** <note>A famous Roman jurist (c. 170–223 AD) whose writings comprise about a third of the *Digest*.</note> is accused of limiting public law to sacred matters and magistrates; and just as that inclusion of the sacred seemed superfluous to us, so this focus on magistrates seems too narrow to some. I certainly do not entirely excuse the Jurisconsult <note>A professional legal expert in Rome.</note>: but if you add a section on public affairs, there would not be much more you could desire. For the term "magistrate" encompasses jurisdiction, administration, and duty: and from this trunk many little branches blossom.

->XIII.<-

<margin>
How many
types of
magistrate.
</margin>
A magistrate is either supreme or inferior. For us, the supreme magistrate is the **Roman Prince** <note>Referring to the Emperor of the Holy Roman Empire, seen as the successor to the Roman Emperors.</note>, who Ulpian rightly says is *released from the laws* <note>original: "legibus solutum"</note>, since the people—who were not bound by their own laws if you consider the majesty of the whole body, rather than the obedience of individuals—conferred upon him and into him all their command and power.

->XIV.<-

<margin>
By what
law ma-
jesty was
transferred
to the
Emperors.
</margin>
The question is, by what law and when was this majesty <note>The supreme sovereign power of the state.</note> transferred to the Emperors? I am reluctant to depart from the authority of the **Pandects** <note>Also known as the *Digest*, a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions.</note>. And so, I believe it was done by law (with all due respect to the most learned **Lord Vultejus** <note>Hermann Vultejus (1554–1634), a prominent German jurist.</note>), and that it was named the **Royal Law** <note>original: "Lex Regia"</note>. I also believe that a single law was established for all future Princes, although **Marius Salonius** <note>A 16th-century legal commentator.</note> imagines there were several. That this law was passed under **Vespasian** <note>Roman Emperor from 69 to 79 AD.</note> is sufficiently proven by that bronze tablet <note>The *Lex de Imperio Vespasiani*, a famous archaeological artifact found in the 14th century.</note> which is kept today in the Capitoline temple at Rome (in the Palace of the Conservators), preserving the very words of the law, with only a few parts cut off...

<summary>The author critiques the Roman and Canon law tendency to merge sacred and public law, then defines the supreme magistrate (the Emperor) as being legally sovereign because the people transferred their power to him via the "Royal Law." This page highlights the "Lex Regia" and its historical evidence found on a surviving bronze tablet in Rome.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of the "Prince" or supreme ruler. While the previous page established that the Roman Prince was technically "released from the laws" (legibus solutus), the author now begins to qualify this by arguing that the ruler is still bound by natural law and must respect the property rights of his subjects.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

...preserves. I do not see, therefore, how many can probably refer it <note>Likely referring to the law conferring imperial power</note> to the Augustan age <note>The era of the first Roman Emperor, Augustus Caesar.</note>. For what need would there have been for the acts, deeds, decrees, and commands (these are the words of the law) from **Vespasian** <note>Roman Emperor (r. 69–79 AD); the author is discussing the "Lex de Imperio Vespasiani," which formally conferred power on him.</note> backwards to be held as just and ratified by that law, if a law already existed which established the emperor's power as greater than any exception?

### XV.

<margin>To what extent the Prince is released from the laws.</margin>
Furthermore, if one asks about natural laws and the law of nations <note>original: "ius gentium" — a concept of law held to be common to all human beings.</note>, I state firmly that the **Prince** is not exempt from them. Those who believe they are permitted anything degenerate into tyrants, unworthy of a bloodless death; they think they are fulfilling their majesty while they snatch goods from their subjects without just reason, without purchase, without exchange, without prescription, and without succession. *From this it follows that the Prince cannot, out of the fullness of power, take away anyone’s right without cause.*

### XVI.

This conclusion, being based on natural law, admits no distinction. Nor can that opinion of the interpreters be anything but detrimental to the State <note>original: "Reipub." — the Republic or Commonwealth.</note>, which distinguishes between the Prince’s absolute power and his ordinary power. This is gathered from **Gail** <note>Andreas Gail (1526–1587), an influential judge and legal scholar in the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, Book 2, Observation 56, who would have done better to reject that common calculation (upon which a great foundation of tyranny is built) rather than to encourage it.

### XVII.

Since these things are absolutely true concerning the Prince, it is well inferred that these formulas—*Notwithstanding [any law], from certain knowledge, by his own motion, from the fullness of power, etc.* <note>original: "Non obstante ex certa scientia, proprio motu, de plenitudine potestatis"</note>—change nothing of the law of nations. Therefore, that presumption of absolute power, which the Doctors <note>The "Doctors of Law," or academic legal experts.</note> believe to be present in those formulas in a Prince's <term>decree</term> <note>original: "rescripto" — a formal written response or decree from the ruler.</note>, must be considered poorly conceived.

<meta>catchword: Prince's</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while a ruler may be "above" civil laws, he is strictly bound by natural law and the law of nations, particularly regarding private property. He critiques legal scholars who use technical formulas to justify absolute imperial power or tyranny.</summary>

<keywords>Princeps, natural law, ius gentium, absolute power, Andreas Gail, Vespasian, property rights, legal formulas, tyranny</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal argument regarding the limitations of the ruler's power, specifically critiquing the idea that a Prince is above questioning. It transitions into a discussion of the "Rights of Majesty" (sovereignty), focusing on the ruler's exclusive power to create laws and appoint officials (magistrates).</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Second Disputation</header>

...added of the Prince; and these words of **Baldus** <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400), an influential Italian jurist specializing in civil and feudal law.</note> in the preface to the Feuds <note>Referring to the *Libri Feudorum*, a collection of feudal law.</note> are less than sound: *“When the Prince wills something from certain knowledge, no one can say to him: Why are you doing this?”* <note>original: “Quando Princeps aliquid vult ex certa scientia, nemo potest ei dicere, cur ita facis?”</note>

### XVIII.

<margin>The promulgation of laws and the creation of magistrates are rights of Majesty.</margin>
Furthermore, by the right of majesty <term>juris Majestatis|The sovereign rights or prerogatives that belong exclusively to the supreme ruler.</term>, the proclamation and drafting of laws, as well as the creation of magistrates, belong to the **Emperor** alone and pertain to public law. Indeed, the state cannot stand or be safe without them, as **Justinian** <note>The Roman Emperor (r. 527–565 AD) who oversaw the massive codification of Roman law known as the *Corpus Juris Civilis*.</note> indicates in these words: *“Civil rights began to exist at the time when cities began to be founded, magistrates to be appointed, and laws to be written.”* <note>original: “Civilia jura tunc esse cœperunt, cum & civitates condi, & magistratus creari, & leges scribi cœperunt.”</note>

### XIX.

Every inferior magistrate, therefore, is appointed by the Prince and is a public person who presides over the administration of justice <term>jurisdictio|Jurisdiction; the legal authority to administer the law and hear cases.</term> in his own assigned areas. *By Hercules, this definition is to be held as superior to the description by **Bodin** <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), a French jurist whose work “The Six Books of the Republic” defined the modern concept of sovereignty.</note> in Book 3, Chapter 3 of “The Republic.”*

### XX.

<margin>The nature of the Roman Magistrate.</margin>
The Roman magistrate commanded either at home “before the tribunal” <note>Presiding over civil or criminal court proceedings.</note> or abroad “for the camp” <note>Acting as a military commander in the field.</note>; that is, he managed the Roman state in either times of peace or war. He who was the director of peace presided over legal administration either in the city or in the provinces. Hence there were urban magistrates, such as **Consuls**, **Urban Prefects**, and **Tribunes**; and provincial ones, such as **Presidents** of provinces, **Proconsuls**, **Legates**, **Duumviri** <note>A pair of officials holding joint power, often at the local municipal level.</note>, etc. Some, however, were tossed about following the Prince by the winds of the court, such as the **Praetorian Prefects**, **Quaestors**, and others.

### XXI.

<margin>The manifold nature of jurisdiction.</margin>
All jurisdiction truly depends upon the Prince. Nor do we think it is a contradiction if we say that a certain jurisdiction belongs to a magistrate by the right of his office, and yet say the same is granted by the Prince: Just as—

<meta>The catchword "madm" is an abbreviation for "quemadmodum" (just as), suggesting the next page will provide a comparison or analogy for how power flows from the ruler to the officials.</meta>

<summary>This page identifies the proclamation of laws and the appointment of officials as the core powers of a sovereign. It provides a historical overview of various Roman administrative and military roles, asserting that all such authority ultimately derives from the Prince.</summary>
<keywords>Sovereignty, Majesty, Law-making, Magistrates, Roman Law, Justinian, Jean Bodin, Jurisdiction, Urban Prefects, Proconsuls</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the appointment of magistrates and the nature of their authority. It specifically addresses how jurisdiction—the legal power to hear and decide cases—is granted and the limits of that power among equals.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

...just as it is not contradictory that the power to appoint a judge belongs to the right of a magistrate, and that same power is granted to certain individuals by law, as stated in *Book 12, Section 1 of the Digest regarding trials.* <note>The *Digest* (or *Pandects*) was a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions organized under the Emperor Justinian.</note>

### XXII.

<margin>How jurisdiction is held.</margin>
However, if we wish to examine the matter more precisely, it is necessary to employ the elegant division found in *Book 1 of the Digest regarding the office of the one to whom jurisdiction is delegated.* We should say that jurisdiction either belongs by the right of the magistrate, or is specifically permitted by law, by a decree of the Senate <term>Senatusconsultum|A resolution or law enacted by the Roman Senate.</term>, or by a constitution of the Princes. Therefore, the former type of jurisdiction can be delegated to another, but the latter cannot—not even by a delegate of the Prince. I am well aware, however, that the most learned Scipio Gentilis <note>Scipio Gentilis (1563–1616) was an influential legal scholar known for his work on Roman law and international relations.</note> wrote in *Book 3 of "On Jurisdiction," chapter 28*, that in this case, "pure power" <term>merum imperium|The highest form of judicial authority, including the power to impose the death penalty.</term> can indeed be sub-delegated.

### XXIII.

At this point, that vast subject of simple jurisdiction, of crimes, and of punishments would certainly need to be explained, since that also pertains to public law. But since these topics require specialized treatises of their own, we shall pursue other matters that are no less rewarding.

### XXIV.

<margin>An equal does not exercise jurisdiction over an equal.</margin>
An equal cannot exercise his jurisdiction over another of equal rank. <note>original: "Par in parem exercere jurisdictionem suam non potest." This is a foundational legal principle: no one has authority over a peer of the same status.</note> Because of this, some believe it was established that a Praetor <note>In ancient Rome, the Praetor was the high-ranking official responsible for the administration of justice and the courts.</note> cannot manumit <term>manumittat|The formal act of releasing a slave from bondage.</term> a slave in the presence of his colleague, according to *Book 1 at the end of the Digest on the office of consuls*. Yet, in that same passage, it seems a Consul is permitted to manumit in the presence of his colleague, even though it is said that just as a Praetor lacks authority over another Praetor, so a Consul has no power over another Consul (*Book 13 of the Digest on the Trebellian Decree of the Senate*). Alberico Gentili <note>Alberico Gentili (1552–1608), the brother of Scipio Gentilis mentioned above, was a pioneer of international law.</note> in a recent writing on the *Book of Maccabees* attempted to untie this knot; we shall explain the matter in a different way.

<meta>catchword: They protect</meta>

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding the definition and classification of Roman officials. While the previous page dealt with the source of jurisdiction—whether from the prince, the law, or the Senate—this page examines whether specific roles, such as town leaders, senators, and deputies, truly qualify as "magistrates."</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>Second Dissertation</header>

Other officials also protect and support the Roman state, even though they lack jurisdiction <term>jurisdictio|The legal authority to administer justice and hear cases.</term>. These include Decurions <term>Decuriones|Members of a local town council in the Roman provinces.</term>, provincial deputies, notaries, scribes, bailiffs, couriers, lictors <term>Lictores|Attendants who carried the fasces (bundles of rods) as a symbol of authority before high-ranking Roman officials.</term>, and other public servants.

### XXVI.

<margin>Whether the office of the Duumvirs among the Romans was a magistracy.</margin>
There is great doubt regarding the Duumvirs <term>Duumviri|A pair of officials (literally "two men") who served as the chief magistrates of a Roman colony or town.</term>. For **Bodin** <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), a French jurist and political philosopher who defined modern sovereignty.</note> denies that these were magistrates. However, the affirmative view seems truer to me, even though the legal remedies granted to them—which are necessary for protecting and exercising jurisdiction—were quite restricted, as is clear from the law *Ea quae* in the *Digest* regarding municipalities.

### XXVII.

<margin>Whether the Senate was higher.</margin>
There is an even greater controversy regarding the Senate: was it a magistracy? There are those who deny it, among whom are **Carlo Sigonio**, **Coras**, **Bodin**, and **Cujas** <note>These were all giants of 16th-century legal scholarship: Carlo Sigonio, Jean de Coras, Jean Bodin, and Jacques Cujas.</note>—great names indeed! To us, however, their opinion does not seem entirely true. For **Tacitus** writes in *Book 13 of the Annals* that during the time of **Nero**, a certain "image of the Republic" <note>original: "imaginem Reipublicæ"</note> remained when the Senate prohibited the Tribunes from usurping the jurisdiction of the Praetors, or from summoning people from Italy against whom legal action could be taken, or from punishing people within their own homes by their own power <note>original: "intra domum pro potestate animadvertere"</note>. He writes of many other things that the Senate either prohibited or commanded.

### XXVIII.

<margin>Whether the deputy of a proconsul was a magistrate.</margin>
It is a matter of debate concerning the deputy <term>legatus|An appointed assistant to a high official, such as a provincial governor.</term> of a proconsul: was he a magistrate? Although **Pierre Fabre** <note>A 16th-century French jurist known for his work on the Rules of Law.</note> denies this in his commentary on law 70, *On the Rules of Law*, it is more correct to say that he was a magistrate—but not until his jurisdiction had been activated by the proconsul. For my opinion, I have the best authority in **Asconius Pedianus**, in his commentary on the third *Verrine Oration* <note>Asconius was a 1st-century scholar who wrote commentaries on the speeches of Cicero.</note>, where he twice calls a deputy a magistrate.

<keywords>magistrates, Duumviri, Senate, Proconsul, Legatus, Jean Bodin, Tacitus, jurisdiction, Roman law, legal status</keywords>

<header>On public law.</header>

<margin>Whether a Deputy sent from a Republic to a Prince [is a magistrate].</margin>

### XXIX.

A deputy of a Republic who is sent by his Republic to the Prince for the sake of public business is no more a magistrate than is an ambassador of nations <note>original: "legatus gentium"</note>, concerning whom Asconius <note>Asconius Pedianus (9 BCE – 76 CE), a Roman historian known for his commentaries on Cicero's speeches.</note> writes in his commentary on the third speech against Verres: *An orator sent with hostile intent added this, since he speaks of a magistrate, not a deputy.* This reading is correct, whereas that of Hotman <note>François Hotman (1524–1590), a prominent French legal scholar.</note>—which claims he speaks of a "magistrate's deputy"—must be rejected. For the words that immediately follow sufficiently indicate that Asconius wished to contrast an ambassador of nations with a magistrate, rather than a deputy of a magistrate. Add to this that Asconius had noted, not long before, that Verres, who was a deputy, had diminished his own magistracy.

<margin>Whether a deputy who promises payment in Rome for a debt contracted in a province before his embassy can be sued in Rome.</margin>

### XXX.

Nevertheless, a deputy of a Republic enjoys certain privileges, such as the right to be sued at home <term>revocandi domum|The legal privilege allowing a defendant to move a lawsuit from a foreign or imperial court back to their home jurisdiction.</term>, according to *Law 2 in the Digest on judgments*. However, a great question arises: if a deputy, while in Rome, promises to pay <gloss>constituens</gloss> a debt he contracted in a province before his embassy began, can he be sued in Rome? In *Law 8 of the Digest on judgments*, it says he cannot. This seems to be contradicted by *Law 5, paragraph 1 of the Digest on promised money*. But Cujas <note>Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), a leading figure of the legal humanist movement.</note> in *Observations, Book 13, Chapter 27* interprets this law to apply when someone promises in Rome that he will specifically pay in Rome. On the other hand, Pacius <note>Giulio Pace (1550–1635), a famous Italian jurist.</note> in *Antinomies 2, Chapter 21* reads the aforementioned *Law 5* in the negative: *it is agreed he ought not to be sued.* For my part, while I do not admit this correction of the text, I also consider the conjecture of the great Cujas to be tolerable, yet not necessary. For in that passage, Ulpian <note>A famous 3rd-century Roman jurist whose writings make up a large portion of the Digest.</note> is not defining the place where a deputy promising payment in Rome can be sued; he simply says he *can* be sued, which is true if you understand it as referring to his home province.

<margin>Ambassador of Nations</margin>

### XXXI.

A weightier question concerns an ambassador of nations <note>An international diplomat or foreign envoy.</note>: whether he is subject to the jurisdiction of the place where he himself off- <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "linquit" suggests the full word is "delinquit," meaning "commits an offense."</note>

<header>Disputation II.</header>

<margin>
Whether he acquires a legal venue in the place where he commits a crime.
</margin>
...commits a crime <note>original: "delinquit"</note>? In this matter, it seems a distinction can truly be made between crimes perpetrated against the state or the Prince to whom he was sent, and those by which only private individuals are harmed. In this latter case, I believe the ambassador should be sent back to his own master so that he may be punished with fitting penalties. But in the former case—because he violates and breaches the trust to which he is reciprocally bound—he should be treated (in the words of Livy regarding the ambassadors of Tarquin) as an enemy. I believe he should not be regarded as a sacred ambassador and must be punished. Therefore, it must be said that in previous years, the Spanish ambassador <note>Refers to Bernardino de Mendoza, who was expelled from England in 1584 for his involvement in the Throckmorton Plot to assassinate Elizabeth I.</note>, who had conspired against the life of the Queen of England and the safety of the realm, could have been executed, regardless of what Kirchner writes in *Book 2, Chapter 1, On Ambassadors* or the author of the second Dialogue titled *The French Morning-Alarm* <note>original: "Reveille matin des Francois," a famous 16th-century Huguenot political pamphlet.</note>.

### XXXII.

Just as magistrates in cities take care that nothing disturbs the public peace, others keep watch abroad so that nothing harms the Republic. And if any inconveniences threaten the Republic—whether by war or by a peace treaty being made or a league contracted—they must address them.

### XXXIII.

<margin>
War must be declared.
</margin>
Concerning the causes of war—which are drawn from that part of the law of nations <term>jus gentium|A body of laws or principles considered common to all people and nations, forming the foundation of international law.</term> that deals with offense and defense—I will now offer no debate. However, even if someone has a just cause for war, they do not act justly unless they declare the war. For only those are considered "enemies" <note>original: "hostes"</note> against whom we have publicly decreed war, or who have decreed it against us; all others are considered robbers or pirates. <note>Referencing the Digest of Roman Law: book 118 "On the Significance of Words" and book 24 "On Captives and Postliminy."</note> Cicero, in the first book of *On Duties*, says: *No war is held to be just unless it has been announced, unless it has been declared, unless restitution has been...* <note>original: "repetitis rebus," referring to the formal Roman demand for satisfaction or return of property before hostilities began.</note>
