<header>On Public Law.</header>

...[concerning these] matters. Nor was this only a rite of the Romans, but of other nations as well, so much so that God himself commands the declaration of war in *Deuteronomy 20*. From this I conclude that what Bartolomæus Facius <note>Bartolomæus Facius (c. 1400–1457) was an Italian humanist and diplomat known for his histories and biographies.</note> writes was superfluous: that in the peace treaty struck between the Genoese and King Alfonso, it was decreed that they should not wage war against each other without a formal declaration.

### XXXIV.

<margin>The declaration of war rests with the supreme Prince.</margin>
<margin>Between citizens it is not war, but sedition.</margin>

The power to declare war rests with the supreme Prince, and the *Julian Law on Treason* <note>original: "Lex Iulia majestatis," a foundational Roman law concerning crimes against the state or the sovereign.</note> condemns anyone who has waged war without the Prince's command. *See Vultejus on fiefs, page 84; Zoannettus on the Roman Empire; Gail on public peace, book 1, chapter 4, number 6 and following; Covarruvias on the chapter "Peccatum" concerning the rules of law in the 6th book, part 2, section 9, where many authors are named.* For this reason, it follows that what occurs between citizens of the same state is not "war" but <term>sedition: an internal uprising or civil unrest rather than a formal conflict between sovereign powers</term>. Consequently, the legal effects of war—such as the status of captives and the right to plunder—cease to apply between them. *See Doneau, book 4, commentary 21.* Although I am aware that Roman citizens captured by an opposing internal faction were often sold "under the spear" <note>original: "sub hasta," referring to the Roman custom of placing a spear in the ground to signal a public auction, often for war spoils or property.</note> and held in slavery.

### XXXV.

<margin>A declared war is not to be waged immediately.</margin>

The Romans were not accustomed to declaring war and launching an attack at the same time. This was most equitable, so that the enemy might deliberate whether they wished to yield or not. Let those wicked "politicians" be gone, then, who advise that an unprepared enemy should be overwhelmed. Regarding how long this period of delay lasted, it is clear from Livy <note>Titus Livius, the Roman historian.</note>, who writes that ambassadors were often sent ahead to demand restitution; friendship was renounced if the demands were not met; and finally, after thirty-three days had passed, war was declared. Oldendorpius <note>Johann Oldendorp (c. 1487–1567), a prominent German jurist.</note> indeed says, in his work *On Usucaption, in the chapter on time as it relates to the matter of war*, that war...

...between citizens. If a declaration has been announced three days before the conflict is joined, it is generally considered plainly honorable.

### XXXVI.

<margin>Things captured in war become the property of the captors.</margin>
It is customary that things captured in war become the property of those who take them <note>The author cites several sections of the *Digest*, a major part of the Body of Civil Law (Corpus Juris Civilis), specifically regarding the acquisition of ownership: *laws 5, 7, and 51 of book 41, title 1*.</note>. Nor do I distinguish between movable and immovable property—as many great authors do—for the established rule remains nonetheless true. However, when the Commander, having repelled and conquered the enemy, claims the land and the spoils, these then become public property <note>citing *Digest book 49, title 15, law 20, section 1* regarding captives and the return of rights after war.</note>. What private individuals capture becomes the property of those individuals; these are typically movable goods taken while the war is still ongoing and the enemy has not yet been fully defeated, as Donellus <note>Hugues Doneau (1527–1591), a French legal scholar known for applying systematic logic to Roman law.</note> well teaches in his *Commentaries, book 4, chapter 2*.

### XXXVII.

<margin>Whether the captivity of humans is tolerable among Christians.</margin>
Concerning the captivity of humans, the question is asked: is servitude tolerable among Christians? Truly, the most learned men, distinguished in law and the more refined doctrines, deny it. These include Alciatus <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), an Italian jurist and founder of the legal humanism school.</note> in his notes on the *Digest, book 50, title 16, law 239*, as well as Wesenbeck, Covarruvias, Duarenus, Donellus, and others. They argue this because wars waged between Christians are considered "more than civil wars" <note>This phrase suggests that because all Christians belong to one spiritual "body," war between them is like a family or internal conflict where the harsh laws of foreign enslavement should not apply.</note>. We, however, agree with several pious theologians that it is consistent with reason that those who could have been killed in the heat and fervor of battle may instead be subjected to a just and moderate servitude. From this, bondservants are called <term>mancipia: because they were "taken by hand" (manu capti) in war</term>. Nor should we rely on that etymology where "slaves" (*servi*) are said to be named from "preserving" (*servando*) <note>The traditional Roman etymology was that slaves were called *servi* because generals "preserved" them rather than killing them; the author rejects this for a different linguistic origin.</note>, even if a truer explanation is sought elsewhere, as Joseph Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a towering figure in Renaissance philology and history.</note> teaches in his notes on Varro.

### XXXVIII.

<margin>Whether servitude [is necessary] among Christians.</margin>
Nevertheless, I do not think that servitude is necessary, as Gregory <note>Likely Pierre Grégoire (1540–1597), author of *De Republica*, a significant work on political theory.</note> seems to suggest in his *Republic, book 1, chapter 13, number 12*. For experience te-

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

...the experience of many years has taught us that Christendom <note>original: "Christianam Rempublicam," often translated as the "Christian Commonwealth," referring to the collective body of European Christian states.</note> can function without slaves <note>original: "mancipiis," specifically referring to chattel property or those held in total ownership.</note>. For although it appears that slaves existed until the year 1200 (which is clear from a Canon of Innocent III, who presided over the Roman See around that time <note>A reference to the *Decretals* of Pope Innocent III regarding the marriage of slaves.</note>), it is nevertheless believable that the number of slaves had already greatly diminished by that period. Indeed, in his own time, the Emperor Leo <note>Leo VI "the Wise," Byzantine Emperor (reigned 886–912 AD).</note> had decreed that no one could contract to sell themselves into slavery, and that anyone who did so should be punished with lashes while keeping their freedom intact <note>citing the *Novels of Leo, 59* (New Laws).</note>. Furthermore, Bartolus <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato (1313–1357), one of the most influential medieval jurists.</note>, who flourished in the 1300s, writes in his commentary on the laws regarding captives <note>citing the *Digest*, book 49, title 15, law 24.</note> that in his age, no slaves existed at all.

### XXXIX.

<margin>On the <term>Right of Postliminium</term> <note>Postliminium is a Roman legal concept where a person who was captured by an enemy is restored to all their former rights and property upon returning to their own territory.</note>.</margin>
Just as captives are justly subjected to servitude in war, so it is consistent with natural equity that captives be granted the right of <term>Postliminium</term>, as defined in the laws concerning captives <note>citing *Digest* 49.15.19.</note>. This right of return is said to apply either in war or in peace. Regarding war, the matter is clear; however, in my judgment, a great difficulty exists regarding peace in the law <note>citing *Digest* 49.15.5.2.</note> where a place for captivity is left even in times of peace between peoples who are not enemies. Aelius Gallus, quoted in the works of Festus <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian who preserved older legal definitions.</note>, agrees with this opinion. This is despite the fact that it seems only the property of enemies can be acquired by us through capture <note>The author provides several citations to the *Institutes* and *Digest* regarding the acquisition of ownership through the laws of war.</note>. I am certainly not unaware that Alberico Gentili <note>1552–1608, a founding father of the science of international law.</note> attempts a reconciliation of these views in his first book *On the Law of War, chapter 12*, but whether he succeeded, I leave to the judgment of others. Jean Bodin <note>1530–1596, a French jurist and political philosopher.</note> says in his first book *On the Republic* that this right is not accepted today; but I ask: why was it ever accepted? Accursius <note>c. 1182–1263, a Roman jurist famous for his "Great Gloss" on the law.</note> understands this to apply to those against whom war has not been formally declared, but rather a "guerra" <note>An informal or private state of war/conflict as opposed to a formal public war.</note>

## XL.

If a soldier who has lost his weapons in war is punished with death <note>original: "capite punitur," literally "punished by the head," referring to capital punishment.</note>, it is much more reasonable that those who were conquered by force and surrendered themselves to the enemy should lose the right of <term>Postliminium</term> <note>The Roman legal fiction that a returning captive is treated as if they had never left, restoring their property and status.</note>. This is found in Law 17, *On Captives*. For as **Wesenbeck** <note>Matthaeus Wesenbeck (1531–1586), a prominent jurist of the 16th century.</note> says, they subject themselves to the enemy voluntarily. From this, **Silius Italicus** <note>A Roman epic poet (c. 26–101 AD) known for his work on the Second Punic War.</note> writes in his tenth book:

->*Indeed, it would surpass all crimes and every offense*<-
->*for an armed man to allow himself to be captured.*<-
<note>original: "Cuncta adeo scelera & noxam superaverit omnem / Armatum potuisse capi."</note>

To this, one might add **Horace**, in his third *Ode*.

## XLI.

For those captured in war, <term>Postliminium</term> exists at least during the duration of the war. Nor does Law 12, paragraph 1, *On Captives* overturn this opinion, if we—along with **Cujas** <note>Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), a French legal scholar famous for reconstructing the historical context of Roman law.</note> in his *Observations*, Book 24, chapter 31—assume an "elliptical speech" <note>An "elliptica oratio" refers to a sentence where some words are omitted but understood. The author suggests a specific interpretation of the legal text's grammar.</note>. The text that follows forces this interpretation, because the Romans wanted the hope of returning to the citizenry to depend more on martial valor <note>original: "virtute bellica."</note> than on a state of peace. This concept is specific to Roman virtue. Thus, Scipio Africanus, as cited in **Justin**, book 31: He never even discussed ransoming his son, nor did he permit the Senate to act on the matter; instead, as was worthy of his majesty, he said he would recover his son himself <note>Scipio preferred to win his son back through military victory rather than through diplomatic negotiation or payment.</note>.

## XLII.

<margin>Lands captured by enemies, if the enemy retreats into their own strongholds.</margin>
Regarding the lands which the enemies have seized, a doubt arises: if the enemy happens to retreat back to their own strongholds, do those lands return to their previous owners? I believe a distinction must be made. We should say that lands held by enemies, as if they were merely guests or travelers, are not considered to have changed their legal status—just as a flood does not destroy the essential form of a piece of property. But if those lands which they occupied...

<summary>This page discusses the legal penalties for surrender and the Roman concept of martial virtue, arguing that those who surrender willingly may lose their right to have their status restored. It also begins a legal distinction regarding whether occupied land reverts to its original owners when an enemy retreats.</summary>
<keywords>Postliminium, Roman Law, Surrender, Martial Valor, Budé, Cujas, Scipio Africanus, Occupied Land, Legal Property Status</keywords>

<sig>D 4</sig>

<margin>Whether [lands] return to their former owners.</margin>
...the enemies occupied <note>original: "occuparunt"</note> as victors and masters; these things are not recovered unless the enemies themselves are driven out again. This is established in *Law 20, On Captives* <note>original: "l. 20. de captiv." This refers to a specific section of the Digest, the Roman legal code.</note>. Therefore, it rightly seemed arrogant and unworthy to Hannibal that the very soil he possessed as a prize of war—having captured it in battle—had found a buyer in Rome, with the price not being lowered at all because of his occupation. This is attested by **Livy** <note>Titus Livius (64 BC–17 AD), a Roman historian.</note> in *Book 6 of the Third Decade*. He adds that this leader [Hannibal], immediately calling for a herald, ordered that the silver-smiths' shops, which were then around the Roman Forum, should be sold [in retaliation].

### XLIII.

There seems to have been a distinction between **movable property** <note>original: "res mobiles," such as goods, animals, or money.</note> and **immovable property** <note>original: "res immobiles," such as land or buildings.</note>. The former, if they were part of the plunder, did not return to their original legal status <note>original: "in pristinum statum," referring to the owner's original rights.</note> according to *Law 28, On Captives*. The latter, however, would return to their former owners in every case, per *Law 20* of the same title. **P. Faber** <note>Pierre Du Faur de Saint-Jory (1540–1600), a French jurist known for his meticulous style.</note>, who was accustomed to refine all his works with great care <note>original: "sub ascia polire," literally "to polish under the adze," a metaphor for careful craftsmanship.</note>, proves this elegantly in his *Semestria, Book 2, Chapter 2*. **Covarruvias** <note>Diego de Covarrubias (1512–1577), a Spanish jurist and canon lawyer.</note> and others cited by him move in a different direction in their commentary on the rule *Peccatum* <note>A famous rule in Canon Law regarding the restitution of stolen goods.</note>. However, lest anyone be swayed by certain passages from historians where examples are given of all plunder being returned to the original owners, we attribute those instances to the favor of the Generals and rewards for good citizens, rather than to the strict letter of the law. **Polybius** <note>A Greek historian of the Hellenistic period.</note> records that **Lucius Aemilius** restored the plunder taken from the defeated Gauls to those who had originally owned it. And Livy writes: *The plunder taken from the Volscians was all laid out in the Campus Martius, so that each person might recognize and take back his own.*

### XLIV.

Regarding the legal opinion on "The Deserter" <note>original: "l. transfugam," a specific law in the Digest concerning those who flee to the enemy.</note> in the section *On acquiring ownership of things*, a controversy not to be ignored was raised by a "great observer" <note>likely referring to Jacques Cujas, a famous legal humanist.</note> in *Book 4 of Observations and Emendations, Chapter 9*, and by his great disciple **P. Faber** in *Semestria, Book 2, Chapter 3*. It was decided by the authority of **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman orator and statesman.</note> that enemies who were never among us, [are to be treated] by us... <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "bis" indicates the next word on the following page is "nobis" (by us).</note>

### XLV.

**Bodin** <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), a French jurist and political philosopher.</note>, the pinnacle of political science, writes in *Book 1 of the Republic, Chapter 6*, regarding *Law 4, On Captives*, that the reading of the Florentine Code <note>The "Codex Florentinus," a famous 6th-century manuscript of the Roman Digest.</note> is inept, as can be sufficiently understood from **Cicero** in his *Topics*. We, on the contrary, shall argue otherwise, and we will not defend this criticism of Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman orator and statesman.</note> as being confirmed by his testimony.

### XLVI.

It is primarily consistent with equity <note>The principle of fairness and justice that supplements or overrides narrow legal rules.</note> that those captured in war can be ransomed. However, **ransom** <note>original: "redemptio"</note> provides the opportunity to return, but not the right of **postliminium** <note>original: "jus postliminii." This was a Roman legal doctrine where a person captured by the enemy regained their former status, rights, and property upon returning home.</note>, according to *Law 5 of the Code, under the same title*. I wonder, therefore, that the most learned **Wesenbeck** <note>Matthaeus Wesenbeck (1531–1586), a prominent jurist.</note> noted in his commentary on the *Digest, On Captives, number 9*, that those who are ransomed possess the right of postliminium. This opinion is sufficiently contradicted by the Title <note>original: "Rubrica," the red-ink heading of a legal chapter.</note> of both the *Digest* and the *Code*.

### XLVII.

<margin>Whether the victorious soldier [is entitled] to the ransom.</margin>
Captives ought not to be unfair toward just enemies who release them after a price has been accepted. Indeed, those who promised a **ransom** <note>original: "λύτρον" (lytron), the Greek word for the price paid for freedom.</note> upon their departure are obligated to return to fulfill the payment, according to *Law 21, along with Law 23, Digest, on business transacted*. This is truer than the opinion of **Bartolus** <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato (1313–1357), one of the most influential medieval jurists.</note>, by which he makes his students enemies of nature itself, while he instructs them to violate the faith given to an enemy. The Megarians and Corinthians, though pagans, acted better; they marked their own citizens—who had been dismissed by the enemy to bring back the ransom price—as treacherous, ungrateful, and unjust if they broke their word. *Plutarch is the author in "Greek Questions."*

It disputes contemporary legal scholars like Bodin and Wesenbeck, emphasizing that ransom is a private contract rather than an automatic restoration of citizenship rights.</summary>

-> ### XLVIII. <-

<margin>Withdrawing from the first agreements of captives.</margin>
In these agreements made with soldiers, it sometimes happens that after a price for ransom has been agreed upon, it is discovered that the captive possesses much greater wealth than was known. Hence it is asked: can the victor withdraw from the previous agreement <note>original: "pactione priori"</note> and increase the sum of the price in proportion to the riches of the person ransoming himself? It was decided in the Parliament of Grenoble <note>original: "Parlam. Gratianopolino"</note> in the year 1457 that the first agreements must be upheld. This is found in the work of **Guido Pape** <note>A celebrated 15th-century French jurist from Grenoble</note>, *Decision 113*, beginning "in the matter of wars" <note>original: "in facto guerrarum"</note>.

---

-> ### XLIX. <-

Following the order of the **Digest** <note>The *Digest* was the primary compilation of Roman legal precedents under Emperor Justinian</note> concerning military matters, one should deal with the persons, crimes, punishments, and discipline of soldiers; many such points could be drawn from books that show the way, such as **Obrecht** <note>Georg Obrecht (1547–1612), a professor of law at Strasbourg</note> in his *Treatise on Military Matters*, and **Zoannetus** <note>Petrus Zoannetus, a 16th-century Italian jurist</note> in his book *On the Roman Empire*. But we shall note the more refined points better suited to our training—namely, regarding public pacts: truces, peace, and treaties.

---

-> ### L. <-

<margin>Which agreements are to be called public.</margin>
Agreements are not to be called "public" simply because they are contracted by those who lead the Commonwealth <note>original: "Reipublicis"</note>. Rather, they are only those which concern the state of the Roman <term>res publica</term> <note>While the text says "Roman affairs," the author uses it as a general term for "the State" or "the public interest"</note> and are made for a public cause. Outside of this, if a **Sovereign** <note>original: "Princeps"</note> enters into a contract as a private individual, he is bound by it as a private person.

---

-> ### LI. <-

Stratagems and deceits which are carried out without <term>perfidy</term> <note>original: "perfidiam"; in a legal sense, this refers to the breaking of a sworn oath or a specific promise of safety</note> are useful in war; in all other matters, <term>religion</term> <note>In this context, *religio* refers to the moral and sacred obligation to keep one's word</note> must be observed. For all trickery and malicious interpretation ought to be absent from pacts, even if such an interpretation relies on some subtlety of civil law. For this reason, the Romans are deservedly blamed because they [promised] the state of the Carthaginians...

<header>Disputation II.</header>

...that they had agreed the state <note>original: "civitatem"</note> would be safe, they would not have destroyed their city <note>original: "urbem"</note>. For it is not credible that the Carthaginians had thought about that distinction between "city" (the physical structures) and "state" (the political community), which was in use among the Romans, as Cicero testifies in a fragment of *On the Republic*.

### LII.

<margin>Whether there is a place for poison in war.</margin>
Treachery is sometimes no less present in deeds than in words. Therefore, we leave no room for poisons in war, although **Baldus** <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400), a prominent Italian jurist</note>, out of an "Italian spirit," contends that an enemy can be killed with poison. Here I seek the ancient integrity of the Roman character. **Florus** <note>A Roman historian of the 2nd century AD</note> writes in Book 2: "He made the victory infamous by the unspeakable act of poisoning the springs. For against the divine law of the Gods and the customs of the ancestors, he violated the sacrosanct Roman arms with impure drugs." Certainly, there ought to be a defense in war, but against poison no one can protect themselves <note>referencing a principle in the Roman Digest regarding the impossibility of defending against secret poisons</note>.

### LIII.

<margin>Whether a people is bound by sworn pacts if led by the deceit of an adversary.</margin>
The question is asked whether a people is bound by sworn agreements which it entered into because it was misled by the deceit of an opponent. Some think so, based on the argument in the **Book of Joshua**, chapter 9 <note>referring to the biblical story where the Gibeonites tricked the Israelites into a peace treaty by pretending to be from a far-off land</note>. Indeed, **Hurault** <note>Michel Hurault, a 16th-century French political advisor</note> in *On the Office of State*, chapter 12, builds the argument from this that faith must be kept even with the treacherous. To us, the negative answer seems closer to the truth on both counts: look, there are other reasons why God punished the family of Saul in the **Second Book of Kings** <note>original: "2. Regum cap. 21"; in modern Bibles, this is 2 Samuel 21, describing a famine sent because Saul violated the Gibeonite treaty</note>, chapter 21. On this passage, one should consult **Peter Martyr Vermigli** <note>A leading Reformed theologian</note> and **Alberico Gentili** <note>An Italian-English jurist and a founder of international law</note> in *On the Law of War*, Book 3, chapter 19, and the author of *A Defense of Liberty Against Tyrants* <note>original: "Vindiciae contra Tyrannos," a famous 1579 political treatise arguing against tyranny</note>.

### LIV.

<margin>Whether military leaders can call for truces without the Prince's knowledge.</margin>
Truces are skillfully defined by **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, an ancient Roman polymath</note> as "holidays of war" <note>original: "belli feriae"</note> in the *Digest*, book 12, "On Captives." The question is further asked: can military commanders who have received their authority from a higher Prince contract truces without the knowledge of their Lord? And certainly regarding truces of a shorter duration—because for the purpose of burying the dea-

...it can be affirmed with confidence, based on the argument in Law 2 of the Digest regarding Jurisdiction. <margin>to contract.</margin> 
However, the ruling must be different concerning longer truces and peace; for a general is appointed for the conduct of war, not for its abandonment. And for this reason, the Romans surrendered **Postumius** to the Samnites, **Clodius** to the Corsicans, and **Hostilius** to the Numantines. <note>These Roman generals had made unauthorized peace treaties to save their armies; the Roman Senate, refusing to honor the deals, physically handed the generals over to the enemy to absolve the state of the religious and legal obligation.</note> Add to this the reasoning of **Crabotius** in Disputation 2, chapter 15, where he elegantly argues against **Duarenus** <note>François Douaren (1509–1559), a French legal humanist</note>, who holds a different opinion.

### LV.

<margin>A pact for a truce delays a war but does not remove it.</margin>
An agreement for a truce postpones a war rather than ending it; by it, hostilities are not so much abandoned as they are interrupted. From this, we think that **Alciatus** <note>Andrea Alciati (1492–1550), a famous Italian jurist and founder of the legal humanist school</note> wrongly attributed a certain town (called **Tignale**) to the **Bishop of Trent** in his legal opinion 17, when it had been agreed between the Bishop and the **Venetians** that each should possess what they held "before the most recent current war." 

The Bishop had in fact occupied the town during the war, and afterwards a truce had intervened; when this ended, a call to arms was made once again. <margin>A note on Alciatus's response.</margin> Alciatus interpreted these most recent acts as belonging to a "new" most recent war, denying that it was the same war that had been divided or delayed by the truce. If this were true, then a legal action suspended by a temporary delay <note>original: "dilatoria exceptio," a legal defense that delays a case without ending it</note> would no longer be the same action—which is false. 

However, I would not want these points to be accepted indiscriminately regarding any truce whatsoever. For very long truces seem to be counted as peace, as I gather from the Digest on captives <note>referencing Law 19, paragraph 1</note> regarding the phrase "for a short time." This opinion is confirmed by the author of *Meditations on Treaties*, chapter 3, number 39.

### LVI.

<margin>new cau-</margin>
The learned Doctors <note>original: "D d.," an abbreviation for *Doctores* of law</note> discuss whether, just as peace is said to be justly broken for a new cause, a **truce** <note>original: "Treuga"</note> can also be broken for a new cause? <margin>Whether a truce [can be broken] for a new cause.</margin>

<header>Disputation I.</header>

<margin>...whether it can be [violated].</margin> I do not agree with those Doctors of Law who conclude negatively for this reason: that the law is held more sacred during a truce than during peace. For if truces are more sacred (as **Bodin** says) and less subject to violation than peace itself, then those who violate a truce should be punished all the more severely. <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596) was a French jurist and political philosopher who argued for the sanctity of international agreements as a foundation of sovereignty.</note> Therefore, I embrace the opinion of **Gail** (Book 1 on Public Peace, chapter 4, number 45, and chapter 19, number 3). <note>Andreas Gail (1526–1587) was a leading judge in the Holy Roman Empire's supreme court; his works were standard references for legal procedure.</note> This is despite the fact that **Mynsinger** writes (in his *Observations*, Book 4, Observation 7) that the Imperial Court at Speyer approved a different view in the case of the **Count of Henneberg** against the **Dukes of Bavaria**. <note>Joachim Mynsinger von Frundeck (1514–1588) recorded the influential "Observations" or rulings of the Imperial Chamber Court, the highest judicial body in the Empire.</note>

### LVII.

<margin>A note on Bartolus's judgment regarding the observance of a truce.</margin> The judgment of **Bartolus** was far too harsh regarding the keeping of faith given to an enemy. <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato (1313–1357) was perhaps the most influential medieval jurist, known for his commentaries on Roman Law.</note> This is made manifest in the hypothetical case he presents in his commentary on Law 15 of the Digest regarding tax collectors (number 3), in these words: 

*“A truce was made between the King of France and the King of England, and a decree was issued: that the soldiers of the King of France could enter the Kingdom of England without penalty until Easter; however, after that time, it would be lawful for anyone finding a soldier of the other King to kill him. Now it happened that a certain soldier of the King of France went into England, and when he was returning by the English Channel before Easter, the sea held him back [by a storm] and drove him back into the Kingdom of England. The question is: can that man now be killed?”* 

Bartolus affirms that he can be, but with what face or under what pretext he does so, I do not see. For the argument he brings forward regarding the strict interpretation of the decree is futile.

### LVIII.

<margin>Regarding the observance of safe-conduct.</margin> But what if a safe-conduct (which is also a type of public pact) has been granted for the purpose of departing within a certain time, and someone's departure is delayed because of a bout of ill health or a storm? In this case, we also wish to avoid that "extreme law" which, according to **Cicero**, is "extreme injustice." <note>original: "summum jus, summa injuria"</note> <note>This famous legal maxim from Cicero’s *De Officiis* warns that a rigorous or literal application of the law, without considering equity or circumstances, often results in the greatest possible unfairness.</note>

<summary>The author argues for a more humane and flexible interpretation of truces and safe-conducts, refuting the harsh legalism of Bartolus by appealing to the principle that unforeseen circumstances (like storms or illness) should excuse technical violations of a deadline.</summary>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

...we are guided by law 15 *on public [laws]* and law 14 *on military affairs*. <note>Original: "de publ." and "de re milit." These refer to specific titles within the Corpus Iuris Civilis, the body of Roman law that formed the basis of legal education in the early modern period.</note> However, we do not support the cause of those who deceitfully extend a granted security to other matters which were likely not intended. We believe, therefore, that those women acted quite craftily when, having made a pact that they could leave with whatever they could carry away, they carried out their husbands—including **Welf, Duke of Bavaria**—on their shoulders while he was besieged by **Conrad III**. <note>This refers to the 1140 Siege of Weinsberg. According to legend, the "Loyal Wives of Weinsberg" saved their husbands by taking advantage of the Emperor's promise to let them leave with their most precious possessions.</note> Indeed, the Emperor was so moved by this witty act that he granted grace to all of them, even though he could have insisted on his legal right.

### L I X.

<margin>Whether military leaders, when making peace, can agree that no account shall be taken of the damages received on either side.</margin>
"Whenever kings act foolishly, it is the common people who pay the price." <note>Original: "Quidquid delirant Reges plectuntur Achivi," a famous line from the Roman poet Horace (*Epistles* 1.2.14) meaning that the subjects suffer for the mistakes of their leaders.</note> From this arises a rather elegant treatise: can military leaders, when negotiating peace, agree that no investigation shall be made into the damages suffered on either side? The Doctors of Law have split into different factions regarding this question, as is clear from **Bodin** (Book 1 of *The Republic*, chapter 9). <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596) was an influential French jurist and political philosopher who laid the groundwork for the modern concept of sovereignty.</note> We believe that such a pact by a Prince is valid if the common safety of the citizens cannot be ensured in any other way; with this moderation applied, we will easily allow private inconveniences to be swallowed up by the public utility.

### L X.

To this belongs the question treated by **Matteo d'Afflitto** (Naples Decision 150): whether the debtors of **King Alfonso**, who—after Alfonso had been put to flight—paid certain French soldiers on the orders of **Charles VIII, King of France** (whose brave service Charles had used), could be sued for payment again by Alfonso, who recovered his kingdom four months later? <note>Matteo d'Afflitto (1447–1523) was a prominent Neapolitan jurist. This case refers to the Italian War of 1494–1498, where Charles VIII of France briefly seized Naples from Alfonso II.</note> I will defend the negative view, along with **Hotman** and **Pierre Grégoire**, against the opinion of Matteo d'Afflitto. <note>François Hotman (1524–1590) and Pierre Grégoire (1540–1597) were major legal theorists who often argued for the stability of civil life despite changes in political power.</note>

<meta>catchword: Noble-</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the limits of legal pacts in wartime, using the famous "Loyal Wives of Weinsberg" as an example of clever interpretation, and argues that private claims for damages can be waived by leaders to achieve a lasting peace for the common good.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and political discussion regarding the conclusion of peace treaties, specifically addressing whether peace should be sought at any cost and the legal status of a sovereign who signs a treaty while held captive.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>Disputation I.</header>

### LXI.

<margin>Whether peace should be redeemed even at a great loss.</margin>
Peace is the most noble of agreements: for it is the end of war and is equally useful to the victor and the vanquished. As **Seneca** says, "it is expedient for the victor to wish for peace to be restored; for the vanquished, it is a necessity." <note>Seneca the Younger was a Roman Stoic philosopher; this sentiment reflects the practical reality that while the loser must submit, the winner also benefits from stability.</note> Nevertheless, the **Serene King of England** in his *Book on the Prince* <note>Referring to *Basilikon Doron*, written by King James I of England for his son.</note> and the author of the *Book on Treaties* (p. 198) believe that peace should not be redeemed at the cost of great personal loss or disgrace. For as **Cicero** says in his *7th Philippic*: *If we wish to enjoy peace, war must be waged; if we avoid war, we shall never enjoy peace.* On the other hand, we must not be so eager for war that we neglect the arts of peace, as noted by **Lanius** in his 9th Disputation. For this reason, the descendants of **Aeacus** <note>The Aeacidae, the mythological lineage of Achilles and Ajax, known for their martial prowess but often depicted as lacking political wisdom.</note> are said to have fared poorly because they were "more powerful in war than in wisdom," as an ancient author puts it. This same trait was criticized in the **Romans** by the greatest political thinkers, such as **Paolo Paruta** at length in his *1st Disputation* and **Contarini** in his *1st book on the Republic of Venice*. <note>Paruta and Gasparo Contarini were influential Venetian diplomats and historians who often contrasted Roman military expansion with the stable, diplomatic "myth of Venice."</note>

### LXII.

<margin>Whether a captive Prince making peace can claim "restitution to his former state."</margin>
The authority to establish war and peace lies with the Emperor (*New Constitutions 105, regarding consuls, in the preamble*). <note>The *Novellae Constitutiones* were the laws issued by Emperor Justinian after the publication of the main Code of Roman Law.</note> However, a question can be asked quite elegantly: can a Roman Prince who is a prisoner claim **restitution in integrum** <term>restitution in integrum: a legal remedy allowing a person to be restored to their original legal position, usually because a contract was signed under duress or fear</term> based on the edict regarding fear? We shall defend the negative position, contrary to several Doctors of Law who are "unskilled in the law of heralds" <note>The "law of heralds" (*ius foeciale*) refers to the ancient Roman religious and legal college responsible for declaring war and signing treaties, considered a precursor to modern international law.</note> (as **Bodin** calls them in *5. On the Republic 6.*). And although the Emperor cannot cause great detriment to the empire through his pacts (according to **Zoannetus** in *On the Roman Empire*), I do not release his person from the obligation.

### LXIII.

In former times, pacts were often made between military leaders so that he who was defeated in single combat would yield to the victor; but some theologians deny that such pacts are legitimate. Po-

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

...political writers, however, among whom are **Patrizi** and **Ciccarelli** in his *6th Disputation on Livy*, <note>Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597) and Antonio Ciccarelli were influential Renaissance thinkers who commented on the works of the Roman historian Livy to derive political lessons.</note> deny that such agreements are expedient for the Republic. Because of this, one might doubt which side to take. However, I am more inclined to agree with **Hotman**. <note>François Hotman (1524–1590), a French legal scholar. The author is likely referring to Hotman's views on the binding nature of legal contracts.</note> For as the saying goes: *Trust must not be given rashly, but once given, it must be kept.* <note>original: "Fides enim temerè danda non est, data autem servanda." This is a foundational principle of international law, emphasizing that while one should be careful when making promises, those promises are sacred once made.</note>

->LXIV.<-

<margin>*Three kinds of treaties.*</margin>
The most common example of a public agreement is found in treaties. **Pierre Fabre** <note>P. Faber (1540–1600) was a French jurist; his *Semestria* was a well-known legal commentary.</note> identifies three types of treaties from the works of legal experts and classical literature in his *Semestria, Book 1, Chapter 7*. The first occurs when peoples agree that one shall be under the protection and dominion of the other. The *second* type of treaty binds the allies to a relationship of "courteous respect." <note>original: "comem observantiam." This refers to a "free alliance" where one party acknowledges the superior dignity of another without actually losing its sovereignty.</note> The *third* type is established for the purpose of providing military aid or under other specific conditions.

->LXV.<-

Under the second category, we can include those agreements where a people places itself under the protection of a certain Prince without any loss of their own liberty. <note>This legal argument (arg. l. 7. D. capt.) suggests that "protection" does not automatically mean "subjugation."</note> This is discussed by **Gail** and **Bodin**. <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596) was a famous French political philosopher who defined the concept of sovereignty.</note> Nevertheless, less scrupulous Princes today—and in the past—often use the pretext of "protecting" a client city as an opportunity to seize it entirely. As **Cicero** wrote in a fragment of *On the Republic*: *By defending our allies, our people have now become masters of all lands.* <note>original: "Noster autem populus sociis defendendis terrarum jam omnium potitus est." Cicero is being somewhat ironic or critical here, noting that Rome’s empire grew by intervening in the wars of others under the guise of "protection."</note> Ciccarelli proves this with examples from Roman history in his *Discourses on Livy*.

->LXVI.<-

Furthermore, **Bodin** writes that "patrons" <note>In this context, a "patron" is a powerful state or ruler acting as a protector for a smaller "client" state.</note> ought to defend their clients against enemies without charging a fee. He believes that those who bargain for anything more than simple gratitude lose the title of "patron" or "protector" and instead become "mercenaries." However, this does not seem likely to me. For instance, a legal advocate <note>The author uses a play on the word "patron," which in Latin (*patronus*) can mean both a political protector and a courtroom lawyer.</note> is no less a patron simply because...

### LXVII.

<margin>*Whether we ought to bring aid to foreigners who are unjustly oppressed, even against our own allies.*</margin>
It can be discussed quite elegantly: ought we to bring aid to foreigners who are being unjustly oppressed, even if it means acting against our own allies? That pious man, **Philipp Melanchthon**, <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560) was a German reformer and a primary collaborator with Martin Luther; he wrote extensively on ethics and natural law.</note> raises a doubt about this in his *Ethics*, where he says that the case of a "just defense" is always excepted in treaties. He also brings up the example of the **Athenians**, who brought help to the Corcyraeans against their own allies, the Corinthians. However, the example of the **Romans** is perhaps safer. When the Campanians sought help against the Samnites, the Romans responded—according to **Livy**—as follows: 
<note>original: "Auxilio vos Campani dignos censet Senatus, sed ita vobiscum amicitiam institui par est, ne qua vetustior amicitia & societas violetur. Samnites nobiscum foedere juncti sunt: Itaque arma, Deos prius quam homines violatura, adversus Samnites vobis negamus. Legatos sicuti fas est, precatum ad socios mittemus, ne qua vobis vis fiat." This famous refusal highlights the Roman priority for older treaties over new requests for aid.</note>
> The Senate deems you Campanians worthy of aid, but it is proper that friendship be established with you in such a way that no older friendship and alliance is violated. The Samnites are joined to us by a treaty. Therefore, we refuse to take up arms against the Samnites—arms which would violate the Gods before they violate men. We shall, as is right, send ambassadors to our allies to request that no violence be done to you.

### LXVIII.

If someone has two allies who are waging war at the same time in different places, the distinction made by **Baldus** <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400) was a celebrated Italian jurist whose commentaries on civil and canon law remained authoritative for centuries.</note> can be applied: that the person, who is indivisible, is owed to whichever ally requested help first, while resources are owed to both. If, however, out of three allies joined together by treaties, one implores help against the violence brought by the third, we believe—following the Roman view—that the older ally ought to be helped. This is despite the fact that another view was held by **Alberico Gentili**, <note>Alberico Gentili (1552–1608) was an Italian jurist and one of the founders of the science of international law.</note> unless perhaps the treaty is of a more "sacred" type—for example, between people of the same nation or the same body against another ally. Thus, the **Helvetians** <note>The Helvetii refers to the Swiss Confederacy.</note> rightly support other Helvetians against any other allies.

<header>On Public Law.</header>

...they bring aid to those who are confirmed by treaty. For besides the treaty, by which they are most closely bound to each other, they also seem to constitute a single Republic, if we believe **Simler** rather than **Bodin**. <note>Josias Simler (1530–1576) was a Swiss scholar who argued the Swiss Confederacy formed a single state; Jean Bodin (1530–1596), a French jurist, argued that sovereignty cannot be divided, thus seeing such confederations as separate entities.</note>

### L X I X.

Now let us weigh the second branch of our discussion, namely, regarding matters of public law, such as the affairs of the treasury <term>fiscus; originally the Emperor's private purse, here referring to the state treasury</term>, which includes those things attributed to the treasury from penalties, privileges, tributes, and taxes. Likewise public roads, places, rivers, and buildings, as well as the walls and gates of the city: concerning each of these we shall pose questions.

### L X X.

<margin>Whether a penalty from a "popular action" is owed to the treasury.</margin>
The law of the treasury dictates that penalties exacted from a criminal action are granted to the treasury. But a difficulty arises in "popular actions" <term>actio popularis; a legal action brought by a private citizen to protect a public interest</term>: should the penalty be applied to the plaintiff <note>original: "actori"</note> or the treasury? Various authors feel differently here; I, along with **Baldus**, <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400) was an influential Italian jurist and expert in Roman and Canon law.</note> judge that as a rule it should be awarded entirely to the plaintiff, although *Law 25, final paragraph, of the Digest on the Silanian Decree* <note>The *Senatus Consultum Silanianum* (10 AD) was a Roman decree concerning the punishment of slaves if their master was murdered.</note> disturbs this opinion not a little. Regarding that law, it seems it should be said that it is believable that some unique circumstance lies hidden there. For it is established that property is otherwise confiscated in cases involving the *Silanian Decree* (Law 5, Digest of the same), but a fine of one hundred gold pieces <note>original: "centum aureorum"</note> is not usually inflicted.

### L X X.

<margin>Whether the property of the condemned or proscribed [is owed to the treasury].</margin>
Today, the property of condemned or proscribed persons <note>Proscribed persons were those declared outlaws whose property was traditionally forfeit to the state.</note> does not pass to the treasury, because political reason—as **Bodin** suggests in Book 5, chapter 3—persuades us that this is more useful for the Republic. This was introduced by the Emperor **Justinian** in his decree. <note>original: "Authen. bona. C. bonis proscript." This refers to a law in the Code of Justinian protecting the inheritance rights of the family of a condemned person.</note> In this law, however, **Irnerius** <note>Irnerius (c. 1050–1125) was a foundational figure in the medieval recovery of Roman law at the University of Bologna.</note> added these words from his own hand: *From the collateral line up to the third degree.* For nothing concerning collateral relatives was included in the *Novels* <term>Novellae; the 'New Constitutions' issued by Justinian</term> themselves. Thus, the authority of Irnerius is not beyond all exception, as others also find his reliability lacking in other matters...

# Disputation II.

->LXVII.<-

<margin>Whether we ought to help foreigners who are unjustly oppressed against our own allies.</margin> It can be discussed with some elegance: should we provide aid to foreigners who are being unjustly oppressed if the oppressors are our own allies? The pious man **Philipp Melanchthon** <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560) was a German reformer and scholar who wrote extensively on "Ethica" (Ethics), applying Christian morality to law and politics.</note> raises a doubt about this in his *Ethics*, where he states that an exception for just defense is always implied in treaties. He also brings up the example of the Athenians, who brought aid to the people of **Corcyra** <note>Modern-day Corfu.</note> against their own allies, the **Corinthians**. However, a safer example might be that of the **Romans**, who, according to the historian **Livy**, responded in this way to the people of **Campania** when they sought help against the **Samnites**: <note>original: "Auxilio vos Campani dignos censet Senatus, sed ita vobiscum amicitiam institui par est, ne qua vetustior amicitia & societas violetur. Samnites nobiscum foedere juncti sunt: Itaque arma, Deos prius quam homines violatura, adversus Samnites vobis negamus. Legatos sicuti fas est, precatum ad socios mittemus, ne qua vobis vis fiat."</note> *“The Senate deems you, Campanians, worthy of aid; but it is right that friendship be established with you in such a way that no older friendship and alliance <term>societas; a formal political association or partnership</term> is violated. The Samnites are joined to us by treaty <term>foedus; a solemn agreement or league</term>: Therefore we deny you arms against the Samnites, for such an act would violate the Gods even before it violates men. We shall send ambassadors to our allies, as is right, to pray that no violence be done to you.”*

->LXVIII.<-

If someone has two allies who are waging war in different places at the same time, the distinction made by the jurist **Baldus** <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400) was an influential Italian expert in Roman and Canon law.</note> may be applied: namely, that the person themselves—who is a single individual—is owed to the first ally in terms of time, but their resources <note>original: "res vero utræque"</note> are owed to both. If, however, one of three mutually allied parties implores the help of another against violence brought by the third ally, we believe, following the **Roman** example, that the older ally ought to be helped. This is our view, although **Alberico Gentili** <note>Alberico Gentili (1552–1608) was a professor of civil law at Oxford and is regarded as one of the founders of the science of international law.</note> held a different opinion; unless, perhaps, the alliance is of a more "sacred" nature—for instance, an alliance of one people or one body against another outside ally. Thus, the **Swiss** <note>original: "Helvetii"</note> rightly help other **Swiss** against their other external allies.

### LXIX.

Now let us examine the second part of our discussion, namely concerning matters of public law, such as the assets of the <term>fiscus; the state treasury or public purse</term>. This includes things attributed to the treasury from penalties, privileges, tributes, and taxes. Likewise, we consider public roads, places, rivers, and buildings, as well as the walls and gates of the city: about each of which we shall pose questions.

### LXX.

<margin>Whether a penalty from a popular action is owed to the treasury.</margin>
The law of the treasury dictates that penalties exacted from a criminal action are granted to the treasury. But there is a difficulty regarding <term>popular actions; legal actions that any citizen could bring on behalf of the public interest</term>: should the penalty be applied to the plaintiff or the treasury? Various authors feel differently here; I, along with **Baldus** <note>Baldus de Ubaldis (1327–1400) was a renowned Italian jurist and authority on Roman and Canon law.</note>, believe that as a rule, it should be awarded entirely to the plaintiff, although Law 25, the final paragraph of the Digest on the **Syllanian Decree of the Senate** <note>The *Senatus Consultum Syllanianum* (10 AD) was a Roman law decreeing that if a master was murdered in his home, all his slaves were to be tortured and executed.</note> causes no small amount of confusion here. Regarding this, it seems necessary to say that it is credible that some unique circumstance lies hidden there. For it is established that goods are otherwise confiscated in the case of the Syllanian Decree (*Law 5 of the same Digest title*), but a penalty of one hundred <term>aurei; Roman gold coins</term> is not actually inflicted.

### LXX.

<margin>Whether the goods of those condemned or proscribed [belong to the treasury].</margin>
Today, the goods of those condemned or <term>proscribed; individuals declared outlaws whose property could be seized</term> do not go to the treasury, because political reasoning suggests this is more useful for the Republic, as **Bodin** argues in Book 5, Chapter 3. Moreover, the Emperor **Justin** introduced this via his sanction <note>Referring to the *Authenticum* (a collection of Emperor Justinian's new laws), specifically the section on the property of those proscribed.</note>. However, in this law, **Irnerius** <note>Irnerius (c. 1050–c. 1125) was an influential jurist in Bologna who helped revive the study of Roman law in the Middle Ages.</note> added these words from his own mind: *“From the side up to the third degree”* <note>original: "Ex latere usque ad tertium gradum"</note>. For nothing regarding <term>collaterals; relatives not in a direct line of descent, such as siblings or cousins</term> was included in the <term>Novel; a "Novella," or new law, issued by a late Roman emperor</term> itself. Thus, the authority of Irnerius is not beyond all exception; indeed, others have found his reliability wanting in other matters as well.

Whether a penalty from a public action is owed to the treasury.

Whether the goods of those condemned or proscribed [go to the treasury].

## LXXII.

<margin>
The privilege of the Treasury in a mortgage.
</margin>
The Treasury is preferred in a mortgage <term>hypotheca — a legal claim or security over property</term> because of a privilege <note>original: *privilegium*</note>, a very fine example of which is found in Law 28 of the Digest, *On the Right of the Treasury*. I follow Accursius <note>Accursius (c. 1182–1263) was a medieval Italian jurist famous for his "Great Gloss" on Roman law.</note> in interpreting this to mean that a contract with the Treasury takes precedence over a private contract. This is contrary to the view of Donellus <note>Hugo Donellus (1527–1591), a French legal scholar known for systematic legal theory.</note> in his treatise *On Pledges* (who refers the word "Contraxerit" <note>original: *contraxerit* — "will have contracted"</note> not to future time, but to past time) and Hotman <note>François Hotman (1524–1590), a French jurist and humanist.</note> who, supporting the same opinion, replaces "Contraxerit" with "Contraxerunt." Both are wrong: For what would be the privilege of the Treasury if we were to imagine that the Treasury’s contract was already prior in time?

## LXXIII.

<margin>
How taxes and tributes differ.
</margin>
Taxes <note>original: *vectigalia* — typically indirect taxes like customs duties or tolls</note> and tributes <note>original: *tributa* — typically direct taxes on individuals or land</note> are paid into the Treasury. Perenotus (whose untimely death the Muses still mourn) demonstrates the difference between them in Book 2, the final chapter of his *Animadversions*, where he defines the tax as ordinary and the tribute as extraordinary. The utility of this distinction is seen in Cujas <note>Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), the leading figure of the legal humanist movement.</note>, *Observations* 2, 53. Indeed, Bodin <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), the French political philosopher discussed on the previous page.</note> elegantly notes that under Roman laws, no taxes were imposed on those things which are transported for living more comfortably and honorably, but only on those things which serve luxury. This can be seen in Ulpian <note>A famous 3rd-century Roman jurist.</note> in Law *Interdum*, Section *species*, *On Tax Collectors*.

## LXXIV.

The Deified Brothers <note>The joint Roman Emperors Marcus Aurelius and Lucius Verus.</note> willed that half of a treasure <note>original: *thesaurus* — refers to ancient buried money or valuables whose owner is unknown</note> found in a place belonging to the Emperor [Caesar] or in a religious place should go to the Treasury (Law 3, Section *si in locis*, Digest, *On the Right of the Treasury*). However, that most debated Section, *thesaurus*, in the *Institutes on the Division of Things*, is contrary to this. From these and other passages, it is not inaptly concluded that [treasure] was always divided...

<summary>This page discusses the legal priorities of the state treasury in debt recovery, the definitions of various tax types, and the laws governing found treasure.</summary>
<keywords>treasury (fiscus), mortgage (hypotheca), taxes (vectigalia), tributes (tributa), found treasure (thesaurus), legal privilege, Roman law, Jean Bodin</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal discussion regarding the administration of the state treasury (fiscus) and transitions into the regulations governing construction on public property and the limits of sovereign authority over public lands.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>On Public Law. 91</header>

...citing the Emperor and the various intentions in the reports of crimes <term>delationibus — reports or denunciations of legal infractions</term> by which the treasury is enriched. However, the decision of the Divine Brothers <note>original: *Divorum fratrum* — referring to the joint Roman Emperors Marcus Aurelius and Lucius Verus</note> seems more acceptable, both because it is more recent and because it was approved by a constitution of Frederick <note>Likely Holy Roman Emperor Frederick I (Barbarossa) or Frederick II, whose medieval decrees were integrated into Roman law study</note> and has been accepted by custom in many places, such as France, as Cujas <note>Jacques Cujas (1522–1590), a preeminent French legal scholar known for applying historical analysis to Roman law</note> testifies.

### LXXV.

<margin>*Whether a private individual may build in a public place without the Prince’s permission.*</margin>
The question is whether a private person can build in a public space without the Prince’s permission. We are more inclined to affirm this with Donellus <note>Hugo Donellus (1527–1591), a French jurist famous for his systematic approach to law</note> (Book 4 of his *Commentaries*, chapter 9) and Sixtinus (*On Royal Rights*, chapter 5). In this context, the word "Especially" is understood (following Accursius <note>The author of the standard medieval commentary on Roman law</note>) to mean *especially when it is without public inconvenience*. The legal expert <term>IC. — Iurisconsultus, a legal authority or counselor</term> responds: the Prince's license is only necessary in that specific case where something is done that causes public inconvenience.

### LXXVI.

<margin>*Whether the Prince can permit it if it causes inconvenience to someone.*</margin>
From this, a further question blossoms: can the Prince permit buildings in a public place if it results in someone's inconvenience? Law 2, section 16 of the Digest, *Lest anything be done in a public place*, is applied to this question. Accursius understands this as granting permission for cases of "minor prejudice" <note>original: *modici præjudicii* — slight legal harm or disadvantage to a neighbor's rights</note>. However, Pinellus <note>Arius Pinellus, a 16th-century Portuguese jurist</note> (in his *Treatise on the Rescinding of Sales*, page 29) interprets this law to mean that the Prince grants such permission involving another's inconvenience only for a just cause. For our part, we deny that it is absurd for the Prince—who is the lord of the public place—to have this power, since even private individuals, as the masters and arbiters of their own property, may build even to the inconvenience of their neighbors (following the logic of Laws 9 and 11 of the Digest, *On Urban Servitudes*).

### LXXVII.

However, it is not the case that just as a building placed in a public spot becomes the property of the builder, so too does something constructed in a public river become the property of its author; I hold this even though I acknowledge I have many who disagree with me on this point.

<meta>catchword: De (Concerning)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the legality of private construction on public land, concluding that while individuals may build if it causes no public harm, the Prince holds the ultimate authority to permit construction even when it creates minor inconveniences for others.</summary>
<keywords>public law, building rights, Prince's permission, public inconvenience, urban servitudes, treasury reports, Cujas, Donellus, Accursius</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on city walls and municipal law, following the previous section on public construction and the rights of the treasury. It shifts from the physical nature of walls to their legal status and the consequences of their destruction.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>Disputation II.</header>

### LXXVIII.

Something must be added regarding walls, and with these points our dissertation is to be concluded: These walls are held to be **sacred** <note>original: *sancti* — In Roman law, city walls were *res sanctae* (holy things), placed under divine protection; to damage them was considered a capital offense</note>, and if anyone violates them, he is **punished by death** <note>original: *capite punitur* — literally "punished by the head," referring to capital punishment</note>. However, it is doubted whether this also applies in municipalities <note>independent towns or local jurisdictions</note>? We believe it does, according to *Law 8 at the end of the Code on the division of things* <note>original: *l. 8. in fine C. rer. divis.* — a reference to the Code of Justinian</note>, although certain famous Doctors <term>Doctores — high-ranking legal scholars and professors</term>, namely **Boetius**, **Ruinus**, **Menochius**, and **Corasius** <note>These were influential jurists of the 15th and 16th centuries, such as Giacomo Menochio and Jean de Coras, who specialized in civil and Roman law</note>, are authors of a different opinion.

### LXXIX.

Just as the ways in which cities commit offenses and are punished have been explained by philologists, it is worthwhile for us to consider whether the **privileges of a city perish** if its walls are leveled to the ground? I believe a distinction is necessary here: if the walls collapsed due to the **fury of an enemy** and by being captured in war, this should occur without the loss of privileges. But if they were torn down because of a **crime committed by the citizens**, the privileges fall along with the walls; furthermore, these privileges are not restored by the simple rebuilding of those walls. **Peter Gregory** <note>Petrus Gregorius (1540–1597), a French jurist known for his work *De Republica*</note> disagrees in *Book 2 of On the Republic*.

->*And let it suffice to have gathered here these few flowers*<-
->*concerning the law of the state, which I was able to pick*<-
->*summarily in this shortness of available time.*<-

<summary>This page concludes the legal dissertation by discussing the sacred status of city walls and the legal survival of a city's privileges after the physical destruction of its fortifications. The author argues that privileges are lost only if the walls are destroyed as a punishment for the citizens' crimes, rather than by enemy action.</summary>

<page-num>93</page-num>

# SUPPLEMENTARY PROPOSITIONS ON PRI-
# VATE, CIVIL, AND CANON LAW.
<note>original: *Parerga ex iure privato, civili et canonico* — *Parerga* refers to "side-works" or supplementary arguments added to a main dissertation</note>

1. *Natural law was not defined by Ulpian.* <note>**Ulpian** was one of the most influential Roman jurists; the author here disputes Ulpian's famous definition of natural law as that which "nature teaches all animals"</note>
2. *The distinction of a general agent with or without free administration belongs to the Doctors and the Canons, not to civil law.* <term>Doctorum — high-ranking legal scholars</term> <note>original: *procuratoris generalis cum libera vel sine* — This refers to the scope of power given to a legal representative; the author argues this specific distinction is a medieval or ecclesiastical invention rather than an original Roman one</note>
3. *A contract of antichresis cannot be established in such a way that the legal interest is exceeded, contrary to almost all modern writers.* <term>Antichresis — a legal agreement where a debtor gives the use of land or property to a creditor in place of paying interest</term> <note>original: *interusurium* — the interest or profit derived from a loan</note>
4. *The Publician action is not abolished by pontifical law, which requires good faith in prescription perpetually.* <term>Publiciana — a legal remedy protecting someone who has lost possession of property before the time required to become the legal owner (usucapion) has passed</term> <note>original: *jure pontificio* — Canon or Church law. In Roman law, "good faith" was often only required at the start of a period of possession, but Church law demanded it continue throughout the entire duration</note>
5. *Good faith is required in vain in the prescription of servitudes.* <term>Servitus — a "servitude" or easement, such as a right of way over a neighbor's land</term>
6. *The statute concerning the gaining of a dowry does not favor a bridegroom by present consent.* <note>original: *sponso de præsenti* — likely referring to *matrimonium per verba de præsenti*, a marriage contracted by a declaration of current intent rather than a future promise</note>
7. *A legal representative for litigation cannot be appointed if he is under 25 years of age.* <note>original: *Procurator ad lites* — an attorney or person designated to manage a court case. In Roman law, 25 was the standard age of full legal majority</note>
8. *Infamy is not branded by the law itself upon those demanding wicked interest.* <term>Infamia — a formal loss of legal standing or reputation</term> <note>original: *improbum fœnus* — excessive or "wicked" usury. The author argues that the status of *infamia* is not automatic (*ipso jure*) but perhaps requires a specific ruling</note>
9. *Hunting on another's land is not a servitude, but another type of right.*
10. *A pledge, understood in a general sense, is neither a contract nor an agreement.* <note>original: *Pignus in genere sumptum*</note>

<summary>This page lists ten "parerga" or supplementary legal theses that contrast classical Roman law with medieval Canon law and the opinions of legal scholars. The propositions cover a wide range of private law topics, including natural law, the powers of legal agents, usury, and property rights.</summary>

### X.

It is asked, however, whether by this very act the empire was transferred to foreigners? No. <note>The author argues that while the leadership changed, the legal identity of the "Roman Empire" remained continuous rather than becoming a foreign entity.</note>

### XI.

<margin>Whether the Roman Empire is Aristocratic.</margin>
Whether the German Empire is Aristocratic, or indeed Monarchic, is a matter of great debate. *a* I do not make this my own dispute, but leave it to others. <note>This was a sensitive 17th-century debate: did power lie with the Emperor (Monarchy) or the collective Electors and Princes (Aristocracy)?</note>

*a* See Kirchner, *Political Disputation 3*, final thesis, and Disputation 5, thesis 1, letter c. Johann Gerhard, *Political Disputations 1*, thesis 9. Schonborner, book 1, chapter 16. Arnold Clapmar, book 5, *On the Secrets of Republics*, final chapter, and thesis 73, *On Public Law*.

### XII.

<margin>Whether Ulpian correctly says that public law consists in sacred rites, priests, and the magistrate.</margin>
Ulpian *a* says that public law consists in sacred rites, in priests, *b* and in magistrates. *b* [He says this] too narrowly. For public law consists in all things which pertain to the Commonwealth <term>Respublica<gloss>the state or public interest</gloss></term> by their own nature, or on account of something else. *c* By nature, persons and things pertain to the Commonwealth. Persons are the Magistrates and the subjects.

### XIII.

<margin>To which laws the Prince is unbound.</margin>
A magistrate is either supreme *e* or subordinate. *f* The supreme magistrate is the **Roman Prince** <note>In this context, the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> whom Ulpian <note>A famous Roman jurist (c. 170–223 AD). His statement "The Prince is not bound by the laws" became a cornerstone for theories of absolute monarchy.</note> writes is **unbound by the laws.** *g* However, it is asked to which laws this should be understood to apply: I understand it to refer to **merely positive laws**, <note>Laws created by humans or governments (statutes), rather than "natural" laws that are inherent to humanity.</note> and not indeed to **divine, natural, and international laws.** *h*

*e* Who is improperly called a magistrate. *Matthias Stephanus, book 2. on jurisdiction, part 1. chapter 1. number 1. & following. Scipio Gentilis, book 2. on jurisdiction, chapter 26. & book 3. chapter 18. Hermann Kirchner, political disputation 2. thesis 1. letter a.* <note>The author is noting a technical debate: some jurists argued the Emperor is "above" the rank of a magistrate.</note>

*f Lambertus Danaeus, book 6. Christian Politics, chapter 2. g in law "the prince," 31. Digest, on Laws. h Vultejus in the section "but also what to the Prince." Institutes, on natural, international, and civil law. Fachineus, 1. controversy, chapter 2. Master Gottfried Muller, disputation 2. thesis 1. Matthias Cuno in the treatise on pacts, chapter 24. number 5.* Where from this he concludes that the Prince is **held to keep his own contract.** However, *Cujacius, 15. observations 30.* feels otherwise; *Donellus, 1. commentary 17.* feels otherwise; and *Corasius, 2. miscellany 4. & 5.* feels otherwise. The schools of the Greek interpreters feel otherwise, as does *Gothofredus in the said law 31.* <note>This long list of citations highlights a major legal conflict: even if the Emperor is "above the law," is he still required to honor private contracts and treaties? The author argues "yes," based on natural law.</note>

### XIV.

<margin>Rights of the supreme magistrate.</margin>
The rights *b* of the supreme magistrate *a* are generally these: To establish laws, and to interpret or abolish them; *c* to declare and end wars; *d* to appoint magistrates; *e* to found universities; *f* to create Doctors, Licentiates, Masters, etc.; *g* to mint coins; *h* to demand taxes and tributes; *i* to hold the power of life and death, and the right of final appeal, etc. *k*

*a* Whether it be a Monarchy or a Polyarchy <note>original: *Polyarchicus* — A government where power is shared by many, such as an aristocracy or a republic.</note>. For as far as the rights of command are concerned, the types of Republics are not distinguished. *Friedrich Tileman, part 1, Disputation on the Pandects 2, thesis 8.* *b* These are called rights of majesty, regalia <note>original: *regalia* — specific royal prerogatives or "crown rights" that belong to the sovereign</note>, or rights of empire. *Doctors of Law in the single chapter "which things are regalia." Arnold Clapmarius, book 1, on the Secrets of Republics, chapter 10.* *c* *Law 1, 3, 8 and the last law, Code, on laws.* <note>The "Code" refers to the *Codex Justinianus*, part of the Corpus Juris Civilis.</note> Even a lower magistrate can establish a local law (which they call a statute, *chapter "as souls," 2. on constitutions in the Sixth Book* <note>original: *in 6.* — referring to the *Liber Sextus*, a collection of canon law</note>). *See Bartolus in Law "all," 9. numbers 7 and 8, Digest, on justice and law. Wesenbeck on the title of the Digest, on laws, number 3. Borcholten, chapter 5, on feuds, number 37. Andreas Gail, book 1, on Public Peace, chapter 6, number 10.* A decree of the Prince <note>original: *Interlocutio Principis* — a preliminary or intermediate legal judgment</note> does not have the force of law, unless the contrary will of the Prince is expressly established. *Julius Pacius, 1. Antinomies 17.* *Franciscus Duarenus* dissents *on the title of the Digest, on the constitutions of princes.* See also *Menochius, book 5, counsel 412, number 39.* *Bartolus* teaches in *Law Plautius 9, number 5, Digest, on bequeathed gold and silver,* that a Prince’s rescript <note>original: *Rescriptum Principis* — a formal written answer from the ruler to a legal query</note> (which has the force of law, *Law 1, Digest, on the constitutions of princes*) can be invalidated by an evident grammatical error. This is also handed down regarding the Pope’s rescript in the *chapter "to the audience," Extra, on rescripts* <note>original: *ext. de rescript.* — referring to the *Decretals of Gregory IX*</note>; but *Lord Scipio Gentilis, chapter 4, on the errors of witnesses,* says that this was handed down pretentiously, since most writings of the popes [overflow with] solecisms <note>original: *solœcismis* — grammatical mistakes</note> and barbarisms...

->XV.<-

<margin>To which magistrate jurisdiction belongs.</margin>
Jurisdiction belongs to both the supreme magistrate<a> and the inferior magistrate,<b> which, in a general sense, is the understanding of civil and criminal cases, along with the power of decreeing and executing.<b>

<a> See *Obrecht, chapter 6, on jurisdiction, number 39.* <b> Not only does jurisdiction "specially so-called" belong to the inferior magistrate, but today—though it was formerly considered otherwise according to *Alciat, 2. Paradoxes 6; Scipio Gentilis, book 3, on jurisdiction, chapter 3*—even <term>mere empire<gloss>merum imperium; the highest legal authority, traditionally including the power to impose the death penalty</gloss></term> belongs to them; see *Kirchner, political disputation 8, thesis 1, letter B.* (Matthias Stephan disagrees, *book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 11, number 10 and following.*) <c> It is so named from "stating the law" <note>original: *a jure dicundo* — the Latin etymological root of the word 'jurisdiction'</note>, *based on the argument of law 12, Digest, on common and pupillary substitution; Wesenbeck on the title of the Digest, on jurisdiction, and in the Economy, book 2 of the Digest; Obrecht, chapter 1, on jurisdiction, number 6.* (The Gloss <note>The 'Gloss' refers to the standard medieval commentary on Roman law.</note> disagrees on the word "mixed," as does *Bartolus, number 3, on law 3, Digest, on jurisdiction; Zasius in the Rubric of the Digest, same title, number 1.*) <d> See *Scipio Gentilis, book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 1.* For other definitions, see *Bocer, class 5, disputation 10, thesis 2; Matthias Stephan, book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 2, number 32.*

->XVI.<-

<margin>What jurisdiction is.</margin>
Jurisdiction is either jurisdiction "specially so-called"<a> or <term>mere empire<gloss>the authority to punish crimes</gloss></term>.<b> Jurisdiction "specially so-called" is the understanding of civil cases, to which <term>mixed empire<gloss>imperium mixtum; the power to enforce civil rulings, less than the power of capital punishment</gloss></term> is attached.<c>

<header>On Public Law.</header>

<a> For the term "jurisdiction" is used in the place of both a general category <note>original: "generis"</note> and a specific type <note>original: "speciei"</note>. *Johannes Ramus, book 2, Handbook of Legal Rules, axiom 154.* <b> Scipio Gentilis <note>A famous 16th-century legal scholar and professor at Altdorf</note> teaches that **mere empire** <note>original: "merum imperium"</note> is a species of jurisdiction understood in its general sense. *Scipio Gentilis, book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 3.* Obrecht disagrees, *chapter 2, on jurisdiction, number 13.* Therefore, when a castle is granted as a <term>fief<gloss>feudum; land or property held from a lord in exchange for service</gloss></term> along with every kind of jurisdiction, it is also deemed that **mere empire** has been granted. *Lord Scipio Gentilis, book 3, on jurisdiction, 23; Vultejus, on fiefs, book 1, chapter 5, number [omitted].* Baldus disagrees in *chapter "Quanto," number 5, at the end, [on the title] outside the court.* However, under a general grant of every kind of jurisdiction, <term>regal rights<gloss>regalia; exclusive rights belonging to the sovereign, such as minting coins or tax collection</gloss></term> are not included. *Matthias Stephan, book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 2, number 7.* And likewise, the Lord **Gregorius Ruprecht the elder**, Jurist-Consult—my parent of pious memory—responded as counsel in the case of *S. against H.* Furthermore, other Doctors of law add a third species here, that is, **mixed empire** <note>original: "mixtum imperium"</note>, but they do so erroneously. For mixed empire is the same as jurisdiction, or certainly so closely tied to jurisdiction that it cannot be torn away from it; and whatever belongs to jurisdiction also belongs to mixed empire, and vice versa. *Cujas, 21, observation 30; Scipio Gentilis, book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 2.* Nevertheless, I do not deny at all that in certain cases "empire" <note>the raw power of the magistrate</note> is more visible and "jurisdiction" <note>the formal administration of law</note> is almost hidden; for this reason, they are said to be more of empire than of jurisdiction in *Law 4 of the Digest, on jurisdiction.* Neither Bartolus, in his commentary on that Law 4, nor Matthias Stephan, *book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 5, number 58,* have yet persuaded me that the word **more** <note>original: "magis"</note> is used there in an elective sense. See *Scipio Gentilis, book 1, on jurisdiction, chapter 24; Thomas Michael, conclusion 92, on jurisdiction, letter C; Digest, book 3, on jurisdiction.*
