<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

[ju]risdiction, specifically so-called, can be mandated <note>delegated to a subordinate</note>, as proven by **Law 1 of the Digest, "On the office of him to whom jurisdiction is mandated"** <note>original: "l. 1. ff. de off. ejus cui mand. est jurisd."</note>; **Covarruvias**, *3. Various Resolutions, chapter 20*; **Scipio Gentilis**, *book 2, On Jurisdiction, 24.* However, **pure sovereignty** <note>original: "merum imperium"; the highest level of judicial power, including the authority to impose the death penalty</note> can be mandated neither by **Civil Law** <note>Roman Law</note> (*Law 1 of the Digest, On the office of him to whom jurisdiction is mandated; Law "No one can" 70, Digest, On the rules of law; Law "They are accustomed" also 6, Digest, On the office of the proconsul*), nor by **Canon Law** <note>Church Law</note> (*argument from the chapter "if for weakness," at the end, Extra, On the office of the legate; chapter 1, Clementines, On heretics*), nor even by our own **customs** <note>original: "moribus nostris"</note> (**Zasius**, *on Law 3, Digest, On the office of him, etc.*). **Wesenbeck** dissents (*at the said title of the Digest, On the office of him, etc., number 4, and at the title of the Digest, On jurisdiction, number 11*). It cannot be mandated even if the person who holds the pure sovereignty suffers from poor health (**Matthias Stephanus**, *book 1, On Jurisdiction, chapter 18*; **Lord Doctor Scipio Gentilis**, *book 3, On Jurisdiction, chapters 28 and 29*). I ask whether the **extension of jurisdiction** <note>original: "prorogatio jurisdictionis"; when parties agree to submit to a judge who otherwise lacks authority over them</note> is rightly granted to a legate? I affirm it (*Law 1 and the gloss there, Digest, On trials*). **Azo** dissents (*on Law 1, number 7, Code, On jurisdiction*), as do **Cynus** (*on Law "if anyone in consent," number 15, Code, On the bishop's court*), **Zasius** (*on Law 15, Digest, On jurisdiction*), and the **Imperial Chamber Court** <note>original: "Camer. p. 2. titul. 4. §. zum vierdten"; the supreme court of the Holy Roman Empire</note>; **Mynsinger**, *2. observation 52*; **Andreas Gail**, *1. observation, number 3.*

### XIX.

<margin>
Whether interdicts are available for jurisdiction.
</margin>
It is a disputed question whether **interdicts** <note>urgent legal remedies or court orders to protect a right or possession</note> are available to protect one's jurisdiction? The **Doctors of Law** <note>original: "Dd."; leading legal scholars</note> affirm this. Based on customary practice and for the state of the modern **Republic** <note>meaning the State or Holy Roman Empire</note> (in which public matters are confused with private ones), *a* they are correct; however, according to our law, it is not so. *b*

*a* **Matthias Hubner**, *thesis 117, On Interdicts.* *b* **The same Hubner**, *thesis 52, On Interdicts*; **Bocer**, *class 4, dispute 5, thesis 52.* **Giphanius** states excellently in his *Treatise on the Order of Trials, under the title On causes and questions of trials:* "Public cases," he says, "are discussed and decided not in the regular courts, but in councils or the **Senate**, such as matters regarding **sovereignty** <note>original: "imperio"</note>, jurisdiction, the **Principate** <note>the office of the Prince</note>, or...

### XX.

<margin>Division of subjects.</margin>
**Subjects** are of either an **eminent** <note>higher-ranking</note> or an **inferior** condition. Those of the eminent condition are the **nobles**, among whom **Doctors** <note>In this era, holders of a doctoral degree—especially in law—were granted personal nobility and high social standing equivalent to the knightly class</note> are also numbered.

*a* What should truly be thought of **Doctors** created by the **Prince** or by **Counts Palatine** is debated: they are certainly called **Bullate Doctors** <note>original: "Bullati"; these were individuals granted a doctorate by imperial patent or decree (a "bull") rather than through the traditional rigorous examinations of a university faculty</note> by a poor custom, and if they are not to be preferred to Doctors created by academic colleges, they are at least equal to them.

*a See Matthias Stephanus, book 2, On Jurisdiction, part 1, chapter 7, member 2, number 67 and following; also there Matthias Stephanus, book 2, On Jurisdiction, part 1, chapter 6, member 1, number 82 and following; Laurentius Ohm in the Treatise on Episcopal Law, thesis 17, letter T. Nor should anyone here object to me with the contrary common opinion. For truly, **Seneca** says in the book **On the Happy Life**: "Nothing must be more carefully ensured than that we do not, after the manner of sheep, follow the flock of those going before us, proceeding not where one ought to go, but where the path actually goes." And do not say to me, he says, "this side seems to be the larger," for it is worse for that very reason, because things do not go so well with human*...

<meta>The catchword "manis" completes the word "humanis" (human), leading into the next page's discussion of human affairs.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses whether public rights like sovereignty and tax-collection can be litigated in court, citing a historical example of a Roman Emperor's "madness" in trying to sue for his throne. It also establishes the social hierarchy of the state, noting that Doctors of Law are legally considered part of the nobility.</summary>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>

[...hu]man affairs are conducted, so that better things please more people; or rather, the crowd is proof of the worst <note>original: "imo turba est argumentum pessimi"; a common legal and philosophical maxim suggesting that the opinion of the masses is often the least reliable or least virtuous</note>.

### XXI.

<margin>
Whether a Doctor can seek restoration to his original legal status.
</margin>
A **minor** <note>a person under the age of 25 in Roman law</note> under 25 years of age can seek **restoration to his original legal status** <note>original: "restitutionem in integrum"; a legal remedy where a person is returned to their position before a contract or judgment due to being disadvantaged by their youth</note> on account of harm suffered in a judgment, even if that minor has attained the rank of the **Doctorate** <note>the highest academic degree</note> (which Johannes Garfias writes is little esteemed by the learned).

*a. Decianus (Counsel 91, number 1, volume 2) responded that the Doctorate can be restored in its entirety. b. Johannes Garfias in the treatise on expenses and improvements, chapter 4, number 6. c. Fachinæus (Book 3, Controversies, chapter 9) maintains this opinion, and Steinhaufen reports it in his commentary on the Digest title 'on minors,' number 3. For, he says, the **science of law** <note>theoretical knowledge</note> is one thing, but **prudence in handling affairs** <note>practical wisdom</note> is another; the latter is not gained from books but is acquired through use and experience. This is supported by what **Bartolus** says in his treatise on testimony, numbers 72 and 73, where he states: "Though it may be established that someone is a great Jurist or Physician, it does not therefore follow that he is a **prudent** Jurist or Physician." For this requires that, through previous practice, he has formed the habit of adapting universal principles to particular cases. See also Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, Book 5, chapter 9, and the commentaries there by Acciaiolus, Gelsius, and Johannes Magirus. However, some disagree; see Lord Doctor Rittershusius in his treatise on last wills, part 5, chapter 2; Bocer, class 6, dispute 10, thesis 40, letter a; Vigelius, book 5 of the method of legal controversies, chapter 13, rule 18, exceptions 11 and 12.*

---

Of an **inferior condition** <note>referring to social and legal standing within the hierarchy of the state</note> are Merchants, Craftsmen, farmers, shepherds, etc.

*Bartolus, in his treatise on testimony, number 91, says: "The city needs many things; it needs a lord, and it needs subjects of diverse conditions. For some are counselors to the rulers, others undergo other duties, others...*

<header>Disputation III.</header>

...there are merchants, and others practicing various services, crafts, and trades, all of whose actions are directed toward the ordering of civil life. **Bartolus** <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato (1313–1357), one of the most influential legal scholars of the Middle Ages</note> adds, however, that merchants and craftsmen engage in business and trades for the purpose of their own personal benefit—specifically, so that they might increase their own wealth—and therefore their wisdom should be called **domestic management** <note>original: "oeconomicam"; from the Greek oikonomia, referring to the management of a private household or estate</note> rather than **political wisdom** <note>original: "politicam"; wisdom pertaining to the governance of the city-state</note>.

### XXIII.

Among the things that pertain to the **Commonwealth** <note>original: "Rempublicam"</note> or the city, public and private buildings are also included. **a** Public buildings pertain either to religion, to defense, or to the convenience of the city. **b** To religion belong sacred houses, temples, and so forth. Defense consists of walls, **c** towers, gates, **d** and the like. Items of convenience are marketplaces <note>original: "fora"</note>, **e** theaters, **f** stadiums, **g** etc.

<margin>
**a** See Vigelius, book 3, *Method of Civil Law*, chapters 31 & 32. And add Petrus Gregorius, *On the Republic*, in which he treats the affairs of cities and the Commonwealth in nine chapters.
**b** Petrus Gregorius, book 30, *Syntaxis Artium Mirabilis*, chapter 2.
**c** Sacred walls are defined in Law 1, and the commentary <note>original: "gloss."; the standard medieval marginal gloss on Roman law</note> there, in the Digest <note>original: "ff."; the Digest or Pandects, a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions commissioned by Emperor Justinian</note> *On the division of things*. Add Gilberto Regio, book 2, chapter 4. And if anyone violates the walls, they are punished by death <note>original: "capite punitur"; literally 'punished by the head'</note> according to the final law of the same title. I interpret this as applying only to the walls of the city of Rome (Bartolus on the said final law; Clarus, book 5, *Sentences*, question 68, number 25; Rolando a Valle, volume 2, counsel 8; Gaeddæus on Law 5, numbers 17 & 18 of the Digest *On the meaning of words*). There are indeed "sacred walls" in municipalities as well <note>Law 'Sanctum', 8, section 'in municipiis', 1, Digest *On the division of things*</note>. Indeed, the goods of municipalities are also called "public" <note>Law 'Inter publica', 17, Digest *On the meaning of words*</note>. And yet, one who takes them is not punished under the **Julian Law on Embezzlement** <note>original: "Lex Iulia peculatus"; a Roman law concerning the theft of public or sacred property</note> against one who has stolen the goods of the city of Rome...

In our times, almost no consideration is given to theaters, according to **Francesco Patrizi**, *On the Institution of the Republic*, book 8, chapter 14. The ancients, however, thought that nothing more pleasing could be provided to the masses than to give them plays and to put on spectacles and games. **Patrizi** says the same. For this reason, **King Theodosius** strongly recommended to **Symmachus** the repair and support of the Roman theaters, as found in **Cassiodorus**, *Variae*, book 4, and cited by **Petrus Gregorius**, *On the Republic*, book 2, chapter 9, number 4. Indeed, both in a monarchy and in an aristocracy, certain **illusions** <note>original: "simulacra"; appearances or diversions used by rulers to keep the public satisfied and distracted</note> are thrust upon the common people, on which they feed as if on milk and are thus infatuated. Spectacles are numbered among these types of illusions. **Arnold Clapmarius**, *On the Secrets of Republics*, book 6, chapter 10. One must be careful, however, lest that which...

### XXIV.

Regarding private buildings, the magistrate should provide that they are not built or equipped with excessive pride, nor adorned with statues, tapestries, and other burdens <note>original: "oneribus"; likely referring to the financial burden of maintenance or the social burden of ostentation</note>.

*See Schonborn, Political Science, book 1, chapter 15. Christoph Colerus's notes on Sallust’s first letter "On the Ordering of the Republic," at the word "For a house or villa," where he says: "Where such expenses are less necessary, they openly prove corrupted luxury." But yet, he adds, "what do the citizens of the lowest census—especially those followers of Mercury <note>original: "Mercuriales isti"; a reference to merchants and traders, Mercury being the god of commerce</note>—not dare in our cities? I know what arises from it: the hatred of the lower classes, the envy of fellow citizens combined with the hatred of neighboring Princes, and indeed, what is most ruinous to the Republic: destitution." For God the Avenger is never far. Lest I myself should speak an ill omen, let Pliny say it for me: "truly fires punish luxury," etc.*

<margin>What the Treasury is.</margin> The affairs of the Treasury <term>Fiscus<gloss>Originally meaning a "woven basket" for holding money, it came to represent the private funds of the Emperor as distinct from the public aerarium</gloss></term> also pertain to the Republic. Properly speaking, the Treasury is the Prince's treasure-chest, to which flow the taxes and tributes, **b** treasures, **c** fines and penalties...

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>On Public Law.</header>
<meta>catchword: Gæd. [Likely a reference to the legal scholar Jacobus Gaedaeus]</meta>

...and the punishments of the condemned <insert>d</insert> and other matters which are brought in regarding the **single chapter on which things are regalian rights** <note>original: "c. un. quæ sint regalia"; *regalia* refers to the specific rights, lands, and taxes that belong exclusively to the sovereign or the state</note>.

<margin>What a public tax consists of.</margin>
*See Matthaeus Stephanus, book 2, On Jurisdiction, part 1, chapter 7, member 1; Scipio Gentilis, book 2, On Donations between Husband and Wife, chapter 2; and the law "Of all things," 6, in the section on what is customarily brought to the treasury. Code on Taxes.* Moreover, a **tax** <note>original: "vectigal"; a general term for public revenue, often specifically a toll or customs duty</note> consists of counted money; it cannot be introduced by custom that goods be paid in place of the tax <note>original: "species loco vectigalis solvatur"; this prevents citizens from paying taxes with physical goods (like grain or cloth) instead of currency</note>. *Henning Goden, counsels 7 and 8; Schneidewin on the section "By the head," number 3, Institutes on the Aquilian Law.* Here, the following may be asked: I. Can a Prince impose a tribute for the marriage of his daughter and the redemption of captives? **Affirmed**, along with Boerius, decisions 126 and 128. II. Can a magistrate move his **toll house** <note>original: "telonium"; a place where customs or tolls were collected</note> from one accustomed place to another without the Emperor's consent? **Denied**, based on the law "If to anyone," 2, the verse "but," in the Digest on Servitudes; Petrus Friderus Mindanus, book 2, On Mandates, chapter 42, numbers 4 and 5.

<margin>Several questions pertaining to this matter.</margin>
III. Does the fault of a partner, a driver, a sailor, or a servant—who is carrying goods but not presenting them at the toll station—harm the partner or the Master? **Denied**. Rosenthal, chapter 5, On Fiefs, conclusion 40, number 4, in the note to letter d, and conclusion 41 at the end; based on the law "Cheated," section 1, Digest on Publicans; and the law "We decree," 22, Code on Punishments. If a **treasure** <note>original: "thesaurus"; specifically referring to hidden money or valuables found by chance whose owner is unknown</note> is found in a public religious place, half belongs to the finder and half to the **treasury** <note>original: "fisco"; the *fiscus* was the state or imperial treasury</note>. But if it is found in one’s own private religious place, the whole remains by right in the power of the finder. See Dominicus Arumaeus, Justinian Exercises 4, thesis 8; Donellus, 4, commentaries 14, and there Hilliger in the note on the single chapter "Which things are regalian." Regarding the difference between a **fine** <note>original: "mulcta"</note> and a **penalty** <note>original: "pœna"</note>, see Matthaeus Wesenbeck on the title of the Code "On the manner of fines which are imposed in judgment"; Matthaeus Stephanus; the Distinguished Doctor Scipio Gentilis, thesis 124, on regalian rights. The penalty against one who defaces the **Praetor’s white board** <note>original: "album prætoris"; the board where the Roman Praetor’s edicts were publicly posted</note>, established in law 7 of the Digest on Jurisdiction, is to be granted to the plaintiff alone.

->XXIV.<-

The magistrate should see to it that private buildings are not constructed or equipped with excessive pride, nor adorned with signs, tapestries, and other burdensome ornaments.

Regarding the fact that luxury itself should not rule wickedly, let Pliny speak for me: "Indeed, fires punish luxury," etc.* <note>This refers to the idea that densely packed, over-decorated wooden buildings were a significant fire hazard in early modern cities.</note>

<meta>This page continues the legal analysis of public order, shifting from the regulation of private luxury to the specific financial and administrative rights of the sovereign, known as the "Regalia." <page-num>111</page-num>
# On Public Law.

...and the punishments of the condemned, and other matters which are dealt with in the single chapter *Which things are Regalia* <note>original: "quæ sint regalia"; 'Regalia' refers to the specific rights, lands, and financial prerogatives reserved for the sovereign or the state</note>, are included here.

*See Matthias Stephanus, book 2 on jurisdiction, part 1, chapter 7, member 1; Scipio Gentilis, book 2 on donations between husband and wife, chapter 2; and the law "of all things," 6, there regarding those things which are customarily brought to the treasury, Code on taxes.* <margin>What a tax consists of.</margin> A tax <term>vectigal; a general term for state revenue, often from customs or land</term> consists of a cash payment <note>original: "pecunia numerata"; literally 'counted money'</note>, and it cannot be established by custom that goods in kind be paid in place of a tax. *Henning Goeden, counsels 7 and 8; Schneidewin on the section "capite," number 3, Institutes on the Aquilian Law.* At this point, several questions may be asked:

I. Can a Prince <note>original: "Princeps"; the sovereign ruler</note> impose a tribute for the marriage of his daughter or for the ransom of captives? *This is affirmed, along with Boerius, decisions 126 and 128.*

II. Can a magistrate transfer his toll-house <term>telonium; a station for collecting customs or transit duties</term> from one established and customary location to another without the Emperor's consent? *This is denied, based on the law "if to anyone," 2, the verse "but," Digest on servitudes; Peter Friedrich Mindanus, book 2 on mandates, chapter 42, numbers 4 and 5.*

<margin>Several questions pertaining to this.</margin> III. If a partner, a driver, a sailor, or a servant is carrying goods but fails to present them at the toll-house, does their fault harm the partner or the Master? *This is denied. See Rosenthal, chapter 5 on fiefs, conclusion 40, number 4 in the note to letter d, and conclusion 41 at the end; also the law "defrauded," section 1, Digest on public auctions; and the law "we decree," 22, Code on punishments.*

If a treasure <note>original: "thesaurus"; specifically hidden valuables with no known owner</note> is found in a public religious place, half belongs to the finder and half to the state treasury <term>fiscus; the imperial or public purse</term>. However, if it is found in one’s own religious place, the whole remains by right with the finder. *See Dominicus Arumaeus, Justinian Exercises 4, thesis 8; Donellus, 4 Commentaries 14, and there Hilliger in the notes; and the single chapter "Which things are Regalia."* Regarding the difference between a fine <term>mulcta; a discretionary financial penalty often imposed by a judge</term> and a penalty <term>pœna; a legal punishment prescribed by law for a crime</term>, see Matthäus Wesenbeck on the title of the Code regarding the manner of fines to be inflicted in court; Matthias Stephanus; and the distinguished Doctor Scipio Gentilis, thesis 124 on royal rights. The penalty against one who defaces the Praetor’s notice board <note>original: "album prætoris"; the whitened tablet where the magistrate posted legal edicts and the names of judges</note>, as established in law 7, Digest on jurisdiction, is to be awarded to the plaintiff alone.

<summary>This page defines the "Regalia" (sovereign rights), focusing on the nature of taxation as a cash obligation and the legal rules governing toll-houses and found treasure. It also distinguishes between judicial fines and legal penalties, and protects the integrity of public legal notices.</summary>

<header>Third Disputation.</header>

*Henning Goeden on law 12, at the beginning, number 15 and following, Digest on the meaning of words. ### XXVI.

<margin>Whether citizens can be forced into military service.</margin>
Those things which are prepared for the preservation, expansion, and care of the Republic <note>original: "Rempubl."; refers to the state or the common wealth</note> pertain to it for another reason. The Republic is preserved even by war; concerning this, it must be understood that citizens can absolutely be compelled to military service, and it is considered a crime <note>original: "pro delicto"; a legal offense or transgression</note> to refuse the duty of military service.

*Law: "He who with one," 4, "more seriously however," 10, and the following section, Digest on military matters. Valerius Maximus, book 6, chapter 3. See Panormitanus on the chapter "just as" and below at the final number on the oath. Angelo de Chivasso in his Summary, under the word "war," number 7. Menochius, counsel 298, number 1. Pierre Grégoire, book 11 on the Republic, chapter 3, number 12. It is established, however, that a soldier cannot engage in business, from the title "on the business of soldiers not being conducted," and law "if a member of the cohort," 12, Code on the cohort-members, book 12. See Accursius on law "soldiers," 45, Digest on the same title. Cases of those over 25 years old, and in the said law "if a member of the cohort," but also, Code on the cohort-members, book 12. Claude Coustau, 1 on the law and privileges of soldiers, chapter 8. Lucas de Penna, on the single law, number 1, Code on the business of soldiers not being conducted. Paride dal Pozzo on the extraction of military matters, book 1, under the Title "whether merchants," etc., number 2. Pierre Grégoire, 4 on the Republic, 6 and following; but add Viglius to the Title of the Institutes on the military testament, number 15.*

### XXVII.

<margin>To whom captured things belong.</margin>
Mobile property <term>res mobiles; movable goods like equipment, animals, or money</term> captured in war become the property of the soldiers in such a way that the designation of the General or Leader is hardly necessary. Immobile property <term>res immobiles; fixed assets like land or buildings</term> captured in war are acquired by the King or the Emperor, or by the capturing soldier.

*Boerius, decision 178, number 5 and following. Henning Goeden in...*

<meta>The catchword "Gæd. in" indicates the text continues with further citations from Henning Goeden on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the state's right to conscript citizens for military defense and the legal ownership of spoils of war. It establishes that dodging the draft is a crime and distinguishes between movable loot (which belongs to soldiers) and seized land (which generally falls to the sovereign).</summary>

<keywords>military service, conscription, spoils of war, property rights, Republic, state preservation, legal jurisdiction, res mobiles, res immobiles</keywords>

<header>On Public Law.</header>
<meta>catchword: who</meta>

...who. number 11. Digest on the meaning of words. Differing: Bartolus in his first and second readings on the law "natural," section 5, at the end, Digest on acquiring the ownership of things. Baldus on the section "likewise those," numbers 6 and 7, Institutes on the division of things. Diego de Covarruvias in the chapter "sin," part 2, section 11, number 6, on the rules of law in the sixth book of Decretals. Doneau, 4th commentary, 21.

### XXVIII.

<margin>
Promises made to an enemy must be kept.
</margin>

A promise <note>original: "fides"; literally "faith" or "trust," referring here to one's word or a sworn oath</note> given to an enemy must be kept, whether it is given by a Prince **a** or by a private citizen. **b** From this, I conclude that a prisoner dismissed by a legitimate enemy on the condition that he pay a <term>ransom <note>original: "λύτρον" (lytron)</note></term> is bound to return to make the payment. Furthermore, if a captive Prince enters into a peace treaty, he is not assisted by the legal edict regarding force and fear. **d** <note>This refers to the "exceptio metus," a legal defense where a contract is voided if signed under duress. The author argues that a sovereign cannot claim "fear" to break a treaty made while a prisoner.</note>

**a** *Matthias Cuno in his treatise on pacts, chapter 22, number 6.* **b** *See Hackelman, Illustrious Questions, disputation 9, theory 1, letter i. Canon Law: "do not wish," 23, question 2. François Hotman, Illustrious Questions, 7.* **d** *See Jean Bodin, book 5 of On the Republic, chapter 6, number 596. Pierre Grégoire, book 11 of On the Republic, chapter 15, number 14. Diego de Covarruvias on marriage, chapter 3, section 4, number 13 and following. Fortunatus Garcia on the law of conventions, Digest on pacts. Kirchner, political disputation 17, theory 2, letter o.*

### XXIX.

<margin>
Whether a prisoner can be forced into a second agreement regarding his ransom.
</margin>

A question often arises: if a prisoner of war has once settled with his captor for a certain sum for his ransom, can that same captor increase the amount of the ransom on the pretext that, after the agreement, he discovered the prisoner’s wealth was actually greater? He cannot.

*See Guy Pape, decision 113, beginning with "in the matter of wars." Pierre Grégoire, at the aforementioned number 14, at the end.*

### XXX.

Nicolaus Everhardus of Middelburg, in his *Topics*, **a** correctly defends the Duke of Brabant—who was a vassal of both the Roman Emperor and the King of France—arguing that he could not be accused because of a confederation he entered into with the English, who were the ancient enemies of the Kings of France...

<vocab>Faith toward an enemy, prisoner, ransom, right of war, Duke of Brabant, Vassal</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the legal and ethical obligations of keeping one's word to an enemy during war, specifically arguing that ransoms must be paid and that peace treaties signed by captive sovereigns remain valid. It also notes that a ransom amount cannot be increased after it has been agreed upon, even if the prisoner is found to be wealthier than first thought.</summary>
<keywords>international law, prisoner of war, ransom, treaties, duress, sovereign rights, Duke of Brabant, legal ethics</keywords>

<header>Disputation III.</header>

<margin>*a In the place touched upon, or in respect to it. b See the most distinguished Doctor Rittershusius, book 2 on the division of fiefs, chapter 5, number 13.*</margin>
...who [treats the subject] of <term>felony <note>In feudal law, "felonia" referred specifically to a breach of the bond between a lord and his vassal.</note></term> or infidelity, specifically regarding the resulting loss of a <term>fief <note>original: "feudi"; land held under the feudal system in exchange for service and loyalty.</note></term>. *b*

### XXXI.

<margin>*a My most distinguished teacher, Scipio Gentili, in his oration on Roman and Turkish military affairs. Georg Schönborner, book 6 of Politics, chapter 18. Hermann Kirchner, the final political disputation, hypothesis 5, letter a. b Matthias Wesenbeck on the title of the Digest regarding captives and the right of <term>postliminy <note>original: "postliminii"; the legal right of a person captured in war to have their previous rights and status restored upon their return.</note></term>. Antonio Corsetto in the treatise on Royal Power, part 5, number 36, where he says: "Kings cannot rise up against the faithful in the same way that Christian Princes today wage war among themselves." For this reason, Hostiensis <note>A famous 13th-century canon lawyer.</note> wonders how they are absolved by their confessors. See Bartolus on the law "enemies," number 24, at the end, and the additions there by Albani on the Digest regarding captives and the return of postliminy. Bartolomeo Caepolla in the treatise on urban estates, chapter 1, number 3.*</margin>
By way of a <term>finishing touch <note>original: "Coronidis loco"; a "coronis" was a decorative flourish at the end of a manuscript, used here to mean a concluding remark.</note></term>, I add: I. That an enemy is not lawfully killed by poison. *a* II. That the dissensions of Christians among themselves are not so much wars as they are butcheries <note>original: "lanienas"; literally "slaughterhouses" or "shambles."</note>. *b*

->*And let it suffice to have set down these few points regarding Public Law for the present.*<-

# THE END.

<summary>This final section of the disputation addresses the legal grounds for stripping a vassal of their land for disloyalty and concludes with a sharp ethical critique of the brutality of wars between Christian nations.</summary>
